title: "MIN223 — full lesson text"

# MIN223 — Introduction to Homiletics

## Course objectives

- 1. Justify the importance of preaching as it relates to other aspects of the ministry.
- 2. Define the purpose of preaching.
- 3. Establish clear goals in your preaching ministry.
- 4. Be more aware of your own sense of calling and its effect on the urgency of preaching.
- 5. Develop an effective weekly work schedule for sermon preparation.
- 6. Create a year’s preaching calendar.
- 7. Analyze a passage of Scripture and determine the best structure or form for a particular sermon.
- 8. Distinguish between topical, textual, and expository sermons, and determine the appropriate use of each one.
- 9. Apply the principles of sermon preparation in building sermons.
- 10. Construct and preach sound biblical sermons.

## 1 - The Place of Preaching

### Relationship of Preaching

#### 1.1.1 — Define the role of the pastor in terms of its function.

To Pastoral Activity

The term pastor basically means “shepherd or herdsman” and is derived from the Latin verb pascere (“to feed”). The pastor’s responsibility is both to tend and to feed the flock. Jesus applied both of these functions to the Christian minister when He commanded His apostle Simon Peter to feed and care for the sheep—the Christian believers. The renowned apostle Paul, about thirty-five years later, described the duties of the Christian minister when he said to his young colleague Timothy, “Preach the Word [feed]; . . . correct, rebuke and encourage—with great patience and careful instruction [tend]” (2 Timothy 4:2). Since Jesus declared that “Man does not live on bread alone, but on every word that comes from the mouth of God” (Matthew 4:4), when you preach the Word of God, the Bible, you feed people spiritually.

Although it is possible to be a preacher without being a pastor, it is not possible to be a pastor in the biblical sense of the term without being a preacher. The flock needs a regular diet of nourishing food for spiritual growth and health. The pastor has the responsibility and the opportunity to provide this.

As a pastor, you can share in the lives of the members of your congregation and prepare the best food for them. By knowing the individual members personally, you will know the differing needs of each one and preach accordingly. By knowing the Bible well, you will know where the best food is to be found. This course will help you serve that food well.

To Worship

An American visiting the Scottish capital, Edinburgh, heard two notable preachers. After the first he declared, “What a preacher!” But after the second, “What a Savior!” The second had done his work well; he had moved his audience to worship. This should be a goal of all preaching.

Preaching can inspire worship by focusing attention on the Savior and His redeeming work, on the greatness of God and His constant love, or on the Holy Spirit and His miraculous acts. Clear exposition of the truth of God’s Word will remind Christians why they should praise and glorify God. It will also keep worship real by eliminating ritualism and excessive emotionalism. To be acceptable by God, worship must be both spiritual and scriptural. Jesus said, “God is spirit, and his worshipers must worship in spirit and in truth” (John 4:24). His concern is not with physical factors such as place and posture, but with attitude. Preaching is ideally suited for effecting a change in attitude in an audience and, therefore, can serve as a needed corrective at times.

By carefully studying the Bible teaching on subjects such as praise, prayer, and magnifying and glorifying God, you can promote pure, balanced worship in your audience. By adopting a positive attitude of regularly emphasizing the goodness of God and His gracious acts, you will encourage your audience to worship both in public and in private.

To Mass Media

The outstanding success of the sixteenth-century reform movement in the European Christian church may be traced directly to the development a century before of the movable-type printing press. The age of mass communication began with a resounding success as far as Protestant Christianity was concerned. In addition to printing, the last hundred years have added audiovisual media and telecommunications as effective ways of communicating the gospel. While cost may yet limit the availability of some media, their effectiveness in broadcasting the gospel and surmounting barriers makes them invaluable. They can penetrate into areas where the preacher cannot venture and reach many more people with one radio or television broadcast than some preachers will reach in a lifetime.

However, when God wanted to speak to humankind, He sent prophets, preachers, and even His Son, Jesus Christ. He knew that people respond best in a person-to-person encounter. But the mass media are impersonal. This communication can only be one way—that is, a monologue—since audience needs have to be determined beforehand and individual situations cannot be adequately covered. Those preachers who use the media usually recognize that despite the great advantages, the disadvantages of media ministry make it at best a supplement to, rather than a substitute for, the personal encounter of preaching. If preaching was only telling, the mass media would be ideal; however, preaching, in the full sense, is more than that. The preacher communicates directly with his or her audience and can judge the effectiveness of that communication by the feedback (more often nonverbal than verbal). He or she can spontaneously alter the length of the message, as he or she feels led, without causing any problems, and can be more open to the immediate guidance of the Holy Spirit.

While, in my opinion, preaching is the most effective means of communicating the gospel, the mass media have a vital role in broadcasting the gospel. It is not therefore either-or , but both-and . Despite the limitations of the mass media, you can use them to great advantage if you carefully select the content in the light of what you know about the needs of the audience. Use them to support, extend, and increase your preaching ministry, but do not allow them to become substitutes for it.

### Importance of Preaching

#### 1.2.1 — Explain how the Scriptures demonstrate the importance of preaching.

Scripturally

The Gospels reveal that Jesus gave a high priority to preaching in His ministry. In Mark’s Gospel, the first recorded activity of Jesus, after His testing in the wilderness, was preaching: “Jesus came into Galilee, preaching the gospel” (1:14, KJV). His first sermon in His hometown of Nazareth established this as a prime focus of His ministry: “The Spirit of the Lord is on me, because he has anointed me to preach good news to the poor. He has sent me to proclaim freedom for the prisoners and recovery of sight for the blind, to release the oppressed, to proclaim the year of the Lord’s favor” (Luke 4:18–19).

Even when the people flocked to see His miracles, His response was, “Let us go somewhere else—to the nearby villages—so I can preach there also. That is why I have come” (Mark 1:38). Thomas Goodwin, the seventeenth-century Puritan preacher, aptly said, “God had only one Son, and He made Him a minister” (Whyte n.d.).

Jesus did not exclude other activities. He was a man of action, but most of all He was a man of words. Directed by the Holy Spirit, John, in his Gospel, took the title of the Word as the fullest description of the divine Son in His eternal existence. Since the word is the expression of the person, it is the perfect medium for the revelation of the perfect Being, God. This Divine Word is action. “Through him all things were made; without him nothing was made that has been made” (John 1:3). There is, then, no difference with God between words and deeds. “For he spoke, and it came to be” (Psalm 33:9; see also Genesis 1:1–31; Isaiah 55:11; Hebrews 11:3). To be the perfect revelation of the Divine Person, the Word had to be expressed in personality as well as in action. Thus, “The Word became flesh and made his dwelling among us” (John 1:14). The Word, then, is not so much God’s comment upon history as God’s communication determining history. God’s final revelation is a person rather than a philosophy, an incarnation rather than a theology.

In the Incarnation, God is confronting humans with the perfect personal revelation of Himself. In preaching, God continues to confront people with that revelation. Jesus commanded His followers, “Go into all the world and preach the good news to all creation” (Mark 16:15). The gospel record closes with the simple statement, “Then the disciples went out and preached everywhere, and the Lord worked with them and confirmed his word by the signs that accompanied it” (Mark 16:20). The New Testament abounds with ample references to the importance of preaching in the apostolic church. In Acts, for example, there are ninety-two distinct references to preaching. Acts 5:42 is typical: “Day after day, in the temple courts and from house to house, they never stopped teaching and proclaiming the good news that Jesus is the Christ.”

As important as the rite of baptism may be in the church, Paul wrote, “For Christ did not send me to baptize, but to preach the gospel” (1 Corinthians 1:14–17). His contrast does not deny the place of baptism but emphasizes the priority of preaching. Preaching should provide converts to be baptized and build up the baptized believers in the fellowship of the church.

The same sense of divine compulsion expressed in Jesus’ words, “That is why I have come” (Mark 1:38), is also reflected in the disciples. When threatened with punishment if they continued preaching, Peter and John declared, “We cannot help speaking about what we have seen and heard” (Acts 4:20). Paul also confessed, “I am compelled to preach. Woe to me if I do not preach the gospel!” (1 Corinthians 9:16).

Do you feel that same sense of compulsion to preach? This is really what makes a person a preacher. As a preacher you are essential to God’s program for reaching people.

Historically

The first Christian sermon on the Day of Pentecost had an outstanding effect. Three thousand were added to the church that day, and the number increased daily. Wolston (1896) commented of “the conversion of three thousand souls through one preaching. I am afraid it takes three thousand sermons to convert one soul now.” This contrast is not normally the case. The part that preaching played in the expansion and development of the early Christian church is historically confirmed as the normal and inevitable result of faithful preaching. The spiritual condition of the church is usually a reflection of the power of the pulpit. Periods of decline and decadence in the church are marked by a loss of power in the pulpits.

Throughout the earliest centuries of the Christian era, the church was characterized by strong preaching. A community was formed of men and women from widely differing backgrounds and races that astounded the pagan world by its demonstration of love. Preaching kept that community forceful and whole. In later years, when ritualism had largely taken over the rightful place of preaching, the sermons of those early church leaders became the staple diet of the church. When the Christianized Roman Empire cracked and collapsed before the barbarian invaders, Christian preachers set out to turn them to faith in Christ. Later (around AD 1200), the coming of the Mendicant Friars announced a revival of preaching. Francis of Assisi, Dominic, and their followers toured Europe and even traveled to non-Christian lands, preaching wherever they could gain an audience.

With the great Reformation, preaching once again came to the foreground. Although it was never to lose its prominent position in historic Protestantism, preaching was strongly featured in the revivals of evangelical religion under the Puritans, Wesley, and Whitefield, and the revivalists Finney and Moody. John Wesley, as variously reported, ministered for fifty years, preached over forty thousand sermons and published over two hundred books, tracts, and essays. George Whitefield, similarly, preached for thirty-four years to over a million people. Observers were often amazed that crowds of such size could gather to hear him—and actually hear him—in those days before the voice amplification aids we enjoy today.

When you look at the history of the Christian church in Europe and North America, you will see that the church’s spiritual condition and the effectiveness of the preacher have risen or fallen together. When life has gone out of the preacher, it is not long before it is gone from the church also. But when there has been a revived message on the preacher’s lips, there comes a revived condition in the church also. If you wish to see a spiritual revival in your church, give close attention to your preaching.

Practically

The practical importance of preaching can be easily overlooked. “What good does it do?” some ask. Not only is it one of God’s prime means of communicating the Christian faith, but it also has an uplifting effect on society in general. Jesus tells us that we are “the salt of the earth” and “the light of the world” (Matthew 5:13­–14). He exhorts us, “Let your light shine before men, that they may see your good deeds and praise your Father in heaven” (Matthew 5:16). Paul links this shining as “lights in the world” with “holding forth the word of life” (Philippians 2:15–16, KJV). This provides a verbal framework for our good deeds, which otherwise could easily be attributed to human nature, education, or self-control. As imperfect as the Christian’s life may be at times in showing how God wants people to live, we have the decisive terms of the revelation of God’s will. “What does the Lord require of you? To act justly and to love mercy and to walk humbly with your God” (Micah 6:8). The moral requirements of the holy God make us all stop and take stock of our lives.

The prophet Ezekiel received a special commission from God: “Son of man, I have made you a watchman for the house of Israel; so hear the word I speak and give them warning from me” (Ezekiel 3:17). The Christian preacher stands in the same relationship to God and the community as Ezekiel did here. He or she is the watchperson appointed to warn the people of approaching danger. The Bible is full of examples of what happens to those who forget God, reject His standards, and ignore His offer of mercy. Some of these examples are the times of Noah, Sodom, the Israelites in the wilderness, the oppressions in the days of Judges, and the Assyrian and Babylonian captivities.

Beyond the immediate earthly danger faced by the nation, there is that fearful personal danger which we all face because of our individual sinful acts and rebelliousness. We have failed in our preaching if we are not constantly warning the sinner of the certain results that will follow hardness of the heart. The revelation of what sin does has driven many Christians toward holy living and away from the traps of compromise. No one who truly believes that sin’s dreadful end is an eternity separated from God in unending punishment can lightly and carelessly do what is wrong. By emphatically preaching the truth about eternity, you will promote holiness in your church as well as fulfill your call to be a watchperson warning the people.

While the Bible nowhere excuses sin but rather warns of its fearful consequences, it does extend a message of hope and forgiveness to those who yield to temptation. The Christian preacher’s highest privilege is to proclaim, “If anybody does sin, we have one who speaks to the Father in our defense—Jesus Christ, the Righteous One. He is the atoning sacrifice for our sins, and not only for ours, but also for the sins of the whole world” (1 John 2:1–2).

Knowing the universal scope of the atoning work of Jesus, we should never despair of anybody. God bases salvation upon neither human virtue nor on arbitrary selection; rather, it rests upon the redeeming death of our Lord Jesus Christ. Any person living may enter by faith into the full benefits of this redemption. Everyone is capable of being saved even though not everyone is willing to be saved. “He [God] is patient with you, not wanting anyone to perish, but everyone to come to repentance” (2 Peter 3:9).

The renowned Baptist preacher Alexander MacLaren (1826–1910) declared, “If I had my ministry to live through again, I should strike the note of comfort far oftener than I have done.” This note of comfort is prompted by a desire to do the sinner good rather than to make him or her feel good. Those who cry “peace” when there is not peace deserve God’s sternest condemnation for their deceit (Jeremiah 6:13–14). Nevertheless, the note of compassion should never be missing from your preaching. You represent One who cares infinitely about every person and about every circumstance of his or her life. In your ministry you can make the sad, lonely, and fearful realize the loving concern of the Almighty God.

To act justly and to love mercy and to walk humbly with your God” (Micah 6:8). The moral requirements of the holy God make us all stop and take stock of our lives.

The prophet Ezekiel received a special commission from God: “Son of man, I have made you a watchman for the house of Israel; so hear the word I speak and give them warning from me” (Ezekiel 3:17). The Christian preacher stands in the same relationship to God and the community as Ezekiel did here. He or she is the watchperson appointed to warn the people of approaching danger. The Bible is full of examples of what happens to those who forget God, reject His standards, and ignore His offer of mercy. Some of these examples are the times of Noah, Sodom, the Israelites in the wilderness, the oppressions in the days of Judges, and the Assyrian and Babylonian captivities.

Beyond the immediate earthly danger faced by the nation, there is that fearful personal danger which we all face because of our individual sinful acts and rebelliousness. We have failed in our preaching if we are not constantly warning the sinner of the certain results that will follow hardness of the heart. The revelation of what sin does has driven many Christians toward holy living and away from the traps of compromise. No one who truly believes that sin’s dreadful end is an eternity separated from God in unending punishment can lightly and carelessly do what is wrong. By emphatically preaching the truth about eternity, you will promote holiness in your church as well as fulfill your call to be a watchperson warning the people.

While the Bible nowhere excuses sin but rather warns of its fearful consequences, it does extend a message of hope and forgiveness to those who yield to temptation. The Christian preacher’s highest privilege is to proclaim, “If anybody does sin, we have one who speaks to the Father in our defense—Jesus Christ, the Righteous One. He is the atoning sacrifice for our sins, and not only for ours, but also for the sins of the whole world” (1 John 2:1–2).

Knowing the universal scope of the atoning work of Jesus, we should never despair of anybody. God bases salvation upon neither human virtue nor on arbitrary selection; rather, it rests upon the redeeming death of our Lord Jesus Christ. Any person living may enter by faith into the full benefits of this redemption. Everyone is capable of being saved even though not everyone is willing to be saved. “He [God] is patient with you, not wanting anyone to perish, but everyone to come to repentance” (2 Peter 3:9).

The renowned Baptist preacher Alexander MacLaren (1826–1910) declared, “If I had my ministry to live through again, I should strike the note of comfort far oftener than I have done.” This note of comfort is prompted by a desire to do the sinner good rather than to make him or her feel good. Those who cry “peace” when there is not peace deserve God’s sternest condemnation for their deceit (Jeremiah 6:13–14). Nevertheless, the note of compassion should never be missing from your preaching. You represent One who cares infinitely about every person and about every circumstance of his or her life. In your ministry you can make the sad, lonely, and fearful realize the loving concern of the Almighty God.

#### 1.2.3 — Identify some practical effects of the preach ing ministry.

Scripturally

The Gospels reveal that Jesus gave a high priority to preaching in His ministry. In Mark’s Gospel, the first recorded activity of Jesus, after His testing in the wilderness, was preaching: “Jesus came into Galilee, preaching the gospel” (1:14, KJV). His first sermon in His hometown of Nazareth established this as a prime focus of His ministry: “The Spirit of the Lord is on me, because he has anointed me to preach good news to the poor. He has sent me to proclaim freedom for the prisoners and recovery of sight for the blind, to release the oppressed, to proclaim the year of the Lord’s favor” (Luke 4:18–19).

Even when the people flocked to see His miracles, His response was, “Let us go somewhere else—to the nearby villages—so I can preach there also. That is why I have come” (Mark 1:38). Thomas Goodwin, the seventeenth-century Puritan preacher, aptly said, “God had only one Son, and He made Him a minister” (Whyte n.d.).

Jesus did not exclude other activities. He was a man of action, but most of all He was a man of words. Directed by the Holy Spirit, John, in his Gospel, took the title of the Word as the fullest description of the divine Son in His eternal existence. Since the word is the expression of the person, it is the perfect medium for the revelation of the perfect Being, God. This Divine Word is action. “Through him all things were made; without him nothing was made that has been made” (John 1:3). There is, then, no difference with God between words and deeds. “For he spoke, and it came to be” (Psalm 33:9; see also Genesis 1:1–31; Isaiah 55:11; Hebrews 11:3). To be the perfect revelation of the Divine Person, the Word had to be expressed in personality as well as in action. Thus, “The Word became flesh and made his dwelling among us” (John 1:14). The Word, then, is not so much God’s comment upon history as God’s communication determining history. God’s final revelation is a person rather than a philosophy, an incarnation rather than a theology.

In the Incarnation, God is confronting humans with the perfect personal revelation of Himself. In preaching, God continues to confront people with that revelation. Jesus commanded His followers, “Go into all the world and preach the good news to all creation” (Mark 16:15). The gospel record closes with the simple statement, “Then the disciples went out and preached everywhere, and the Lord worked with them and confirmed his word by the signs that accompanied it” (Mark 16:20). The New Testament abounds with ample references to the importance of preaching in the apostolic church. In Acts, for example, there are ninety-two distinct references to preaching. Acts 5:42 is typical: “Day after day, in the temple courts and from house to house, they never stopped teaching and proclaiming the good news that Jesus is the Christ.”

As important as the rite of baptism may be in the church, Paul wrote, “For Christ did not send me to baptize, but to preach the gospel” (1 Corinthians 1:14–17). His contrast does not deny the place of baptism but emphasizes the priority of preaching. Preaching should provide converts to be baptized and build up the baptized believers in the fellowship of the church.

The same sense of divine compulsion expressed in Jesus’ words, “That is why I have come” (Mark 1:38), is also reflected in the disciples. When threatened with punishment if they continued preaching, Peter and John declared, “We cannot help speaking about what we have seen and heard” (Acts 4:20). Paul also confessed, “I am compelled to preach. Woe to me if I do not preach the gospel!” (1 Corinthians 9:16).

Do you feel that same sense of compulsion to preach? This is really what makes a person a preacher. As a preacher you are essential to God’s program for reaching people.

Historically

The first Christian sermon on the Day of Pentecost had an outstanding effect. Three thousand were added to the church that day, and the number increased daily. Wolston (1896) commented of “the conversion of three thousand souls through one preaching. I am afraid it takes three thousand sermons to convert one soul now.” This contrast is not normally the case. The part that preaching played in the expansion and development of the early Christian church is historically confirmed as the normal and inevitable result of faithful preaching. The spiritual condition of the church is usually a reflection of the power of the pulpit. Periods of decline and decadence in the church are marked by a loss of power in the pulpits.

Throughout the earliest centuries of the Christian era, the church was characterized by strong preaching. A community was formed of men and women from widely differing backgrounds and races that astounded the pagan world by its demonstration of love. Preaching kept that community forceful and whole. In later years, when ritualism had largely taken over the rightful place of preaching, the sermons of those early church leaders became the staple diet of the church. When the Christianized Roman Empire cracked and collapsed before the barbarian invaders, Christian preachers set out to turn them to faith in Christ. Later (around AD 1200), the coming of the Mendicant Friars announced a revival of preaching. Francis of Assisi, Dominic, and their followers toured Europe and even traveled to non-Christian lands, preaching wherever they could gain an audience.

With the great Reformation, preaching once again came to the foreground. Although it was never to lose its prominent position in historic Protestantism, preaching was strongly featured in the revivals of evangelical religion under the Puritans, Wesley, and Whitefield, and the revivalists Finney and Moody. John Wesley, as variously reported, ministered for fifty years, preached over forty thousand sermons and published over two hundred books, tracts, and essays. George Whitefield, similarly, preached for thirty-four years to over a million people. Observers were often amazed that crowds of such size could gather to hear him—and actually hear him—in those days before the voice amplification aids we enjoy today.

When you look at the history of the Christian church in Europe and North America, you will see that the church’s spiritual condition and the effectiveness of the preacher have risen or fallen together. When life has gone out of the preacher, it is not long before it is gone from the church also. But when there has been a revived message on the preacher’s lips, there comes a revived condition in the church also. If you wish to see a spiritual revival in your church, give close attention to your preaching.

Practically

The practical importance of preaching can be easily overlooked. “What good does it do?” some ask. Not only is it one of God’s prime means of communicating the Christian faith, but it also has an uplifting effect on society in general. Jesus tells us that we are “the salt of the earth” and “the light of the world” (Matthew 5:13­–14). He exhorts us, “Let your light shine before men, that they may see your good deeds and praise your Father in heaven” (Matthew 5:16). Paul links this shining as “lights in the world” with “holding forth the word of life” (Philippians 2:15–16, KJV). This provides a verbal framework for our good deeds, which otherwise could easily be attributed to human nature, education, or self-control. As imperfect as the Christian’s life may be at times in showing how God wants people to live, we have the decisive terms of the revelation of God’s will. “What does the Lord require of you? To act justly and to love mercy and to walk humbly with your God” (Micah 6:8). The moral requirements of the holy God make us all stop and take stock of our lives.

The prophet Ezekiel received a special commission from God: “Son of man, I have made you a watchman for the house of Israel; so hear the word I speak and give them warning from me” (Ezekiel 3:17). The Christian preacher stands in the same relationship to God and the community as Ezekiel did here. He or she is the watchperson appointed to warn the people of approaching danger. The Bible is full of examples of what happens to those who forget God, reject His standards, and ignore His offer of mercy. Some of these examples are the times of Noah, Sodom, the Israelites in the wilderness, the oppressions in the days of Judges, and the Assyrian and Babylonian captivities.

Beyond the immediate earthly danger faced by the nation, there is that fearful personal danger which we all face because of our individual sinful acts and rebelliousness. We have failed in our preaching if we are not constantly warning the sinner of the certain results that will follow hardness of the heart. The revelation of what sin does has driven many Christians toward holy living and away from the traps of compromise. No one who truly believes that sin’s dreadful end is an eternity separated from God in unending punishment can lightly and carelessly do what is wrong. By emphatically preaching the truth about eternity, you will promote holiness in your church as well as fulfill your call to be a watchperson warning the people.

While the Bible nowhere excuses sin but rather warns of its fearful consequences, it does extend a message of hope and forgiveness to those who yield to temptation. The Christian preacher’s highest privilege is to proclaim, “If anybody does sin, we have one who speaks to the Father in our defense—Jesus Christ, the Righteous One. He is the atoning sacrifice for our sins, and not only for ours, but also for the sins of the whole world” (1 John 2:1–2).

Knowing the universal scope of the atoning work of Jesus, we should never despair of anybody. God bases salvation upon neither human virtue nor on arbitrary selection; rather, it rests upon the redeeming death of our Lord Jesus Christ. Any person living may enter by faith into the full benefits of this redemption. Everyone is capable of being saved even though not everyone is willing to be saved. “He [God] is patient with you, not wanting anyone to perish, but everyone to come to repentance” (2 Peter 3:9).

The renowned Baptist preacher Alexander MacLaren (1826–1910) declared, “If I had my ministry to live through again, I should strike the note of comfort far oftener than I have done.” This note of comfort is prompted by a desire to do the sinner good rather than to make him or her feel good. Those who cry “peace” when there is not peace deserve God’s sternest condemnation for their deceit (Jeremiah 6:13–14). Nevertheless, the note of compassion should never be missing from your preaching. You represent One who cares infinitely about every person and about every circumstance of his or her life. In your ministry you can make the sad, lonely, and fearful realize the loving concern of the Almighty God.

### Improper Use of Preaching

#### 1.3.1 — Distinguish between examples of the proper and the improper use of preaching.

Selfishness

Every privilege has its peculiar perils and probably none more than preaching. Possibly the greatest occupational hazard is the temptation to use the pulpit to serve yourself instead of the Lord and His people. Being the center of attention can make you forget why you are there. “We do not preach ourselves, but Jesus Christ as Lord, and ourselves as your servants for Jesus’ sake,” Paul affirmed (2 Corinthians 4:5).

This selfishness often finds expression in the passion for popularity. Almost a century ago a bishop lamented, “The disease of modern preaching is its search for popularity.” There is nothing wrong with a desire to be heard by the greatest number possible; the gospel is good news to every person. The danger is in desiring the numbers for your own sake rather than the gospel’s sake. Some preachers cannot give their best unless they have a packed congregation, and they will justify almost any tricks or stunts to achieve it. When I was conducting a revival crusade in a Michigan town in October 1977, a nearby church engaged a clown to distribute hamburgers to all who would attend its Sunday evening meeting. As effective as humor may be in communicating the truth, the pulpit is not a stage for the religious comedian. Nor is it the place from which to impress people with how tech-savvy you are. Charles H. Spurgeon, the great Baptist, implored his students not to go for popularity. In a letter to Rowland Hill (1744–1833), John Berridge (1716–1793) advised, “Preach nothing down but the devil, and nothing up but Christ.”

Popularity in the pulpit ministry, as in private life, is not so much a reward as an opportunity. Use it well for the glory of God and the salvation of those who think so highly of you. Do not allow feelings of self-importance to sweep you away. Your call is to shepherd the flock, not to lord it over the people. Pride has no place in the pulpit. Walk close to God and you will not lose the humility that marks the servant of God. Even your sermons will be God’s choice rather than your favorite theme, and you will certainly never use the sermon as a means of repaying those who either offend or please you. Some enjoy hearing complimentary stories about themselves or uncomplimentary ones about their neighbors from the pulpit, yet your people are there to be fed on the Word of God, not to hear your opinion of them. God will convict the sinner and reward the faithful since He is the Lord to Whom we all must give account. Our responsibility is to feed the flock faithfully.

Substitution

Probably more subtle and therefore more deadly than selfishness is substitution in the life of the preacher. When the zealous Christians are troubled about something in their private lives, they know they must make it right. If it is something in the community, they share their burden with others who feel the same way, and together they will seek to correct the situation. But here lies the danger for preachers: preachers’ burdens often appear as gifts from God—something to preach on. The more they preach on the subject, the more they excuse themselves.

What is even worse is that by preaching about it first, you are less likely to do anything more about it. Thomas Carlyle, the noted Scottish essayist, remarked, “It is a sad but sure truth that every time you speak of a fine purpose, especially if with eloquence and to the admiration of bystanders, there is less chance of your ever making a fact of it in your own poor life” (Galstad 1984, 1).

The burden you may feel for personal evangelism should be put into action before it is put into words in your pulpit. Invert the order and it is unlikely to ever see action.

This temptation to substitute words for deeds can even creep into the way you deal with your sins. If the Holy Spirit convicts you of sin, repent and make it right. Do not try to preach away your guilt. To have preached vigorously against pride will not excuse the proud preacher. Sinclair Lewis’ fictitious evangelist, Elmer Gantry, raged against immorality but never conquered his own sexual sins. As Paul asked, “You who preach against stealing, do you steal? You who say that people should not commit adultery, do you commit adultery? You who abhor idols, do you rob temples? You who brag about the law, do you dishonor God by breaking the law? As it is written: ‘God’s name is blasphemed among the Gentiles because of you’” (Romans 2:21–24).

If you, personally, are not earnestly seeking to reach that peak of excellence you are requiring of others, get down before God in humility until you can rise with a new determination and holy resolve to do so before you dare utter a word. Remember, you can only share what is truly yours.

Superficiality

Possibly the greatest danger for the preacher is superficiality. Its most obvious form is that professionalism which attacks the preacher in middle life. Superficiality is a steady drift into a mechanical approach to the pastoral calling. The first funeral broke his heart; the thousandth was just another fee. He no longer ascends the pulpit with a message burning in his soul from his audience with the King of Kings. This preacher no longer lives up to the words of Richard Baxter, the great Puritan preacher, “I preach as never sure to preach again, and as a dying man to dying men” (Mohler n.d.). The sense of urgency has long gone, leaving in its place a deadly dullness. The thrill and the wonder of it all have faded.

A retired Pentecostal minister was stopped near his home by a zealous young convert, newly filled with the Spirit, who had just witnessed her first miracle of divine healing. “It was marvelous,” she enthusiastically announced, “just to see the man’s leg grow to its proper length.” “My dear,” he retorted, “when you have been in Pentecost as long as I have, you will realize that such things are rather commonplace, and you will not get so excited about it.” Then he strode off about his business leaving a much deflated young lady gazing after him in bewilderment.

Some say, “It is impossible in middle life, when you have said or seen it all a thousand times before, to retain the freshness of the first time,” yet thousands of godly men and women have kept the glow of youthful zeal throughout long ministries. They have not turned callous and indifferent. The secret is in a life of intimate communion with God. There is no need to pretend you are sensitive and concerned when you are living close to Jesus. A frequent note of the Gospels is that He was “filled with compassion” (Mark 1:41). Even now as our Great High Priest in heaven, He is touched by our needs. He will give you His love for the people and keep you from becoming a “professional” preacher.

But what of the young minister with glibness that is just as superficial as the older preacher’s professionalism? It is so much easier to say with Job’s friends that suffering is always the result of human sin than to go to the source of human misery and plant seeds of comfort and strength there. It is so much easier to dismiss those who are not healed as lacking in faith than to try to determine the real teachings of Scripture regarding divine healing. It is tempting to propose ready-made solutions to any and every human ailment rather than seek to understand what God actually has to say to individuals. The good doctor prescribes on the basis of sound diagnosis, while the person who pretends to be a trained doctor (a quack) supplies one cure-all remedy for every illness. In ancient Judah, God condemned the superficiality of the religious leaders: “They dress the wound of my people as though it were not serious. ‘Peace, peace,’ they say, when there is no peace” (Jeremiah 6:14). The prevention of this sickness of the ministry lies in constant study of the Bible and a sincere sympathy with those to whom you minister.

In some Pentecostal churches, there is a peculiar form of superficiality that we shall refer to as manipulation . Because of the high value they place on emotions, some ministers cannot resist the temptation to work up a high level of enthusiasm in a meeting by repeating phrases that appeal to the emotions of a congregation. Sometimes they even ask people to repeat these phrases if they are not responding in the desired way of their own free will. For example, some say, “Let us all speak in tongues,” and some congregations dutifully feed the ego of the leader by doing as instructed even though the Holy Spirit has not led them to do this. These leaders resemble one Pentecostal pioneer who is reputed to have said, “If the Spirit does not move the people, I move the Spirit.” By contrast, Peter said, “Men spoke from God as they were carried along by the Holy Spirit” (2 Peter 1:21).

The temptation to manipulate can be especially great when one is encouraging people to experience the baptism in the Holy Spirit. Some enthusiastic leaders try to induce people to speak in tongues as they seek the Spirit baptism by asking seekers to speak rapidly, to repeat certain phrases, or to follow their example as they speak in tongues. Even when a leader does not do this consciously, subconscious pressure can be exerted subconsciously by speaking in tongues in the ears of seekers while laying hands on them. Obviously, this practice is not the same as that of the disciples on the Day of Pentecost when “all of them were filled with the Holy Spirit and began to speak in other tongues as the Spirit enabled them” (Acts 2:4). Here, every word the disciples uttered came directly and immediately from the Holy Spirit who was filling their lives with power. They did not need anyone to prompt them because they had the real thing.

As a minister of the gospel, you should be concerned with reality more than artificial results. As you minister with the sole intention of pleasing God, true results will follow and you will not need to manipulate people to achieve them. Nowhere does God suggest that the end justifies the means. If you are serving Him, you can only do it His way.

The proper place of preaching is at the forefront of the pastor’s activities. Preaching is uniquely qualified to communicate the truth of God in such a way that it will mold the lives of the hearers. Its potential for good, then, is enormous, and this is why the spiritual adversary—Satan—will use every trick he has to turn us aside from its proper use. By being true to our calling as preachers of the gospel and by avoiding the traps of misuse, we can prepare the way for a spiritual revival in our churches.

**Q:** Provide two examples that accurately describe selfishness as it relates to the minister.

**A:** (1) The pulpit offers me a chance to demonstrate my learning, experience, and natural abilities that I am lending to the ministry. (2) The way to success in the ministry is to be well known. My preaching must not be limited to conventional sermons. I will endeavor to do whatever is necessary to draw crowds.

**Q:** What should the preacher do before preaching on subjects related to his or her own personal weaknesses?

**A:** I would suggest that you put the subject into effect in your life first. If it is a sinful habit, you should truly repent and seek deliverance from it. If it is failure, you should do what you have failed to do. If confession and restoration or reconciliation is needed, you should make this right first. Prove your victory over the weakness before teaching others.

**Q:** Based upon the preceding discussion, how does superficiality develop?

**A:** Because one has fallen out of love with Jesus Christ

**Q:** When the superficiality of ministers keeps them from finding solutions to the problems of their people, how do the ministers usually respond?

**A:** Blaming the lack of success on their people, circumstances, or the unexplainable ways of God. 2 CHAPTER

## 2 - The Purpose of Preaching

### The Presentation of Christ

#### 2.1.1 — Explain the central place of Jesus Christ in biblical preaching.

The Redeeming Christ

The purpose of preaching is to present Christ by the power of His Spirit so that people may be convicted of sin, converted to God, and challenged to attain spiritual maturity.

In one of the first outreaches from the newly formed church in Jerusalem, the evangelist Philip “went down to a city in Samaria and proclaimed the Christ there” (Acts 8:5). The record in Acts of those early Christians’ preaching confirms that Jesus was the grand theme of their sermons. 1 Corinthians 1:22–24 is a strong passage in which Paul presented the crucified Christ as the answer to the quest of both Jews and Greeks. He also declared, “For I resolved to know nothing while I was with you except Jesus Christ and him crucified” (1 Corinthians 2:2).

It is not enough to simply preach about Christ; we must preach Christ. To present the facts about Jesus as the eternal Son of God, as the historical babe of Bethlehem, as the great teacher and healer of Palestine* is inadequate. Our forgiveness is rooted in what He did at the cross. “In him we have redemption through his blood, the forgiveness of sins” (Ephesians 1:7). “Without the shedding of blood there is no forgiveness” (Hebrews 9:22). We must, therefore, direct our hearers to the cross of Jesus as the only means for our salvation. Without proclaiming the benefits of that death on the cross, there is no true gospel preaching. The cross is central to evangelical Christianity.

One Easter, a young preacher preached for an hour from Matthew 27:34 against drinking wine. He may have been related to another who took the next verse for a sermon against gambling. As important as those subjects may be in themselves, they are incidental to the main fact of the chapter: the atoning death of our Savior Jesus Christ. The message of the cross, then, is not a description of the physical properties of the wood but “that Christ died for our sins according to the Scriptures, that he was buried, that he was raised on the third day according to the Scriptures” (1 Corinthians 15:3–4). This, Paul declared, is “of first importance.” We need to major on the truths of the Bible since they are crucial to our faith.

The Resurrected Christ

A story is told of a Muslim speaking to a Christian, “You must admit that we have one thing you Christians do not.” The Christian asked, “What is that?” The Muslim said, “When we go to Mecca, we find Mohammad’s coffin. But what do you Christians find in your holy city, Jerusalem? Nothing but an empty grave.” The Christian responded, “That is just the difference. Muhammad is dead and in his coffin. So are all the other founders of the great religions. But Christ is not entombed. He is risen and is alive forever.”

The link between the crucifixion and the resurrection cannot be broken. Jesus revealed from heaven to His disciple John, “I am the Living One; I was dead, and behold I am alive for ever and ever! And I hold the keys of death and Hades” (Revelation 1:18). As the conqueror of death and the grave, He now holds authority over them. The Christian is delivered from fear of death. Paul’s great chapter on the resurrection, 1 Corinthians 15, reaches its climax in the triumphant words, “Where, O death, is your victory? Where, O death, is your sting? But thanks be to God! He gives us the victory through our Lord Jesus Christ” (1 Corinthians 15:55, 57).

The end of Luke’s Gospel sets the resurrection at the heart of the Christian message. Jesus commissioned His disciples, “This is what is written: The Christ will suffer and rise from the dead on the third day, and repentance and forgiveness of sins will be preached in his name to all nations, beginning at Jerusalem. You are witnesses of these things” (Luke 24:46–48).

In selecting a successor to Judas Iscariot, the betrayer of Jesus, Peter described the successor’s task as being “a witness with us of his Resurrection” (Acts 1:22). The succeeding chapters of Acts confirm that the resurrection was, in fact, the constant theme of the early Christian preachers (Acts 2:24–32; 3:13–15, 26; 4:10, 33).

The renowned British Congregationalist preacher, Robert William Dale (1829–1895) of Birmingham, when writing an Easter sermon, had a glorious revelation of the reality of the Resurrection:

“Christ is alive,” I said to myself; “alive!” and then I paused—“alive!” and then I paused again; “alive!” Can that really be true? Living as really as I myself am? I got up and walked about repeating “Christ is living!” “Christ is living!” . . . It was to me a new discovery. I thought that all along I had believed it; but not until that moment did I feel sure about it. I then said “My people shall know it; I shall preach about it again and again until they believe as I do now.” (Dale 1899, 642)

The sense of personal assurance, characteristic of the early Christians, arises directly from the certainty of a living Savior. Even at times of deepest distress and in the most difficult situations, the words that carried the great missionary pioneer David Livingstone through the unexplored regions of Central Africa can encourage you: “Lo, I am with you always, even unto the end of the world” (Matthew 28:20, KJV). When facing Satan’s strongest attacks that cause you to wonder whether you can make it through, the truth of the resurrection is like a ray of sunshine piercing the gloom. It will bring spiritual victory to your spirit. “He is able to save completely those who come to God through him, because he always lives to intercede for them” (Hebrews 7:25) .

The Returning Christ

The great theme of Jesus’ second coming occupies about one-twentieth of the whole New Testament; we have more information from Jesus about His return than about His death. He speaks of His return in over one hundred verses of Matthew, but He refers to His death in only twenty, which indicates the importance of the second coming in the mind of the Lord. The New Testament writers are more concerned about the effect that the teaching of the second coming of Christ should have on the Christians than the small details of time and order of events. The controversies within evangelical Christianity over these unspecified areas have made many preachers tired of the whole subject. Nevertheless, the broad scriptural facts of this event should be a feature in our preaching if we are to fully preach the gospel.

The relationship between the return of Jesus and personal purity and holiness was prominent not only in the earliest days of the church, but also toward the end of the first century. Then John wrote, “We know that when he appears, we shall be like him, for we shall see him as he is. Everyone who has this hope in him purifies himself, just as he is pure” (1 John 3:2–3). This is the believer’s grand assurance indicating that at the return of the Lord, the work of making us like Him will be completed.

The true purpose of preaching can only be realized as you make the second coming of Christ an integral part of your ministry. You do not have to be a prophetic scholar or understand the fine points of the various theories, but you should not neglect this important theme. Many of the moral problems perplexing Christians would be resolved more easily if they were aware that Jesus could be here at any moment to take account of His servants. His frequent exhortation is, “Therefore keep watch, because you do not know on what day your Lord will come” (Matthew 24:42).

**Q:** What should the central theme of evangelical preaching FOCUS on?

**A:** The atoning work of the cross

**Q:** Explain how you would preach to a congregation of people from mixed backgrounds and nationalities.

**A:** Your responses will vary. The response may concentrate on the central issue of the cross and resurrection and emphasize the uniqueness of the resurrection in comparison to all other religions.

**Q:** 4. From these verses in 1 Thessalonians, identify ways in which Jesus’ personal return affects every aspect of the Christian’s life: 1:10; 2:19; 3:13; 4:17–18; and 5:23–24.

**Q:** What are two keys to preaching for a verdict?

**A:** (1) preparation and (2) expectation

### The Goal of Preaching

#### 2.2.1 — Explain the need to preach for a verdict.

The Need for a Verdict

When T. D. Medhurst was a young preacher, “in the earliest days of [Spurgeon’s] Pastor’s college,” Medhurst complained to Charles H. Spurgeon that he knew of no one who got saved in three months of preaching on the streets of London. Spurgeon asked, “Do you expect the Lord to save souls every time you open your mouth?” Embarrassed, Medhurst replied, “Oh, no, sir!” Spurgeon then answered, “Then that is just the reason why you have not had conversions: ‘According to your faith be it unto you’” (Spurgeon 1962, 388).

We tend to get what we expect. We also prepare for what we expect. If we are expecting tangible results, we will prepare the kinds of sermons that will achieve them. Otherwise, we must be satisfied with imparting information or giving general encouragement. In Preaching and Preachers , Lloyd-Jones (1971) discussed how the Calvinistic Methodist Church in Wales had suffered a decline. The elderly man in his conversation pointed to the Revival of 1859 to be at the root of the problem. That powerful revival brought seemingly everyone into the church. The church stopped preaching for decisions for salvation, so the generation that followed grew up without the challenge for the gospel truth. Lloyd-Jones warns against assuming that all people in the church and even children of Christians are themselves Christians (151–152).

It is obvious from even a cursory reading of the New Testament that the early Christian preachers intended to bring their hearers to faith in Christ, and nothing less than a clear-cut decision would do. Over six hundred times in the Bible, God commands people to come; about four hundred of these are in the New Testament alone. God requires a decisive response to a clear invitation. Early in His ministry, Jesus commanded fishermen busy about their tasks, “Come, follow me, and I will make you fishers of men.” They immediately left their nets and followed Him (Matthew 4:18–20). He did not hesitate to summon them publicly to an immediate commitment, nor should we.

That this was the apostles’ aim, too, can be seen from the New Testament words for evangelistic preaching.

Proclaim

The Greek word kerusso occurs more than sixty times in the New Testament (Metzger 1997, 15). Basically, a herald who delivered the official messages of kings, princes, magistrates, or military leaders used the term for public proclamation. The herald’s manner always conveyed a dignity and an authority that had to be listened to and obeyed as though it were the ruler himself who spoke. It was not simply the recounting of certain events or recommendations; it was authoritative, demanding immediate action and implementation. He usually was given sufficient power to ensure that his message would be accepted.

John the Baptist came as a herald, preparing the way for Jesus and proclaiming, “Repent, for the kingdom of heaven is near” (Matthew 3:1–2). Similarly, the evangelist “Philip went down to a city in Samaria and proclaimed the Christ there. When the crowds heard Philip and saw the miraculous signs he did, they all paid close attention to what he said” (Acts 8:5–6).

Does this not contrast strongly with our timid, tentative approach? Our congregations need to hear forthright, authoritative proclamation of the word God has given us to deliver.

Evangelize

The Greek word evangelizo , which occurs some forty-nine times in the New Testament, is “to bring or announce good news” (Metzger 1997, 16). The term suggests that people need good news since the facts of human sin and need will be prominent. The fact that it is good news involves us in speaking of the content of the messages, such as, of the One who can meet human needs and of how He will meet those needs. Barnabas and Paul declared to the pagans at Lystra in Asia Minor, “We are bringing you good news, telling you to turn from these worthless things to the living God” (Acts 14:15). To the Ethiopian court official’s questions, the evangelist Philip responded by taking the same Scripture passage that the official had been reading, and he “told him the good news about Jesus” (Acts 8:35). The early preachers went out and “preached the word wherever they went” (Acts 8:4). To the Corinthian Christians, Paul wrote, “Now, brothers, I want to remind you of the gospel I preached to you. . . . By this gospel you are saved, if you hold firmly to the word I preached to you” (1 Corinthians 15:1–2).

From the first mission of the apostles in the days of Jesus, the ministry of healing was a normal accompaniment of gospel preaching. They “went from village to village, preaching the gospel and healing people everywhere” (Luke 9:6). When Paul wrote of having “fully proclaimed the gospel of Christ” wherever he had gone, he included “signs and miracles, [performed] through the power of the Spirit” (Romans 15:19). No wonder he declared, “That is why I am so eager to preach the gospel also to you who are at Rome. I am not ashamed of the gospel, because it is the power of God for the salvation of everyone who believes” (Romans 1:15–16).

Frequently, various intensive prefixes are used with the basic Greek term to emphasize preaching repeatedly, openly, universally, publicly, authoritatively, thoroughly, in anticipation, and to those who will themselves preach to others. Its breadth of use makes it a lesson in the principles of preaching.

Witness

Probably the most common preaching words in the New Testament, which together occur more than 160 times, are based on the Greek verb martureo (Metzger 1997, 14, 20, and 26). Although it has been used in English for those who die for their witness—the martyrs—it does not necessarily have that connotation. In fact, it is often harder to live for Christ than to die for Him. Living takes the rest of your life; dying is over in a moment. Nevertheless, our use of the term does emphasize the persistence and intensity of the witness.

The term properly means to testify to that which is personally known and experienced. It is normally used in the New Testament with the thought of imparting that experience to others:

That which was from the beginning, which we have heard, which we have seen with our eyes, which we have looked at and our hands have touched—this we proclaim concerning the Word of life. The life appeared; we have seen it and testify to it, and we proclaim to you the eternal life, which was with the Father and has appeared to us. We proclaim to you what we have seen and heard, so that you also may have fellowship with us. (1 John 1:1–3)

The basic factors of personal experience, public testimony, and evangelistic purpose are prominent in this account of John.

Testifying is not reminiscing or even recounting past events. Instead, it is revealing what God has done in our personal lives and therefore can do in the lives of our hearers. In modern times, we have separated the testimony from the sermon, but apostolic preaching was primarily witnessing and only later exposition. The first apostles were commissioned as witnesses (Luke 24:48; John 15:27) and gladly accepted that as their prime responsibility (Acts 1:22; 2:32; 3:15). When forbidden to speak further of Jesus, Peter and John responded, “We cannot help speaking about what we have seen and heard” (Acts 4:20).

The Holy Spirit in the believer’s life is directly linked with the work of witness, both as the enabling power (Luke 24:49; Acts 1:8) and as the confirming agent (John 15:26; Acts 5:32). The New Testament points to His ministry as essential to effective witnessing, for when we have said all, only the Holy Spirit can make our experience valid in the life of another person.

Do not be satisfied with anything less than New Testament preaching. Peter declared of the preachers of his day, “[They] have preached the gospel to you by the Holy Spirit” (1 Peter 1:12). They are marked by this strongly evangelistic urge. “We try to persuade men,” Paul asserted (2 Corinthians 5:11). Do you? Do it as the normal expression of your preaching ministry, and you stand in that pattern of ministry that reaches back to the New Testament apostles and our Lord Jesus Himself.

**Q:** How would you, as a messenger of Christ, apply Matthew 3:1–2 and Acts 8:5–6 to your preaching ministry?

**A:** Your response may vary, but could include that the passages teach us to preach with authority, expecting that lives will be changed through hearing the proclamation of the Word.

**Q:** For each of these passages, state what normally accompanied gospel preaching in the New Testament: Luke 9:6; Romans 15:19; and Romans 1:15–16.

**A:** Healing; Signs and miracles; Power of God; Salvation for the unbeliever

**Q:** Witnessing, as a part of preaching, must begin with what?

**A:** Sharing from personal experience

**Q:** How has preaching changed since the first apostles were commissioned? In your opinion, how has this change affected the results of preaching?

**A:** In modern times, we have separated the testimony from the sermon. The second part of the response will vary but should include that it is the personal testimony that often brings people to Christ. It also gives a natural opening for a response to the invitation of the gospel.

### The Social Impact of Preaching

#### 2.3.1 — Compare preaching with teaching.

Education

Preaching without the clear aim for response is inadequate; preaching that does not promote a lifestyle of holiness also leaves the task unfinished. Your hearers have a right to know what kind of life God will expect them to live if they respond to the gospel. The Christian life begins when we enter into that saving relationship with Jesus, but that is not the point of arrival; it is the point of beginning. We now begin to live for Him. From the initial saving experience stems a saved life.

There are more references in Acts to discipling and teaching than to all other forms of ministry. Out of ninety-two references to ministry, forty-seven refer directly to this educational aspect. Jesus stressed the importance of the teaching ministry in the ongoing life of the church when He commanded His disciples, “Go and make disciples of [KJV “teach”] all nations . . . teaching them to obey everything I have commanded you” (Matthew 28:19–20). Among His ministry gifts to the church are “pastors and teachers” who are responsible in the local church, especially for the educational aspect of the work (Ephesians 4:11–13).

Paul described himself as “a herald and an apostle . . . and a teacher of the true faith to the Gentiles” (1 Timothy 2:7). Despite his fame as an apostle, he never forgot that his first public ministry in the church at Antioch had been that of teacher (Acts 13:1).

While it may be possible to differentiate between preaching and teaching on stylistic grounds, this difference is invalid from the point of content. All preaching should include teaching since if there is no communication of truth, there is no preaching in the biblical sense of the term. This unscriptural distinction between preaching and teaching has too often been used as an excuse for preachers to neglect the content of their sermons, and for teachers to dryly and dully overwhelm congregations with half-digested and highly confusing masses of information. “A preacher,” declared Martin Luther, the great Reformer, “should have the skill to teach the unlearned simply, roundly and plainly; for teaching is of more importance than exhorting.” By adopting Luther’s three marks of simplicity of thought, directness of style, and clarity of speech, we will be easily understood by the educated and uneducated alike.

A simply dressed elderly woman approached Hugh Benner, a noted gospel minister, at the close of a Sunday morning meeting and said, “When I hear a man preach, I like to learn something.” So do most people. That is the way they grow and mature in their faith. “Grow in the grace and knowledge of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ” (2 Peter 3:18). The learning process is lifelong. What I learn today, I live tomorrow. If the preacher has not learned the things of God, he or she cannot teach others. If they have not so mastered those things that they can communicate them to others in clear, uncomplicated terms, they are depriving them of the opportunity of learning the things of God. Before you preach, then, ask yourself, “Would I travel five miles (eight kilometers) to hear that sermon?” If not, throw it away; it is not worth preaching.

Ethics

Ethics deals with the principles that govern our actions, right or wrong. Education must be translated into action if it is to prove effective in our individual lives. Clear ethical sections follow the great doctrinal teaching in Paul’s letters. Christianity is not just concerned with what we should know and believe, but also with how we should live. “Faith by itself, if it is not accompanied by action, is dead” (James 2:17). This does not deny the basic doctrines of salvation by faith; it simply stresses the ethical expression of that faith. Paul exhorted the Philippians, “Continue to work out your salvation with fear and trembling.” But he reminded them, “It is God who works in you to will and to act according to his good purpose” (Philippians 2:12–13). We can only work out what God has first of all worked in us. Even the very will that precedes the act is the direct result of His operation within us. Nevertheless, we are responsible to God for the outworking of our faith.

At the heart of the Christian faith, molding the believer’s actions, is love. When asked which was the greatest commandment, Jesus replied, “‘Love the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul and with all your mind.’ This is the first and greatest commandment. And the second is like it: ‘Love your neighbor as yourself”’ (Matthew 22:37–39). Paul took up the same point when he wrote, “The commandments, ‘Do not commit adultery,’ ‘Do not murder,’ ‘Do not steal,’ ‘Do not covet,’ and whatever other commandment there may be, are summed up in this one rule: ‘Love your neighbor as yourself!’ Love does no harm to its neighbor. Therefore love is the fulfillment of the Law” (Romans 13:9–10).

James, the early church leader, suggested the ironic example of the Christian greeting a naked and hungry fellow Christian with, “Go, I wish you well; keep warm and well fed,” but doing nothing about the man’s physical needs (James 2:15–16). “If anyone has material possessions,” wrote John, “and sees his brother in need but has no pity on him, how can the love of God be in him? Dear children, let us not love with words or tongue but with actions and in truth” (1 John 3:17–18).

Teaching Christians to translate verbal professions of love into active involvement in the needs of suffering humanity is as much the ethical concern of the gospel as is keeping free from sin and carnality. By stressing the ethical challenge of the gospel, you will fulfill your call to mature believers in Christ.

Edification

The purpose of the ministry in the church is ultimately to see the individual believer “reach unity in the faith and in the knowledge of the Son of God and become mature, attaining to the whole measure of the fullness of Christ” (Ephesians 4:13). Knowledge is an important aspect of our growth since we need a regular, nourishing diet of spiritual food—the Word of God—to sustain our spiritual life. This spiritual feeding must be on a daily basis to maintain proper growth patterns.

Picture a household where the members deliberately eat only once a week, on a Sunday. Or, imagine one in which the children are told that they are too young to eat at all, but when they are about twelve years old, they can decide for themselves whether to eat or not. Such situations would be laughable if they were not so serious. In conditions of famine, parents will deprive themselves to feed their children, and when food is plentiful, all will eat. Yet many Christian families are behaving in the way we have pictured as far as spiritual food is concerned. Adult believers must feed their children and themselves at home, and the preaching and teaching ministry of the church must feed the whole family.

Paul, however, gave a necessary caution, “Knowledge puffs up, but love builds up. The man who thinks he knows something does not yet know as he ought to know. But the man who loves God is known by God” (1 Corinthians 8:1–3). Knowledge increases size, but love adds substance. Love is knowledge in action. Without appropriate actions, the well-fed person will become overweight and lack physical stamina. He or she may even die. Similarly, in the spiritual realm loving deeds are the healthy activity that will translate food (knowledge) into substantial growth and maturity.

By regularly focusing the attention of your people on Jesus you will steadily build them up to the maturity that reflects Jesus Himself. “We proclaim him, admonishing and teaching everyone with all wisdom, so that we may present everyone perfect in Christ. To this end I labor, struggling with all his energy, which so powerfully works in me” (Colossians 1:28–29). This is the goal of your labor too.

**Q:** List the three marks that Martin Luther considered to be important teaching skills, and explain their importance.

**A:** (1) Simplicity of thought, (2) directness of style, and (3) clarity of speech. The goal is that every person in the audience, regardless of his or her educational level, will clearly understand the presentation.

**Q:** Explain briefly the relationship between preaching and teaching. How are they different? How are they similar?

**A:** Your own response but should include that teaching and preaching have different styles; however, the content should be the same. All preaching should include teaching; therefore, they are closely related.

**Q:** After reviewing the Bible passages in this section, give some specific examples of how love can be put into action in modern society.

**A:** Your response will vary. Some examples are supplying food to the hungry, sheltering the homeless, and giving aid to victims of disaster.

**Q:** If you were the pastor of a congregation, how would you edify or help your church grow and mature as believers?

**A:** Preaching and teaching messages will feed people spiritually. Then, challenging them to exercise spiritually by actively sharing God’s love with others will enable them to grow. 3 CHAPTER
