By regularly focusing the attention of your people on Jesus you will steadily build them up to the maturity that reflects Jesus Himself. “We proclaim him, admonishing and teaching everyone with all wisdom, so that we may present everyone perfect in Christ. To this end I labor, struggling with all his energy, which so powerfully works in me” (Colossians 1:28–29). This is the goal of your labor too.

**Q:** List the three marks that Martin Luther considered to be important teaching skills, and explain their importance.

**A:** (1) Simplicity of thought, (2) directness of style, and (3) clarity of speech. The goal is that every person in the audience, regardless of his or her educational level, will clearly understand the presentation.

**Q:** Explain briefly the relationship between preaching and teaching. How are they different? How are they similar?

**A:** Your own response but should include that teaching and preaching have different styles; however, the content should be the same. All preaching should include teaching; therefore, they are closely related.

**Q:** After reviewing the Bible passages in this section, give some specific examples of how love can be put into action in modern society.

**A:** Your response will vary. Some examples are supplying food to the hungry, sheltering the homeless, and giving aid to victims of disaster.

**Q:** If you were the pastor of a congregation, how would you edify or help your church grow and mature as believers?

**A:** Preaching and teaching messages will feed people spiritually. Then, challenging them to exercise spiritually by actively sharing God’s love with others will enable them to grow. 3 CHAPTER

## 3 - The Preparation of the Preacher

### The Preacher’s Walk with God

#### 3.1.1 — Explain how the preacher’s Christian life relates to his or her role as a preacher.

A Sense of Personal Forgiveness

True preaching is rooted in the preacher’s experience of God. An old pastor once explained, “You can no more tell them what you do not know than you can come back from where you have not been.” Preachers must know God themselves if their hearers are to know Him. Knowing that God has forgiven all their sins, preachers can enthusiastically recommend their Savior to others. “There is nothing that makes a man so able to preach Christ to the people, as getting Christ inside him,” asserted the Puritan Thomas Brooks.

Preachers must retain their sense of indebtedness to God if they are to retain their fervor. The self-made person is foreign to the ministry. “By the grace of God I am what I am,” Paul declared. “I worked harder than all of them—yet not I, but the grace of God that was with me” (1 Corinthians 15:10). Whatever Paul had achieved in pioneering the gospel message across Asia Minor and Europe was due entirely to God’s grace (undeserved favor). Humility marks men like Paul. The preacher is not a breed apart from other people. If anything, he should be more conscious than ever that he is of the common stock of life.

This sense of identification is reflected beautifully in the life of noble Father Damien. For years, his ministry to the lepers on Molokai was ineffectual. He was an intruder into their world of suffering until the fateful Sunday morning when he climbed into the pulpit of the little chapel and began his sermon with “We lepers.” He was now one of them. For the preacher, it is ever “we sinners!” Forgiven? Yes! Redeemed? Yes! Delivered? Yes! But they are unable to forget where they began.

As a preacher, you must avoid any feeling that you are better than your hearers or are unaware of the needs of sinful people. By reminding yourself of how God met your needs through our Lord Jesus Christ, you will keep that vital humility, that deep sense of identification, and, most of all, that burning love for the Savior.

A Longing for Personal Holiness

A preacher will seldom deliberately turn from the narrow path of holy living, but through failing to guard his or her devotional life with the Lord, he or she may drift into bad habits and eventually suffer spiritual loss. Solomon warned that it is “the little foxes that ruin the vineyards, our vineyards that are in bloom” (Song of Solomon 2:15). It is easy to guard against the “big” sins, but the little secret sins can creep in sometimes hardly noticed.

Some preachers have gained a reputation for exaggerating the truth to make an illustration more appropriate or spectacular. Exaggeration is still lying. If that story did not happen the way you tell it, you are not speaking truth. “Daddy,” said the little boy as his preacher-father was entertaining dinner guests with one of his stories, “Is that really what happened or are you just preaching again?” God forbid that preaching should ever become another name for lying.

But the preacher’s life may be a lie too. God alone knows what a person is really like. Remember, “Man looks at the outward appearance, but the Lord looks at the heart” (1 Samuel 16:7). Even if our people do not see our failures or ignore them when they do, it is to God that we must one day give an account of the things we have done and even of our thoughts. In the home we must live the same kind of life that we preach in the pulpit. If your spouse and children were to describe publicly your life at home, would your ministry be confirmed or contradicted? At home and in public, the preacher is to be “an example for the believers” (1 Timothy 4:12).

You cannot encourage others in the way of holiness if you are not walking in that way yourself. Paul’s commitment was to “press on toward the goal to win the prize for which God has called me heavenward in Christ Jesus” (Philippians 3:14). You should never even give the impression that you are prepared to settle for less than the best kind of life for God. Maintain that close walk with Jesus in personal Bible reading and prayer, and you will maintain that deep longing to be like Him.

A Personal Recognition of the Lordship of Christ

The honored title of prophets and preachers alike in biblical terms was “servants” of God (2 Kings 17:13; Titus 1:1). “So then, men ought to regard us as servants of Christ and as those entrusted with the secret things of God,” explained Paul. “Now it is required that those who have been given a trust must prove faithful” (1 Corinthians 4:1–2). To be servants of Jesus Christ means that we not only have a saving relationship with Him, but also have surrendered to His authority. He is Lord.

We are His representatives summoning the world of lost humanity to repentance and faith in the Savior Jesus Christ. “We are therefore Christ’s ambassadors, as though God were making his appeal through us. We implore you on Christ’s behalf: Be reconciled to God” (2 Corinthians 5:20). Servants are directly responsible to their Lord for the way they perform their work. Whether we are approved or not rests wholly upon the Lord’s judgment. Therefore, we must serve by obeying absolutely His revealed will. “‘Why do you call me, ‘Lord, Lord,’ and do not do what I say?’” asked Jesus (Luke 6:46). Unless we have learned to obey our Lord we will be serving ourselves rather than Him. What is your call to the ministry? Is it the ministry of your local church, your denomination, the church of God worldwide? Or are you called to the ministry of God Himself? If you are His servant, your primary loyalty is to Him. Our loyalties are always subordinate to that supreme commitment to His service.

A Personal Gift of the Holy Spirit

When Jesus commissioned His disciples to be His witnesses and to preach “repentance and forgiveness of sins” throughout the world, He warned them to first “stay in the city until you have been clothed with power from on high” (Luke 24:47–49). He expressly identified this power with the Holy Spirit when He said, “You will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes on you; and you will be my witnesses . . . to the ends of the earth” (Acts 1:8). This promise was clearly fulfilled on the Day of Pentecost when “all of them were filled with the Holy Spirit and began to speak in other tongues as the Spirit enabled them” (Acts 2:4). Peter reminded his hearers that the same power of the Holy Spirit is available to all believers in every age (Acts 2:38–39).

Throughout the Old Testament the servants of God were endued with the Spirit of God for the service they were to perform. It was His Spirit that rested on individuals and gave them gifts and talents to use for His glory.

Even Jesus did not begin His public ministry until after the Holy Spirit rested upon Him at His baptism in the river Jordan (Matthew 3:16–17). On this F. B. Meyer commented, “If Christ waited to be anointed before He went to preach, no young man ought to preach until he, too, has been anointed by the Holy Ghost.” Of Jesus’ earthly life the Scriptures reveal, to Him “God gives the Spirit without limit” (John 3:34). But now ascended into heaven, “exalted to the right hand of God, he has received from the Father the promised Holy Spirit and has poured out what you now see and hear” (Acts 2:33).

The power, which was occasional in the Old Testament, was unlimited in the ministry of Jesus. That same power of the Holy Spirit is now available to every believer. It is the necessary equipment of every preacher. Trying to do God’s work without using the essential power He has made available is like trying to deliver gasoline without filling the truck’s own gas tank. Paul asserted, “My message and my preaching were not with wise and persuasive words, but with a demonstration of the Spirit’s power, so that your faith might not rest on men’s wisdom, but on God’s power” (1 Corinthians 2:4–5). As important as education and eloquence may be, they can never be substitutes for the power of God’s Spirit.

Conviction of sin is part of the Holy Spirit’s work in this age. When preachers rely for conviction upon their own personalities or preaching abilities or the strength of their arguments, they may persuade the minds of their hearers, but they cannot reach their hearts and bring them to repentance and faith in the Savior. “Our gospel came to you not simply with words,” Paul reminded the Thessalonian believers, “but also with power, with the Holy Spirit and with deep conviction” (1 Thessalonians 1:5) . So often as they preached, the apostles saw that power of the Spirit demonstrated in miracles and healings accompanying and confirming the Word. Paul’s preaching was fully confirmed “by the power of signs and miracles, through the power of the Spirit” (Romans 15:19). If we have the same power resting upon our ministry, we can expect the same confirmation.

As you receive the Holy Spirit and learn to rely upon Him, more than your own resources are available to you. You have the great ability of the Almighty at work with you, in you, and through you. You may be the one seen preaching, but God is the one doing the work of convicting and converting sinners and confirming the Word.

**Q:** How will you seek humility and a sense of identification with members of your congregation?

**A:** Your own response

**Q:** Based on Romans 6:16, 22, what is the reward of obedience? What effect will obedience have on your preaching ministry?

**A:** The reward of obedience is righteousness. As servants of the Lord, our obedience will lead to a life of holiness, which can then be an example in our preaching ministry.

**Q:** Why is Acts 2:38–39 significant to the modern-day preacher?

**A:** We can depend on the Holy Spirit’s power to lead men and women to Christ today, just as on the Day of Pentecost.

**Q:** Identify the ways in which the servants of God listed in these passages were used to carry out His plan: Numbers 11:25; Micah 3:8; and Exodus 31:1–5.

**A:** Numbers 11:25—Moses exercised the gift of leadership. Micah 3:8—Micah prophesied about God’s redemptive purpose. Exodus 31:1–5—Bazalel supervised the building of the tabernacle.

**Q:** What has God accomplished in your life through encounters when His Spirit came upon you?

**A:** Your own response

### The Preacher’s Burden for Souls

#### 3.2.1 — Explain the significance of a spiritual burden for the preaching ministry.

A Passionate Desire to Share

“The best sermon is preached by the minister who has a sermon to preach and not by the man who has to preach a sermon,” declared a church leader. The deep earnestness that marks out the effective preacher is nothing less than a passion or burden for the lost. This preacher seeks to rescue the perishing not because duty demands it , but because a real sense of urgency burns within his or her heart. He or she can do nothing else. “We cannot help speaking about what we have seen and heard,” testified Peter and John (Acts 4:20).

In his book Power in Preaching , W. E. Sangster (1900–1960) asserts, “Some preaching fails in power because it fails in passion” (1958, 86). The sermon may be well prepared and well delivered, but it is a performance rather than real preaching. Preaching must be real if it is to be anything. The preacher must be in it heart and soul, feeling the emotions that he or she wishes to portray in the sermon. Artificial passion is as distasteful in preaching as it would be in love. Pretense of any kind is a form of hypocrisy that robs the ministry of its credibility. People can detect it more easily than we often realize.

A real passion for souls is a glow from within; the counterfeit is a shine applied to the outside. The glow comes from a heart that is burning with love for God and for the people. In India, Henry Martyn prayed, “Here let me burn out for God.” Every person entering the preaching ministry must be spiritually on fire for God if that one is to do anything of enduring worth. This passion arises from the awareness of several important facts.

The Consequences of Sin

If anything will give a man or woman a burden for the lost, the knowledge of the consequences of sin will. “The wages of sin is death,” Paul wrote (Romans 6:23). These wages will surely be paid unless a person receives God’s gift of life in Christ. For those who have not entered into a saving relationship with Jesus, “It is a dreadful thing to fall into the hands of the living God” (Hebrews 10:31). Hell is a dreadful reality.

General William Booth, founder of the Salvation Army, said that he would like to dangle every one of his preachers over hell for twenty-four hours and then send them out as the greatest evangelists of the day. Could a man or woman look into a lost eternity and not feel such a stirring in his or her soul that nothing would distract him or her from seeking to turn the sinners from that awful fate? How could such a person remain indifferent to the sinner’s fate? It was said of John Wesley, “All who perish are his parish.” Like him, you know what sin is and you know what sin does. This knowledge should compel you to exert every effort to reach the sinner for Christ before it is too late.

The Confidence of Salvation

William Booth once reminisced, “I was made a red-hot Salvationist by an infidel [bolding added] lecturer. That lecturer said, ‘If I believed what some of you Christians believe, I would never rest day or night from telling men about it.’” Not only are we aware of the reality of hell, we know the saving power of Jesus. He “rescues us from the coming wrath” (1 Thessalonians 1:10). We know that “God has given us eternal life, and this life is in his Son” (1 John 5:11).

The zeal with which people recommend effective products, remedies, and recipes to their friends should be far surpassed by our commendation of our Savior. Our confidence rests upon our personal experience. What He has done for us in forgiving us and making us part of that new creation, we know He can do for all. Speaking of Jesus, John affirmed, “He is the atoning sacrifice for our sins, and not only for ours but also for the sins of the whole world” (1 John 2:2). Sir Henry Holland, the honored missionary doctor, was once asked the meaning of his motto: “Not only . . . but also.” He told how as a young medical student in Edinburgh, Scotland, the above Scripture verse had burned into his soul at a missionary rally. He then realized that Christ had died for the whole world, not just the chosen few as he had previously imagined. That was what sent him to India’s northwest frontier. In the same way, you, too, can work confident that your Savior is also the Savior of the world.

The Constraint of the Savior

Paul testified, “Christ’s love compels us, because we are convinced that one died for all . . . that those who live should no longer live for themselves but for him who died for them and was raised again” (2 Corinthians 5:14–15).

Imagine the scene in heaven before Jesus came to earth. As He looks forward at all that is to happen, He sees His birth in a stable to a young girl in an obscure corner of the Roman Empire. He sees His life of poverty, privation, and at times persecution. He sees the agony of the Garden, the betrayal by Judas Iscariot, the denial by one of His closest friends, the cruelty of the judgment, the barbarity of the crucifixion, and the utter desolation of His death. Contrast all that earth held for Jesus with all the glory and magnificence of heaven, and ask the question, “Can you pay that kind of price? Knowing what it will cost, can you still do it?” “Jesus . . . for the joy set before him endured the cross, scorning its shame” (Hebrews 12:2). In Romans, Paul explained, “You see, at just the right time, when we were still powerless, Christ died for the ungodly. Very rarely will anyone die for a righteous man, though for a good man someone might possibly dare to die. But God demonstrates his own love for us in this: While we were still sinners, Christ died for us” (5:6–8).

The reason Jesus faced all that redemption cost may be summed in one word, love . That kind of love leaves no choices; it sees what has to be done and does it out of love alone. The old Life magazine once carried the story of an orphaned family of five children from southern California. The oldest child, a sixteen-year-old girl, had worked and kept house for her brothers and sisters for three years so that the family would not be split up in foster homes. “Why did you do it?” she was asked. “You did not have to. No one could have expected you to do this.” “It is true that no one said I have to,” the girl replied, “but what about the ‘have to’ inside of me? I was all they had.” You are all the sinner has. If you do not feel a “have to” inside of you, you have not really seen what the cross is all about.

“To love to preach is one thing,” declared a renowned minister. “To love those to whom we preach, quite another.” Those who love to preach see the pulpit as a place for exercising their abilities. They may excel in learning and eloquence, and delight congregations with brilliant sermons. But the person with a burden for the lost, the person with a burning love for the Lord, the person who thinks no price too high to pay to turn one person back from a Christless eternity: that person is the preacher. “Preach the Word,” wrote Paul. “Press forward with this whether it is convenient or not” (2 Timothy 4:2, author’s translation). Personal convenience is irrelevant to the preacher with a passion for souls. Are you prepared to be that kind of preacher?

**Q:** Think about your reasons for wanting to preach the gospel. Compare your motivations with the ones discussed in the following sections.

**A:** Your answer. After studying the chapter, review your answer and see how your reasons for preaching compare with those listed in the IST.

**Q:** Explain how understanding the consequences of sin can give a preacher a burden for the lost.

**A:** If preachers truly believe in an eternal hell, their preaching will be urgent and passionate. They will not want one person to experience the physical and emotional pain of an eternity in hell.

### The Preacher’s Call to the Preaching Ministry

#### 3.3.1 — Identify biblical examples of the call of God to individuals.

The Necessity of a Call

The call to preach is an essential prerequisite. “If any man does not ‘have’ to preach,” remarked Gerald Kennedy, “let him do something else or he will have a miserable time of it.” This sense of compulsion is often referred to as a call . But is it really necessary?

Some have argued that God does not specifically call people to the ministry; rather, they believe that every person is as much called as the preacher is. The call consists simply in doing the work that is there for one to do. If a person wants to do this work and has the qualifications and talent, that one can be employed as a preacher just as easily as in any other work. With such thinking, ministers will readily change jobs and take up selling or school teaching without any feeling of guilt at leaving the ministry.

Other people believe that anyone can do it: any intelligent Christian who knows the Bible and is fairly eloquent could preach if he or she had the inclination. Instead of the professional we have the gifted amateur , for whom preaching is a hobby to be pursued at leisure. Some people leaving the full-time ministry of the Word maintain this kind of interest in preaching as a sideline.

It is true that all Christians are called upon to serve the Lord in whatever work they do. “Whatever you do,” exhorted Paul, “work at it with all your heart, as working for the Lord, not for men. . . . It is the Lord Christ you are serving” (Colossians 3:23–24). It is also true that all Christians are called to share their faith with others in personal testimony. Peter wrote, “Always be prepared to give an answer to everyone who asks you to give the reason for the hope that you have” (1 Peter 3:15). Even in the body of Christ, the church, the role of ministers is “to prepare God’s people for works of service” (Ephesians 4:12).

Nevertheless, the Bible teaches that God calls believers to the ministry of preaching in such a way that they can do nothing else and be in the will of God for their lives. With Paul they say, “When I preach the gospel, I cannot boast, for I am compelled to preach. Woe to me if I do not preach the gospel!” (1 Corinthians 9:16).

Paul likened the preaching ministry to the service of the Jewish temple, “Don’t you know that those who work in the temple get their food from the temple, and those who serve at the altar share in what is offered on the altar? In the same way, the Lord has commanded that those who preach the gospel should receive their living from the gospel” (1 Corinthians 9:13–14).

Even though Paul voluntarily did not exercise that right while at Corinth, he stressed that preaching is normally a full-time occupation. The preacher should allow nothing to distract him from this.

Even legitimate duties in the church are secondary. When questions arose in the early church over the distribution of food to the widows, the disciples met together and said, “It would not be right for us to neglect the ministry of the word of God in order to wait on tables. Brothers, choose seven men from among you who are known to be full of the Spirit and wisdom. We will turn this responsibility over to them and will give our attention to prayer and the ministry of the word” (Acts 6:2–4).

The call to preach should always take precedence over every other concern. Because of the level of commitment required by those who are to be preachers, a call is essential. Without this special call to the ministry you cannot survive the demands of the work. The sense of urgency within makes you willing to do it.

The Reception of a Call

In the biblical accounts of the calls of Moses, Isaiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, Amos, and the apostles, certain characteristics stand out that indicate the way in which a call usually comes. First, God makes the initial move. Sometimes it is a spectacular burning bush or Damascus Road type of experience. When we speak of a call, we usually have this distinct, unmistakable encounter in mind. However, in the account of his call, Isaiah “heard the voice of the Lord saying, ‘Whom shall I send? And who will go for us?’ and Isaiah said, ‘Here am I. Send me!’ He said, ‘Go and tell this people’” (Isaiah 6:8–9). Although Isaiah volunteered, God made the first move in revealing the need to the young man’s heart.

The next step is a response from the individual that often shows a shrinking from the responsibilities of the ministry, a true fear of the work. “I am too young” (Jeremiah); “I am not eloquent” (Moses); “No one in my family has been a prophet” (Amos); “I am not good enough” (Simon Peter). This is to be expected since no one can truly consider him or herself fit to be an ambassador for Christ. “Who is equal to such a task?” asked Paul (2 Corinthians 2:16). Realizing what is involved in preaching makes people inevitably feel inadequate and unworthy. If they could do anything else, they would gladly do it.

God’s insistence and commission overcomes the reluctance of the called person. With a clear assurance of the enablement of divine power and presence, he or she enters the work. “As the Father has sent me, I am sending you,” declared Jesus. “Receive the Holy Spirit” (John 20:21–22). Paul asked a very pointed question, “How can they preach unless they are sent?” (Romans 10:15). It is this consciousness of being sent by God that is at the heart of the call . Unless you sense this inward awareness of God’s call, all the recommendations of others that you should enter the ministry cannot constitute a warrant for you to become a preacher. God alone makes preachers; the church merely recognizes what God has done.

The Verification of a Call

One hot day a Kansas farmer’s son was out plowing the cornfield behind a team of mules that seemed to have a mind of their own. Feeling hot and very frustrated, he looked to the horizon and saw there an unusual formation of clouds that seemed to spell “PC.” Rushing to the pastor’s home he told of his conviction that the letters meant “Preach Christ.” Considering the young fellow for a moment, the pastor rejoined, “More likely, ‘Plow Corn!’” How can we know whether we are really called or not? It is possible to interpret the most innocent coincidence as a call to the ministry if that is what we really want to do.

Preachers do not have an earthly employer to whom they are accountable. They can laze away the days if they are so inclined. Nor do they face the same moral challenges and pressures that people in their congregations do. Their center of work is the church among the saints. Even when they meet the unconverted, they are often granted a respect that sets them apart from their church members.

The status often granted to ministers can make this a very desirable profession. To the young person, the pastor or the visiting evangelist can appear to be living an exciting, glamorous kind of life. After a furloughed missionary had given a moving account of her work in one of my churches, a young girl breathlessly announced to me, “Pastor, I think God wants me to be a missionary on furlough.” The work itself is not the attraction; it is the prestige.

Well-meaning people will recommend any talented young person to enter the preaching ministry. Even parents sometimes dedicate their children to the ministry before they are born and go to great lengths to ensure that they get there. Sometimes preacher’s children feel that they ought to follow in their parents’ footsteps, and they often enter the ministry as a matter of course.

Whatever brings a person into the ministry, we owe it to the Lord, to the church, and to ourselves to be sure that this is where God wants us to be. We must not be mistaken here. How then can we know that we have a real call? Are there any objective tests? In my opinion, we may ask the following:

1. Do you have a personal experience of salvation and the baptism of the Holy Spirit?

2. Does your life at home and in public confirm the faith you profess?

3. Have you demonstrated a consistent Christian life in service in your local church?

4. Do you have a burden for the lost, and have you been used of God to bring souls to Christ?

5. Could you ever leave the preaching ministry? (1 Timothy 3:1–7; Titus 1:6–9; 1 Peter 5:1–4)

While these are not conclusive, they are firmly based on the scriptural qualifications for the ministry. Finally, only you can know whether you are called or not. No one can decide this matter for you. Let no one push you into the preaching ministry and let no one push you out.

**Q:** Read the accounts of the following individuals who were called to the preaching ministry. Describe their calling and how it affected their personal lives.

**A:** Jeremiah (Jeremiah 1:4–9, 20:9) Paul (Acts 9:3–6, 1 Corinthians 9:16) Simon Peter and Andrew (Matthew 4:18–20) Jeremiah knew that God had called him to His service. The Lord had touched his life as a young person. He could never escape that commission. Paul had a direct encounter with God that lasted three days. It completely changed his plan for his life. From that time on he was aware of being set apart to preach the gospel. For Simon Peter and Andrew, following Christ involved giving up their professions immediately and following Christ in ministry. Among others, you could include Moses, Ezekiel, and Amos in this list.

**Q:** Jeremiah (Jer. 1:4–9; 20:9)

**Q:** Paul (Acts 9:3–6; 1 Cor. 9:16)

**Q:** Simon Peter and Andrew (Matt. 4:18–20)

**Q:** What are some modern-day examples of the situation described in Acts 6:2–4? In your ministry, how will you deal with situations such as these?

**A:** Your own response. Everything from preparing the church bulletin to teaching Sunday school and providing transportation are important aspects of ministry. However, it is necessary that you keep your calling to preach in focus as your primary task. Involve others in helping with the various other needs of the church.

**Q:** Use several biblical examples to explain how the call to ministry may take place.

**A:** The call of God can take place anywhere at anytime. Moses was tending sheep when God appeared in a spectacular, supernatural way (the burning bush). In Paul’s case it was similar: while Paul was walking on the road, Christ appeared in a “light from heaven.” Many times the call is an audible voice as indicated in the life of Isaiah. God also calls at different stages in life. While Jeremiah and Samuel were very young, Paul was already established in his career.

**Q:** How, when, and where did your call take place? Did you resist the call or make excuses? Describe your feelings.

**A:** Your answer.

**Q:** Read 1 Timothy 3:1–7, and describe the qualities that Paul lists as essential for a pastor.

**A:** The pastor should be self-controlled and moderate in all areas of life. Other qualities would include a person who is respectable, hospitable, able to teach, gentle, and generous. He or she should manage family well and his or her children should be well behaved. He or she should be established as a Christian for more than a short time and should have a good reputation in the community.

**Q:** After studying Titus 1:6–9, compare the pastorate to other vocations. What are the areas that are vastly different for the minister of the gospel?

**A:** The pastorate is different from other vocations in that one’s personal and professional life must be an example at all times. The pastor must not only be in control of himself, but his wife and children should also be an example of a godly life to unbelievers. The pastor is never completely off duty as in other walks of life. Instead, he or she is always a faithful servant of God. UNIT 2

#### 3.3.2 — Use biblical examples to describe how a call to ministry may occur.

The Necessity of a Call

The call to preach is an essential prerequisite. “If any man does not ‘have’ to preach,” remarked Gerald Kennedy, “let him do something else or he will have a miserable time of it.” This sense of compulsion is often referred to as a call . But is it really necessary?

Some have argued that God does not specifically call people to the ministry; rather, they believe that every person is as much called as the preacher is. The call consists simply in doing the work that is there for one to do. If a person wants to do this work and has the qualifications and talent, that one can be employed as a preacher just as easily as in any other work. With such thinking, ministers will readily change jobs and take up selling or school teaching without any feeling of guilt at leaving the ministry.

Other people believe that anyone can do it: any intelligent Christian who knows the Bible and is fairly eloquent could preach if he or she had the inclination. Instead of the professional we have the gifted amateur , for whom preaching is a hobby to be pursued at leisure. Some people leaving the full-time ministry of the Word maintain this kind of interest in preaching as a sideline.

It is true that all Christians are called upon to serve the Lord in whatever work they do. “Whatever you do,” exhorted Paul, “work at it with all your heart, as working for the Lord, not for men. . . . It is the Lord Christ you are serving” (Colossians 3:23–24). It is also true that all Christians are called to share their faith with others in personal testimony. Peter wrote, “Always be prepared to give an answer to everyone who asks you to give the reason for the hope that you have” (1 Peter 3:15). Even in the body of Christ, the church, the role of ministers is “to prepare God’s people for works of service” (Ephesians 4:12).

Nevertheless, the Bible teaches that God calls believers to the ministry of preaching in such a way that they can do nothing else and be in the will of God for their lives. With Paul they say, “When I preach the gospel, I cannot boast, for I am compelled to preach. Woe to me if I do not preach the gospel!” (1 Corinthians 9:16).

Paul likened the preaching ministry to the service of the Jewish temple, “Don’t you know that those who work in the temple get their food from the temple, and those who serve at the altar share in what is offered on the altar? In the same way, the Lord has commanded that those who preach the gospel should receive their living from the gospel” (1 Corinthians 9:13–14).

Even though Paul voluntarily did not exercise that right while at Corinth, he stressed that preaching is normally a full-time occupation. The preacher should allow nothing to distract him from this.

Even legitimate duties in the church are secondary. When questions arose in the early church over the distribution of food to the widows, the disciples met together and said, “It would not be right for us to neglect the ministry of the word of God in order to wait on tables. Brothers, choose seven men from among you who are known to be full of the Spirit and wisdom. We will turn this responsibility over to them and will give our attention to prayer and the ministry of the word” (Acts 6:2–4).

The call to preach should always take precedence over every other concern. Because of the level of commitment required by those who are to be preachers, a call is essential. Without this special call to the ministry you cannot survive the demands of the work. The sense of urgency within makes you willing to do it.

The Reception of a Call

First, God makes the initial move. Sometimes it is a spectacular burning bush or Damascus Road type of experience. When we speak of a call, we usually have this distinct, unmistakable encounter in mind. However, in the account of his call, Isaiah “heard the voice of the Lord saying, ‘Whom shall I send? And who will go for us?’ and Isaiah said, ‘Here am I. Send me!’ He said, ‘Go and tell this people’” (Isaiah 6:8–9). Although Isaiah volunteered, God made the first move in revealing the need to the young man’s heart.

The next step is a response from the individual that often shows a shrinking from the responsibilities of the ministry, a true fear of the work. “I am too young” (Jeremiah); “I am not eloquent” (Moses); “No one in my family has been a prophet” (Amos); “I am not good enough” (Simon Peter). ## 4 - The Preparation of the Sermon

### The Planning Stage

#### 4.1.1 — Explain the importance of basing a sermon on the Scriptures.

The Text

What is the relationship between the sermon and the text? For some preachers the text is like the airport: one should get through it as quickly as possible and then forget it. As with a casual acquaintance, after an initial introduction they part company and go their separate ways. The relationship that others establish is more that of pen pals: occasionally in touch but never together. Yet others find their text like quicksand: once in it, they find it increasingly difficult to get out. Ideally, the preacher should unite the sermon and text in a perfect marriage; the two become one.

The word text is from the Latin word textus , meaning “something woven.” The fabric of the sermon should be woven from the substance of the text; the text comes first. The practice of preparing a sermon and then seeking a text to connect it with is improper. The text determines the theme, the scope, and the very form of the sermon. Without a text as its basis, the sermon is not truly preaching. It may be a theological lecture, an inspirational speech, or a moral talk, but it is not preaching as we understand it. As we shall see in Unit 4, every sermon should expound either the theme or the actual wording of a Bible portion. The sermon is the faithful exposition and application of Scripture to the needs of the hearers by the power of the Holy Spirit. We need to get back to the practice of preaching directly from the Bible on every occasion.

The choice of a text, therefore, is vitally crucial since it determines the course of the sermon. Some texts choose us without our even looking for them. As we are reading the Bible, a portion suddenly comes alive and seizes our mind, demanding, “You must preach me!” It may be a phrase, a verse, or a whole section; the length will determine whether we prepare a topical, textual, or expository sermon (Unit 4).

At other times as we pray for the needs of our hearers or about the occasion when we are to preach, the Lord will give us a burden for a message and direct us to the particular portion of Scripture that we are to preach on. By being sensitive to the Holy Spirit, you will never need to look desperately for something to preach on . You are God’s herald; therefore, it is God’s responsibility to give you the message. It is your responsibility to spend time with God in prayer and Bible study so that He can direct you.

If you fall into the habit of sermon-hunting , the temptation will be to select texts for their novelty or to distort the real meaning of the text until it says what you want it to. A zealous young preacher tried to prove the authority of the church from Matthew 18:17: “If he refuses to listen even to the church, treat him as you would a pagan or a tax collector.” The preceding verses, however, show that this is in the context of an attempted reconciliation, not a demonstration of church authority. Another renowned preacher delivered a masterly sermon on “Anchors of the Soul” (Acts 27:29), listing them as prayer, faith, hope, and love. The following verses, however, show that the ship was cut free from these anchors so that it could land (Acts 27:40). Even if the connection between the four anchors and these virtues could be maintained, the context demands that we cut loose from them. Far better to stay with the plain meaning of the text than to use clever tricks to make texts say our words. R. W. Dale compared such preachers to those ingenious entertainers who rub a coin between their hands and turn it into a canary or draw from their coat sleeves bowls full of water and goldfish.

INTERPRETATION OF SCRIPTURE

1. CONTEXT

2. MEANING

3. UNITY OF THE BIBLE

In reading the text we must apply the basic principles of Bible interpretation— hermeneutics —to determine the real meaning. The first and most important factor is context . Read around the verse in various translations, if possible, to see its real meaning in its setting. No verse of Scripture can mean something that is impossible in its context.

From this comes the second major factor: meaning . What is the significance of the words? This is called exegesis (bringing out the real meaning), which is the opposite of eisegesis (putting our meaning into it). We also must distinguish between literal and figurative language. For example, Paul’s promise that “not one of you will lose a single hair from his head” (Acts 27:34) is not intended as a remedy for baldness. It is a descriptive phrase for the extensiveness of divine protection. Much that passes for typology or spiritualizing Scripture is equally a distortion of the true meaning. To attribute a different spiritual truth to each of the 153 fish (John 21:11) is more likely to be exhausting than convincing.

The third major factor that should mold our interpretation of any Scripture is the unity of the Bible. The meaning of one text cannot run counter to the teachings of the rest of Scripture. Since “all Scripture is God-breathed” (2 Timothy 3:16), there is a divine unity running throughout the Bible. By comparing parallel passages we can check any temptation to manufacture strange doctrines on the basis of a misunderstood text. God never contradicts Himself; seeming contradictions are based on misunderstandings. Differing phrases in parallel passages often illuminate the full meaning of the passage under consideration and, therefore, are complementary rather than contradictory.

“Do your best to present yourself to God as one approved, a workman who does not need to be ashamed and who correctly handles the word of truth,” Paul exhorted Timothy (2 Timothy 2:15). Make this your goal in sermon preparation from the beginning. The Word should be as much truth when you have finished with it as it was when you started.

The Theme

Once you have your text and a clear understanding of its basic message, the next step is to summarize that message into a crisp, clear, concise statement. This is the theme. The theme may also serve as the title for your sermon or it may suggest a suitable title. Often in analyzing a text you find that several themes emerge, especially in a longer portion of Scripture. It is important, therefore, to use only the one theme that the Lord directs you to. This will be the most suitable for your congregation on this occasion. Once more, it is important to be sensitive to the leading of the Holy Spirit since God already knows who will be present when you preach. Too often preachers have found, to their embarrassment, that the message they had so carefully prepared is inappropriate for the congregation they are facing. The praying does not stop when the text is found. If there is more than one apparent theme in the text, ask God to direct you in selecting one of them. Then prayerfully work your way through all the stages of preparation. Make a note of other themes for future use as the Lord directs.

As it is impossible to set off in all directions at once, so it is impossible to preach on more than one theme at a time. All your efforts, arguments, illustrations, and supporting material must blend together in following that theme. If in preaching you find yourself wandering from the theme, be ruthless in bringing yourself back into line. God has given you direction; you cannot disobey Him if you are speaking for Him.

Preaching is not intended to be a display of your oratorical skills; rather, it is a communication from the Lord of heaven. Your theme, therefore, should represent this source. Trivial themes may miss the whole point of the story. For example, an Easter sermon on The Folded Napkin (John 20:7) focused attention on the need for parents to teach children to be tidy and “fold their napkins” when they were finished with them. The tidiness of the cloth is quite incidental to the glorious fact of Jesus’ resurrection. In J. H. Jowett’s words, “You cannot drop the big themes and create big saints.” Live in the big truths of God’s Word and you will get such a vision of the grand sweep of God’s revelation that the incidentals cannot distract you. The tragedy of the religious leaders of Jesus’ day was that they were so busy squabbling over minute points of interpretation of their traditions that they “neglected the more important matters of the law—justice, mercy and faithfulness” (Matthew 23:23). They even missed their Messiah. Often the occasion or the season will provide the theme for you, such as Christmas, Easter, and Pentecost. Already the people’s minds are prepared for a message on the theme of the day, which is a great advantage.

To do justice to your theme, live in it. It has to become part of you before you can preach it adequately. Read about it; meditate on it; make rough notes of what you see there; make it truly yours. Then, when you come to form an outline of your sermon, you know precisely where you are going. The outline should properly develop both the text and its theme.

The Aim

If the theme answers “What does the text say?” the aim replies to “What do I want this sermon to do?” In Chapter 2, we discovered the general purpose of preaching, but here we must look at the intended purpose of the individual sermon. We come down to the specifics of the sermon we are preparing, and by answering the question of purpose in definite objective terms, we can gear our preparation to that end.

One thing about the youthful Billy Graham that impressed his future wife, Ruth Bell, was that he was a young man who knew where he was going (Cornwell 1983, 62). The worldwide effectiveness of his ministry since bears adequate testimony to the accuracy of that observation of 1940. So does every sermon he preaches. He aims for a definite target and concentrates everything on achieving it.

As soon as your text and theme are settled, the aim is the next step. Ask yourself, “What do I want this sermon to do?” Ask the Lord, “What do You want this sermon to do?” Let the Lord guide you in the answer; it is His Word. Do not settle for less than the goal that God lays on your heart. Believe for it, prepare for it, plan for it, work for it, expect it, and you will achieve it.

This kind of aim must be clear and specific. The expected results should be expressed in tangible terms. Some prayers are phrased so generally that one never knows whether or not they are answered. Some have prayed, “Lord, bless all the missionaries.” And sermons have the same problem; for example, “I want this sermon to bless the people.” C. H. Spurgeon used to concentrate on a different individual with each sermon and gear everything to ministering to that particular one. At the same time, others received the ministry and were converted too. In fact, any congregation is composed of individuals with varying needs. You cannot cater to everyone simultaneously. However, you can minister to one representative person so that everyone with a similar need will feel that you are speaking to him or her alone. The Bible teaches that Jesus died for the world, but for Paul the wonder was that “the Son of God, who loved me and gave himself for me” (Galatians 2:20). It is when the general becomes specific that individuals can make a positive response to the truth.

It is good to remember that unless you know where you are going and how you are going to get there, you cannot expect your hearers to make the journey. “Would you believe,” the preacher asked the deacon, “that when I began to preach, I had no idea what my subject was to be?” “I sure would,” the deacon replied. “We all felt like that when you had finished.” You need a clear and definite aim to ensure that both you and the congregation know where you are going. Exclude anything that distracts from that aim. To achieve your goal, keep to the point.

The Outline

Once you have your text and theme and have established your aim, develop your general outline in proper relationship to these. By asking questions about your text and theme you can clarify your understanding of the subject. Ask questions such as:

What is the particular truth or statement here?

To what situation did it apply then, and how can it apply today?

What caused the original situation, and what were the conditions?

What might be some of its effects if applied now?

Has this solution ever been tried since then?

What might be some of the objections to this solution?

All of these samples would not be appropriate for every text, but you will see that they are intended to highlight supporting and illustrative material and to add substance to your general outline. They may even help provide an outline, which we will cover in more detail later in this course. In any case, now you can begin the process of selecting a text, discovering its theme and aim, and developing a basic outline. But watch out for wandering thoughts; learn to stay on the line God has drawn.

**Q:** List at least three principles regarding the importance of a sermon text.

**A:** Your response should include: (1) As we pray for needs, God will direct us to a message and the appropriate Scripture; (2) the text of a sermon is important because the whole sermon is based on the text; (3) the text should determine a sermon’s theme.

**Q:** Suppose that you have many problems with jealousy among the members of your congregation. While in prayer, the Holy Spirit impresses you to preach on this subject. How would you approach this sermon?

**A:** Your response should be similar to, Begin by studying the Word of God and asking for divine direction in selecting the Scriptures you will use; preaching will only be accepted when it is based on the authority of God’s Word; it is important to check the text for its proper context, its true meaning, and its complement to the unity of the Bible.

**Q:** List the approach that you would use to discover and develop the theme of a Scripture.

**A:** First, you should seek the Lord’s guidance. Then, when you choose the theme, follow it carefully without straying to other ideas. If you find more than one theme, write it down for future reference.

**Q:** Identify the theme of these passages: John 10:1–10; James 1:2–4.

**A:** Your responses should be similar to these: Following Christ (our Shepherd) leads to a complete and full life. God uses testing and trials in our life to produce Christian maturity.

**Q:** What is the importance to the sermon of the theme and aim?

**A:** The sermon theme answers the question, what does the text say? The sermon aim answers the question, what do I want this sermon to do?

#### 4.1.2 — Identify the message of a biblical text.

The Text

What is the relationship between the sermon and the text? For some preachers the text is like the airport: one should get through it as quickly as possible and then forget it. As with a casual acquaintance, after an initial introduction they part company and go their separate ways. The relationship that others establish is more that of pen pals: occasionally in touch but never together. Yet others find their text like quicksand: once in it, they find it increasingly difficult to get out. Ideally, the preacher should unite the sermon and text in a perfect marriage; the two become one.

The word text is from the Latin word textus , meaning “something woven.” The fabric of the sermon should be woven from the substance of the text; the text comes first. The practice of preparing a sermon and then seeking a text to connect it with is improper. The text determines the theme, the scope, and the very form of the sermon. Without a text as its basis, the sermon is not truly preaching. It may be a theological lecture, an inspirational speech, or a moral talk, but it is not preaching as we understand it. As we shall see in Unit 4, every sermon should expound either the theme or the actual wording of a Bible portion. The sermon is the faithful exposition and application of Scripture to the needs of the hearers by the power of the Holy Spirit. We need to get back to the practice of preaching directly from the Bible on every occasion.

The choice of a text, therefore, is vitally crucial since it determines the course of the sermon. Some texts choose us without our even looking for them. As we are reading the Bible, a portion suddenly comes alive and seizes our mind, demanding, “You must preach me!” It may be a phrase, a verse, or a whole section; the length will determine whether we prepare a topical, textual, or expository sermon (Unit 4).

At other times as we pray for the needs of our hearers or about the occasion when we are to preach, the Lord will give us a burden for a message and direct us to the particular portion of Scripture that we are to preach on. By being sensitive to the Holy Spirit, you will never need to look desperately for something to preach on . You are God’s herald; therefore, it is God’s responsibility to give you the message. It is your responsibility to spend time with God in prayer and Bible study so that He can direct you.

If you fall into the habit of sermon-hunting , the temptation will be to select texts for their novelty or to distort the real meaning of the text until it says what you want it to. A zealous young preacher tried to prove the authority of the church from Matthew 18:17: “If he refuses to listen even to the church, treat him as you would a pagan or a tax collector.” The preceding verses, however, show that this is in the context of an attempted reconciliation, not a demonstration of church authority. Another renowned preacher delivered a masterly sermon on “Anchors of the Soul” (Acts 27:29), listing them as prayer, faith, hope, and love. The following verses, however, show that the ship was cut free from these anchors so that it could land (Acts 27:40). Even if the connection between the four anchors and these virtues could be maintained, the context demands that we cut loose from them. Far better to stay with the plain meaning of the text than to use clever tricks to make texts say our words. R. W. Dale compared such preachers to those ingenious entertainers who rub a coin between their hands and turn it into a canary or draw from their coat sleeves bowls full of water and goldfish.

### The Research Stage

#### 4.2.1 — Prepare a weekly work schedule.

The Schedule

Not all sermons come to us ready-made in a flash of inspiration. If we wait for that, we will probably be scanning old notes or sermon books on Saturday night searching for something to preach on Sunday. Remember the maxim, “A sermon carelessly thrown together Saturday night will not catch fire on Sunday.” While there may be times when we will be called upon to preach with a minimum of preparation, we ought to work within a plan for our normal preaching ministry.

Most full-time preachers devote their mornings to study and sermon preparations, their afternoons to congregational or evangelistic visitation, and their evenings to preaching meetings, business meetings, or family relaxation. Even with such scheduling, “sometimes a man will study a whole day to find out that he cannot do a thing that he was counting on” (Beecher 1893, 203).

I found I needed to allow about six hours per sermon during the first ten years of my ministry when I was getting accustomed to the work. Since then, I have been able to reduce the time to about five hours per sermon.

All preachers must carefully guard their rest day if they expect to have a long and fresh ministry. God appointed for humankind a six-day working week with one day for rest. Since Sunday is usually the main preaching day, many ministers use Monday as their rest day to recover from the exertions of the previous day. As Monday is usually a normal working day for everyone else, I prefer to use Saturday as my rest day. Then I am physically refreshed for Sunday. I can also use Monday for planning my week’s schedule for visiting and appointments in the light of anything that has emerged from Sunday’s meetings.

The following chart outlines a possible weekly schedule that includes preaching preparation. Notice that each morning is consistently filled with sermon research and study. In my own ministry I have found that it is helpful to keep at least an hour a day free for unexpected events. Then, whether you need extra time for sermon preparation or other unscheduled events, you will have available time.

Fill out the following work schedule. If you are in pastoral ministry at the present time, fill it out accordingly, making sure to project a realistic amount of time for sermon planning and preparation. If you are not currently in the ministry, fill the chart out as if you were in full-time pastoral ministry. Analyze your own particular situation regarding sermon planning and preparation.

Obviously, you will need to adjust your schedule to fit your own situation. Each schedule will vary depending on the number of sermons you preach each week. No schedule should be so tight that you cannot rearrange it as needs arise. If you schedule to complete each sermon the day before it is needed, you have a day’s margin to allow for unexpected events. In addition to the actual sermon preparation time each day, you need to allow time for studying the Bible and valuable literature that will enrich your personal and preaching life over a longer period of time.

I did all my graduate work at university level while being involved in full-time ministry, which included preaching an average of four times a week and maintaining a vigorous visitation program. My studies deepened my understanding of the truths of God, and this enriched my preaching ministry. A broader study program will help you avoid becoming simply a “sermon machine.”

Self-Discipline

We must learn to get down to work. “Half the bad theology in the world is due to suppressed perspiration,” asserted W. J. Dawson. We can hardly preach to others about conquering the tyranny of moods if we only work at sermon preparation when we feel in the mood. Self-discipline here is essential. If you do not have victory in this important area of your ministry, you cannot expect your hearers to exercise self-control. Being a disciple involves living a disciplined life. A young preacher asked the great Methodist pioneer Adam Clarke the secret of his lifelong practice of rising at 4:00 a.m., “How do you do it? Do you pray about it?” “No!” replied the aged preacher. “I just get out of bed.”

When it comes to preparing sermons, however, you do pray. Pray until you sense the direction of the Holy Spirit, then read the Bible wherever He directs. Make notes as you do. Even if you will not preach on those portions now, you are feeding your soul and laying up in store for your future ministry. “Every teacher of the law who has been instructed about the kingdom of heaven is like the owner of a house who brings out of his storeroom new treasures as well as old” (Matthew 13:52).

There are no shortcuts to sermon preparation. If you begrudge the work of prayer, Bible study, clear thinking, and sensitivity to the Holy Spirit’s direction, and you expect to be able to share a few blessed thoughts that occurred to you on the way to the meeting, you do not qualify as a preacher. If you gladly devote the work needed, you will be His kind of worker.

**Q:** Read 1 Timothy 4:11–16, then answer questions a, b, and c below.

**A:** a) Why is self-discipline especially important for preachers of the gospel? b) What are some examples of self-discipline that you consider important for a preacher? c) How do these areas of self-discipline affect sermon preparation? a) The preacher should always be an example to others both in his public and private life. b) Some areas of self-discipline are engaging in personal prayer and Bible study, taking regular days of rest, maintaining a proper diet and exercise program, memorizing Scripture, and learning appropriate speech and mannerisms. c) If a pastor fails to take time for his own personal and spiritual development, he is not able to offer help and encouragement to others.

**Q:** a) Why is self-discipline especially important for preachers of the gospel?

**Q:** b) What are some examples of self-discipline that you consider important for a preacher?

**Q:** c) How do these areas of self-discipline affect sermon preparation?

In addition to the actual sermon preparation time each day, you need to allow time for studying the Bible and valuable literature that will enrich your personal and preaching life over a longer period of time.

I did all my graduate work at university level while being involved in full-time ministry, which included preaching an average of four times a week and maintaining a vigorous visitation program. My studies deepened my understanding of the truths of God, and this enriched my preaching ministry. A broader study program will help you avoid becoming simply a “sermon machine.”

Self-Discipline

We must learn to get down to work. “Half the bad theology in the world is due to suppressed perspiration,” asserted W. J. Dawson. We can hardly preach to others about conquering the tyranny of moods if we only work at sermon preparation when we feel in the mood. Self-discipline here is essential. If you do not have victory in this important area of your ministry, you cannot expect your hearers to exercise self-control. Being a disciple involves living a disciplined life. A young preacher asked the great Methodist pioneer Adam Clarke the secret of his lifelong practice of rising at 4:00 a.m., “How do you do it? Do you pray about it?” “No!” replied the aged preacher. “I just get out of bed.”

When it comes to preparing sermons, however, you do pray. Pray until you sense the direction of the Holy Spirit, then read the Bible wherever He directs. Make notes as you do. Even if you will not preach on those portions now, you are feeding your soul and laying up in store for your future ministry. “Every teacher of the law who has been instructed about the kingdom of heaven is like the owner of a house who brings out of his storeroom new treasures as well as old” (Matthew 13:52).

There are no shortcuts to sermon preparation. If you begrudge the work of prayer, Bible study, clear thinking, and sensitivity to the Holy Spirit’s direction, and you expect to be able to share a few blessed thoughts that occurred to you on the way to the meeting, you do not qualify as a preacher. If you gladly devote the work needed, you will be His kind of worker.

**Q:** Read 1 Timothy 4:11–16, then answer questions a, b, and c below.

**A:** a) Why is self-discipline especially important for preachers of the gospel? b) What are some examples of self-discipline that you consider important for a preacher? c) How do these areas of self-discipline affect sermon preparation? a) The preacher should always be an example to others both in his public and private life. b) Some areas of self-discipline are engaging in personal prayer and Bible study, taking regular days of rest, maintaining a proper diet and exercise program, memorizing Scripture, and learning appropriate speech and mannerisms. c) If a pastor fails to take time for his own personal and spiritual development, he is not able to offer help and encouragement to others.

**Q:** a) Why is self-discipline especially important for preachers of the gospel?

**Q:** b) What are some examples of self-discipline that you consider important for a preacher?

**Q:** c) How do these areas of self-discipline affect sermon preparation?

### The Reflection Stage

#### 4.3.1 — Explain the role of a sense of satisfaction in preaching.

Job Satisfaction

“We are God’s fellow workers,” declared Paul regarding his ministry and that of Apollos (1 Corinthians 3:9). This consciousness of working with God and not just working for God transforms the most wearying tasks into a delight. There are times when the strain of preparing sermon after sermon begins to show in the preacher’s stamina, especially if he or she has not carefully guarded a rest day and has meetings every night. Some pastors never learn to delegate other tasks to those who do not share the pastoral ministry and to concentrate their attention on their primary responsibility, which is the ministry of the Word. Consequently, their preaching ministry begins to suffer. They feel dissatisfaction with their sermons and may either downgrade the value of preaching or even leave the ministry completely.

The Bible records that “the disciples went out and preached everywhere, and the Lord worked with them and confirmed his word by the signs that accompanied it” (Mark 16:20). This awareness that the preacher is not alone in the work, but that the Lord is at work also, puts a different appearance on things. All churches are not large and flourishing. Those that are can cast a shadow on the less fruitful works of other preachers and give the impression that numbers and finances measure success in God’s eyes. What counts with God, however, is faithfulness to His will. In Jesus’ parable of the talents the man who had doubled his five talents received no greater commendation than the man who doubled his two talents. Their rewards were identical because they were faithful. In contrast, the man who had neglected his responsibility was condemned. Paul states this principle clearly in his first letter to the Corinthians, “It is required that those who have been given a trust must prove faithful” (4:2).

Whenever you are faithfully pursuing the will of God, God is working with you. Discouragement is impossible when you are conscious of the presence of the Lord Himself. The hardest task seems easier when He is at work within you. There is a deep inner satisfaction that comes from the awareness of His presence.

Inspiration

Behind most failures in the preaching ministry is a neglected devotional life. Behind most sermon failures is also a neglected devotional life. In fact, there is only one way to succeed in the ministry and that is to spend time in intimate communion with God. One of the greatest temptations for the preacher is to use devotional Bible reading time for hunting sermons. Instead of feeding one’s soul, he or she prowls like a lion looking for something to preach on. Some preachers keep a notebook or handheld device handy during their devotional time in order to record anything that especially touches them. Others, like J. H. Jowett, strictly separate their devotions from their serious Bible study time and only use the latter for obtaining sermon material. For most of us, as much as we do not intentionally look for preaching material at this time, it finds us, and if we do not write it down, it will be gone. We do not go to the Bible for a sermon, but we often come from the Bible with one.

Nevertheless, our time of private prayer, Bible reading, and waiting on God must be carefully maintained if our well of inspiration is not to run dry. We must learn just to be in His presence. The reason God could reveal His ways to Moses was that He could speak to this godly man face-to-face as with a friend (Psalm 103:7; Deuteronomy 34:10). The hasty rush in which we so often fellowship with God hardly gives any opportunity for that kind of deep, intimate fellowship. The outstanding characteristic of the greatest and most effective preachers in past ages seems to have been that they were people of prayer. They knew how to spend time with God.

By deliberately preserving these times of daily devotion, you will be better able to receive His truths. Your spirit will be quiet in His presence, and He can speak to you. All the distracting pressures of daily life are left to one side. It is a time of personal, spiritual renewal. Most preachers find it best to begin the day with such a time before they get caught up in the day’s activities. To come from a time of communion with God refreshed with His power can hardly fail to make the day’s work seem easier. Like King David, we feel the rising up of an overwhelming desire to speak out in His name. “My heart grew hot within me, and as I meditated, the fire burned; then I spoke with my tongue” (Psalm 39:3).

This is really where the sermon preparation has its source: in the presence of God. Spend time there and you will find neither inspiration nor congregation deserting you.

**Q:** How does the parable in Matthew 25:14–30 relate to a preacher of a local church, and how will application of the principle affect his or her attitude?

**A:** Just as different amounts of talents were given, churches and ministries are different sizes. However, faithfulness to the calling is the key. If a pastor does the best with what he has been given, whether a large or small responsibility, God will reward him. The knowledge of this principle will cause a great deal of satisfaction in the preaching ministry.

**Q:** What can we learn from Mark 16:20 and 1 Corinthians 3:5–9 regarding having a sense of satisfaction in preaching?

**A:** The disciples went everywhere. We, too, can expect that God’s Spirit will be with us in every assignment, whether a large or small group of people. We will not always see immediate results from our preaching. It may be our responsibility to plant or water. But satisfaction comes from doing God’s will.

**Q:** State the source of sermon inspiration.

**A:** Communion with God is the source of sermon inspiration.

**Q:** Explain how this time of inspiration is different from time spent in sermon preparation. What is a common temptation that a preacher must guard against while maintaining his or her own personal devotions?

**A:** This time of inspiration is a time set aside for the pastor to spend with God. It is not part of the workday. It is important for a preacher to feed his or her own soul before ministering to others. It is often tempting to use devotional time for “sermon hunting.”

**Q:** When a thought or idea does come during personal devotions, how should a preacher react?

**A:** He or she should write down the reference or idea and review it in detail at another time. 5 CHAPTER
