## 5 - The Material for the Sermons

### The Challenge

#### 5.1.1 — Describe how to face the challenge of securing sermon material.

The preacher’s continuing challenge is how to secure enough fresh, relevant material for about four sermons a week for over forty years. The prospect of preaching approximately eight thousand sermons is enough to make a young preacher declare, “I can never do it without repeating myself.” One elderly pioneer preacher rejected prepared sermons, stating that he had only prepared two sermons throughout his long ministry, one on eternity and the other on personal evangelism. Agreeing, one of his deacons commented, “And those are the only two sermons he has been preaching for the past fifty years!” Another preacher boasted that he could take any text in the Bible and preach on Pentecost from it, and usually he did.

While a Paganini (a famous Italian violinist) may be able to produce a virtuoso performance with only one string on his violin, the aspiring preacher should aim for the full-orbed presentation of the whole Christian teaching. Far too many fail to dig deep enough into the Word of God for their material. They quickly exhaust their stock of ready-made sermons and then look for a new pastorate where they can safely repeat the process. If you face the challenge of relevance and supply early in your ministry, you can stay where God wants you to be for the rest of your ministry without running dry or turning stale.

Source Material

Where does sermon inspiration and subject matter come from? From the news reports? From the preacher’s area of expertise or personal interest? From the internet? The answer is bound up with our understanding of the nature of the sermon itself. If the sermon is a speech, then a Christian comment on topics of current interest will suffice. If, however, the sermon is as Bernard Lord Manning says, “a manifestation of the incarnate Word from the written Word through the spoken word” (Baird 1984, 868), if it is a message from God to man, then it must go further than my opinion of current events or even well-meaning advice on the situations of life, as important and beneficial as such may be. It is more than that.

At a time of unprecedented national peril with the conquering enemy at the gates of the capital city, Zedekiah, the king of Judah, sent for God’s spokesman Jeremiah, asking, “Is there any word from the Lord?” (Jeremiah 37:17). Advice and opinions were readily available elsewhere, but the need was for a message of greater perception and insight than human intellect could ever provide. That need is met in the Word of God.

The Bible is more than simply a collection of texts upon which to base our thoughts; it provides the very substance of the sermon. The preacher’s authority lies in this: he or she proclaims the Word of God. Preachers’ advice and opinions are as fallible as any other person’s, but when they faithfully declare the Word of the Lord, they are not speaking as any other person; God speaks through them.

Relevance

The other side of the problem, however, is expressed by the person who, having been slowly led through the wilderness wanderings of the Israelites in Exodus, protested, “That is all very well for them to have arrived in the promised land three thousand years ago, but what about me today? I have problems, too, you know!” Facing us when we preach are people with problems that have no easy answer. We may proclaim the general truths of Scripture, carefully and faithfully expounding the true meaning of every passage we quote. The question is how does this help the family who is facing tragedy in the home, the married couple considering divorce, the unmarried person burning with passion, the businessman tempted to cheat, or the employee expected to lie for his superior? Although preachers may readily see how the timeless principles of the Bible apply to the temporal affairs of everyday life, most hearers do not have the advantages of the preacher’s training. They need to be told in specific terms. “The Word” has to become “flesh” if it is to “live among us” (John 1:14).

The task of the preacher is the application of the Word of God to the lives of the congregation. This involves not only receiving the Word and understanding its meaning in its context, but also in understanding its message to us today. We have to make the life of Abraham in Ur of the Chaldees meaningful to each individual in each part of the world. Although the superficial details of our modern dramas may differ and the faces of the players may change, the basic issues are the same today as in biblical times. God’s Word is still relevant, and the preacher, familiar with its message, is never at a loss for sermon material.

**Q:** Describe the challenge a preacher faces in securing sermon material. Why is it important to recognize this challenge early in one’s ministry?

**A:** The preacher’s challenge is to prepare a number of sermons each week and to find new and interesting sermon ideas for each one. The preacher should not rely on a sermon supply from past ministry as this will limit the scope and depth of current preaching ministry.

**Q:** Suppose that you are preparing a sermon for a very specific need in your congregation. How will you face this challenge? How does Jeremiah 37:17 apply to your situation?

**A:** First, you should seek God’s direction for a useful biblical application for the current problem. Just as in the biblical example in Jeremiah, there is need for a word from God rather than human’s wisdom.

### Faulty Solutions

#### 5.2.1 — Recognize examples of inadequate solutions of sermon preparation.

Recognizing the importance of preaching from the actual words of the Bible, we must face the question of choosing a text. The reason for using a specific text lies in the need for a specific point of authority, a focus for our exposition and application. A text gives the message a direction, and it helps both the preacher and the hearer to concentrate on the central core of the Word.

But how do we choose our texts? Two methods have been tried and found inadequate. It is important for you to be aware of these distractions along the road to solid sermon preparation. Study these areas carefully so that later, as you prepare your own sermons, you will not fall into these bad habits.

The Haphazard Approach

One of my college friends, formerly a confirmed gambler, discovered to his delight that there are sixty-six books to the Bible and the same number on a pair of dice. With three throws he could choose a book, a chapter, and a verse. Others have used random openings of the Bible or selections from packs of text cards to find something to preach on. I pray that you will never allow yourself to rely on chance rather than God to determine the basis of your sermon.

A neighboring preacher, grandly confident of his ability in the pulpit, regularly asked his congregation to select a text on which he proceeded to preach with scarcely a moment’s thought. Only the frequent requests from the first chapter of Numbers, 1 Chronicles 1–10, and Matthew 1:1–16 finally brought his method to a halt. That God can, and often does, bless unprepared sermons is very obvious; nevertheless, too often it is laziness rather than faith in immediate divine inspiration that determines the choice of this approach to preaching.

The crucial text usually levied against prepared sermons is Luke 21:14–15: “Settle it therefore in your hearts, not to meditate before what you shall answer. For I will give you a mouth and wisdom, which all your adversaries shall not be able to gainsay nor resist” (NKJV). The context, however, shows clearly that it is at a time of persecution and trial that such immediate inspiration is promised. Since most of our preaching will not be to such antagonistic congregations, this passage will not normally apply to us.

The idea that God cannot bless prepared sermons obviously limits His power. He can inspire the mind and the pen just as easily as He could make Balaam’s donkey speak words that it could not understand (Numbers 22:28–30). Our people deserve more than “a few thoughts that occurred to me when I opened my Bible.” They need to know that this is the word God gave me when I was praying, studying, and preparing myself for this ministry.

Plagiarism

Plagiarism is the practice of pretending that the work of others is our own. Sometimes it is simply borrowing another preacher’s sermon outline; at other times it is using the complete sermon. Early church father, St. John Chrysostom, wrote, “For if it has occurred to any preacher to weave into his sermons any part of other men’s works, he is exposed to greater disgrace than those who steal money. Nay, often where he has not even borrowed anything from any one, but is only suspected, he has suffered the fate of a thief.” Long before even Chrysostom’s writing, God declared, “I am against the prophets who steal from one another words supposedly from me” (Jeremiah 23:30). To take and use another person’s work as though it were our own deserves the condemnation it receives.

But what about the published homiletical aids such as 1000 Sermon Outlines, Sermon Starters for Busy Pastors, and The Complete Book of Sermon Illustrations , or the many sources of sermons available online? As the author of this IST, I am aware that my work will be used by others. However, when using material publicly, should we not admit to our hearers that the material is from a certain publication or website rather than deceive them? Why do some preachers feel the need to pass the work of others off as their own? Is it so their hearers will form a better opinion of their abilities? Remember, God has not called us to a ministry based upon deception and self-promotion.

Yet few of us can claim to be original thinkers, and even what we think is our own may well be some half-forgotten memory. As Solomon wisely remarked, “There is nothing new under the sun. Is there anything of which one can say ‘Look! This is something new’? It was here already, long ago” (Ecclesiastes 1:9–10). We are all indebted to our teachers and should readily acknowledge that fact. Whatever we knowingly borrow, we should admit to, giving the details necessary to establish its origin and our honesty. For example, you could say, “At our last district camp, God spoke to me through Brother Smith’s sermon on this text,” or “I heard W. E. Sangster once illustrate this point in this way.”

If we want to receive our own reward from the Lord, we must do our own studying, our own preparing, and our own sermonizing. The intellectual and spiritual discipline of digging out our own material will strengthen our preaching ministry.

**Q:** Think of some examples of haphazard sermon preparation and plagiarism that you have observed. Then explain how you could correctly use another person’s ideas or thoughts in a sermon of your own.

**A:** Often a quotation or viewpoint from another individual will add emphasis and interest to your sermon. However, when using material that is not original, give proper credit to the author or speaker.

### An Ideal Solution

#### 5.3.1 — Create a personalized system for organizing Bible study notes.

Personal Bible Study

We should base our preaching on our own personal study of the Scriptures. This type of study is necessary to make us so familiar with the Scriptures that we will be able to draw on an ever-enlarging fund of knowledge for both primary and supporting sermon material.

In Chapter 4, we emphasized the importance of a consistent devotional life distinct from the Bible study time. At this time of intimate communion, we open our lives up to the scrutiny of God and listen earnestly to what He reveals to us from the Scriptures. Very often it is because God has spoken to us through a portion of the Word that we are able to take that Word and minister it to our people. G. Campbell Morgan tells of being in the office of the great Joseph Parker when a man came in to thank Parker for a great sermon he had preached that morning. “It did me good,” he said. “Sir,” replied Parker, “I preached it because it had done me good” (Campbell 1974, 50). The message had been filtered through the preacher’s own life; it was part of him. Powerful preaching is the outflow of the heart God has touched.

We will gain maximum benefit from our devotional reading and certainly in our Bible study times if we will read longer sections of Scripture rather than the brief suggestions offered in some Bible reading courses. In the study time it is quite easy to read a whole book of the Bible at one sitting. Mark’s Gospel, for instance, takes about forty-five minutes at a normal pace; the New Testament letters vary from two minutes for 2 or 3 John to thirty minutes for Romans or 1 Corinthians. By adopting this method, we gain a new perspective on the flow of Scriptural events and actually read the narrative as the writers intended.

We must also learn to apply ourselves to the art of intensive Bible study. An example of this is systematically going through books of the Bible in the original languages, if possible, or else with good modern translations and sound exegetical commentaries, making notes as we go. Long ago I learned the rule of never looking into a commentary until I had first put in the basic work on the text itself. Let the Word speak directly to your heart before you ask what it said to others.

It is helpful to compile some sort of index to the notebooks you write so that you can trace the leading ideas. In this way we lay the foundation for textual, expository, and topical sermons. It is also wise to sort through the notebooks periodically and transfer the material to a loose-leaf binder, using one sheet or more per subject. By using the loose-leaf method, you can add more sheets at the appropriate place as you continue to study.

If you make your notes on a handheld device, you can transfer them to your computer, and file them by making folders for each topic using the note page below as a template for storing the information systematically. You may also want to add a cross reference link to related subject folders. Do not forget however to back up your material or your careful research may be lost.

This means that you can always have sermons that are in the course of preparation for your regular preaching ministry. Sometimes God gives you the full sermon straight off; at other times He puts His finger on a sermon that has been preparing for weeks and says, in effect, “This is the Word for Sunday night.”

Below is a sample of a notebook page for Bible study notes. In your own notebook, create a system that will work for you. Whether you are currently in ministry or not, begin now to organize your Bible study time.

Subject :

Date :

Text:

Original language comments :

Commentary Notes :

Congregational Needs

Effective sermons are related to life; they are not conceived in a vacuum untouched by the pressing needs of the congregation. By visiting the flock, counseling troubled individuals, and observing the concerns of the day, the attentive preacher will find him or herself directed to speak on some themes more than others. So often God shows us a need and then moves us to minister to it. When Jesus encouraged His disciples to share His compassion for the “sheep without a shepherd,” He said, “The harvest is plentiful but the workers are few. Ask the Lord of the harvest, therefore, to send out workers into his harvest field.” Then, “he called his twelve disciples to him and gave them authority to drive out evil spirits and to cure every kind of disease and sickness” and He sent them out to minister to those “lost sheep” (Matthew 9:35–10:8). Unless we see the need, feel the pain, and are moved with compassion we are unlikely to minister effectively.

The preacher, then, should study the congregation. Their needs—whether physical, mental, moral, or spiritual—should be prayerfully considered when preparing a sermon. He or she should carefully tabulate the particular problems coming to light through day-to-day contact with the people and evaluate his or her preaching from that aspect.

Obviously one sermon cannot deal with every problem, but every sermon should minister to some current problem if it is to be relevant to the hearers’ needs. The preacher’s joy is in hearing someone say, “Pastor, your sermon has helped me. Thank you for it.”

The Theme of the Occasion

As preachers, our goal should be to preach the whole range of Christian truths in a systematic , regular fashion. If we approach preaching in a casual way, we will quickly discover that our ministry tends to regularly turn into familiar, comfortable channels. A survey of last year’s preaching will demonstrate how limited a diet we are serving our hearers. Balance is as important to preachers as it is to dietitians.

The easiest and most efficient way to achieve a balanced presentation of truth is to plan our preaching in advance. This does not eliminate the inspiration of the Holy Spirit nor ignore His direction in what to preach. Since God knows everything, He already knows the best sermon for our congregation next week, next month, and next year. There is, therefore, no real reason why we should not already be praying for His direction as to what we will preach on in the future. Throughout fifteen years of busy pastoral life I made it a habit to use December each year for praying over the next year’s preaching plan. No plan was so rigid that it could not be changed nearer the date, but if I had truly prayed and followed the Spirit’s leading, that was seldom necessary.

This concept of advance planning is by no means new to the church. Within the first five hundred years of its existence, the church developed an ecclesiastical calendar in which the major events of the faith were clearly marked and sermons appropriate to the theme of the day were preached. This church year began with the four Sundays of Advent leading up to Christmas, covering themes such as Creation, the Bible, prophecy, and the preparation for the second coming of Christ, climaxing on Christmas Day with the birth of Christ and the doctrine of the Incarnation. Although the first half of the church year was closely organized around the central events of the life of Christ and His Church, only Easter, with its emphasis on the death and resurrection of Christ, Pentecost ( commemorating the outpouring of the Holy Spirit), and Trinity Sunday (the following week) are widely observed today. The second half of the year was deliberately kept free for themes emphasizing Christian life and witness.

In this way, the developing church ensured that the major doctrines of the Bible were covered each year, and we can do the same. We cannot afford to assume that our people know and remember the biblical basis for the main teachings of the faith. The regular, systematic exposition of these truths will both enrich their spiritual lives and strengthen them against false doctrine. Also, the intellectual and spiritual discipline of making these truths clear and of showing their relevance to modern man will be of great value to our own spiritual lives.

Where people already have some knowledge of Christianity, the seasonal sermon will be especially suitable since their minds will already be thinking along the same lines. Of course, we must always be careful not to lose the essential freedom to preach as the Holy Spirit leads.

Other special occasions may include national and community commemorative days; local church events, such as church anniversaries, dedication of buildings, consecration of workers, baptismal and communion services; and occasional services such as infant dedications, weddings, and funerals. Whatever the occasion, Paul advised, “Rejoice with those who rejoice; mourn with those who mourn. Live in harmony with one another” (Romans 12:15–16). Preach sermons that are appropriate to the needs of the hearers on each occasion. Remember, however, when preaching at times of national or community significance, your people came to hear the Word of God, not your opinion of the government and its policies. Politics and preaching do not mix.

Sermon Series

In our quest for a balanced presentation of the whole range of Christian doctrine, we quickly realize the limitations of the single sermon on a topic. If we tried to deal exhaustively with the theme, the sermon would be excessive in length. It would more likely give people spiritual indigestion than inspiration, and to simply return to the theme periodically would not take us much further either. Since so much of the sermon time would be spent in covering introductory matters, our treatment would tend to be superficial. In a sermon series, however, we are able to follow a theme or Scripture portion through in a systematic, regular fashion at a deeper level than the occasional sermon would permit.

The sermon series needs to be clearly distinguished from the serial . In the serial, the whole is sectioned off into parts, each of which continues and is dependent on what has preceded. In the series, however, each section is complete in itself and may be understood independently. While the preceding and succeeding sermons may add to the total picture, they are not essential to understand the individual sermon. Where church attendance is spasmodic , the series, rather than the serial, will be better. Whether in the regular pastoral preaching plan or in the special evangelistic meetings, I have found that an announcement of a theme for the forthcoming five or six meetings helps stimulate enthusiasm in the congregation. This is especially true if the topic is of current interest. Usually in pastoral ministry, it works best to follow the series each Sunday morning or evening rather than use both morning and evening. Some people will be able to attend mornings regularly, and others will only attend evenings. For a week’s special meetings, however, you should encourage attendance at every service so the series may fill the whole period.

Any theme or topic is appropriate for a series of sermons provided there is sufficient material. We need to note Jesus’ parable of the man building the tower (Luke 14:28–30). Before we announce a series, we need to be sure that we can deal adequately with the theme and have enough material to finish. It is wise to prepare an outline of the whole series before announcing it.

The wide variety of source material for sermon series should eliminate any sense of boredom in the congregation. After a thematic or doctrinal series, such as the second coming of Christ, the exposition of a particular book of the Bible could be undertaken, followed by a series on prominent Bible characters or perhaps the parables of Jesus. The preacher can retain enthusiasm by varying the type of series and interspersing each series with occasional sermons. Planning the series to lead up to or out of a special church event, such as the doctrine of the Holy Spirit around the Day of Pentecost, is also effective.

Length is always of major concern, since few of us could rival Harold Ockenga of Park Street, Boston, who preached his way through the entire New Testament at his Sunday morning and Friday evening meetings for twenty-one years (Ockenga 1959, 115); or the noted Bible commentator Matthew Henry, who held a Thursday “course of sermons” over Scripture questions that “occupied no less than twenty years” (Williams 1974, vol. 2, 126); or his father Philip’s sermon series, “once he spent about two months in preaching over the doctrine of the Lord’s Supper...and one he spent about half a year in preaching over the epistle to Laodicea, Revelations [sic], iii” (Williams 1974, vol. 1, 354). For most of us a month to six weeks is adequate early in our ministry. This also helps us fit the series in between special meetings that might interrupt the sequence.

Never plan a series of sermons without spending much time in prayer and sensing the leading of the Holy Spirit. The series is not simply an easy way out of the pressure of weekly preparation. Since there are many different ways to handle a Bible portion, the approach, as well as the subject matter, needs divine direction.

The following is an example of one month in a year’s planning calendar. In a calendar of your own, plan a balanced year of preaching for a congregation. Be sure to include the special events and needs of your own church and culture.

Bible Readings

The Bible reading or Bible study, as it is often called, is the systematic verse-by-verse exposition of a section of the Bible. It differs from the expository sermon in that while the sermon selects one main theme from a portion and applies it to the hearers’ consciences, the reading draws out all the main themes and shows their relationship to each other. The reading emphasizes doctrine, whereas the sermon stresses action. In the sermon we preach for verdicts; in the reading we teach for building up the church. The difference is in emphasis and style rather than in content, since all sermons have a doctrinal basis and involve elements of teaching. (See Chapter 2, Objective 2.3.1.) Paul delighted to describe himself as a preacher and a teacher.

Despite almost twenty centuries of Bible study there is widespread ignorance of the actual content of Scripture. The too-frequent loss of sincere Christian believers to false teaching should make us aware of the need for clear instruction in the truths of God’s Word. Systematic Bible exposition drives both preacher and people to the foundation of their faith in the Word of God.

The goal of the Bible reading is edification, building up God’s people. We must build soundly by personally exploring the scriptural foundations of our faith; we must build surely by carefully reasoning out our teachings on the basis of the Bible; and we must build simply by making abstract doctrines concrete. The key is this: “What does the Bible say about this, and how does it apply to my life?”

In the preparation of the Bible reading, it is essential to be fully familiar with the portion of Scripture to be expounded. Preparation involves the extensive use of study aids such as commentaries, Bible dictionaries, and encyclopedias to provide solid background material. Then, a clear outline is necessary if the reading is not to be a confusing ramble through the Bible countryside, plucking occasional flowers of thought blooming by the wayside.

The use of visual aids such as charts, diagrams, and printed handouts can help the congregation understand the major points of the study. As with any illustrative material, verbal or visual, it is better to have a few accurate and precise illustrations than many that are mediocre or obscure (Chapter 7).

Some teachers successfully involve the congregation in the study by asking and answering questions or by encouraging a general discussion of the topic following the introduction. The more mature the believers are, the better this works. Even so, the teacher has to provide firm and wise leadership to prevent dogmatic individuals from manipulating or dividing the congregation over some controversial issue.

With such a wealth of material from which to draw the substance of sermons, there is no reason for preachers ever to find themselves without fresh, relevant Bible content. As a young pastor, I was astounded when one of my church deacons said, “Pastor, I know where you got your sermon from this morning. I read the book myself last night.” I quickly began to defend the hours spent preparing the sermon when he continued, “The Bible! It was all from the Bible!”

**Q:** Why is it important to study the Bible in longer passages such as a whole book or letter?

**A:** Studying a whole section, book, or letter will give you the original intent of the author. It will also often give a new perspective of the material you are studying.

**Q:** Your author suggests that you study the original text or a modern translation of a text before reading a commentary. What effect will this have on your sermon?

**A:** This allows the Holy Spirit to inspire thoughts from the Word of God that are unique and specific. Your sermons will have a personalized meaning and will deeply minister to those who hear.

**Q:** What is the best way to organize your Bible study notes, and how is this beneficial in preparing sermons?

**A:** Create a system that will work best for you. Use either notebooks or a filing system. It is important to categorize by subject so you will be able to locate material as God directs you in sermon planning.

**Q:** How does Matthew 9:35–38 challenge the preacher? What kind of food (sermon material) should the shepherd provide?

**A:** The preacher (shepherd) needs to understand the people and preach sermons that will help them with their needs and problems.

**Q:** List some practical ways in which you might, as a pastor, learn the needs of your congregation.

**A:** Your own response. Every response will be different, but hopefully you will consider times of relaxing together over a meal or a cup coffee, as well as playing a game of golf or tennis. More intense times of sharing may take place in a counseling session or other private settings.

**Q:** Describe the link between congregational needs and sermon materials. How will ministering to the needs of the congregation expand sermon material possibilities?

**A:** When the pastor is responding to the needs of the congregation, there will not be a need to search for material. It will be a natural response, just as the shepherd feeds and comforts the lost sheep. The needs will dictate the sermons.

**Q:** What was the purpose for developing the ecclesiastical calendar?

**A:** The purpose of the church calendar was to ensure the teaching of the major biblical doctrines each year, as well as the scheduling of major church themes

**Q:** How can advance planning be beneficial to every church?

**A:** Every church should follow a similar plan to ensure balanced teaching, as well as organization.

**Q:** How can you use special occasions such as national or community celebrations as a time of evangelism?

**A:** Special occasions are times when many non-churched people will attend services. It is important to plan in advance to present the gospel at these times.

**Q:** How must you be careful when focusing on a special event?

**A:** The danger of special occasions is that the focus may be too heavily on the theme of the day rather than on Jesus Christ, who should be the central theme of every sermon.

**Q:** Explain the difference between the serial sermon and the sermon series .

**A:** The sermon serial is continued from one sermon to the next. In a sermon series each sermon is complete in itself. These sermons are connected by a common theme, book, or topic.

**Q:** Why would a preaching calendar be especially necessary when planning a series of biblical messages?

**A:** By planning a special series in advance, you can keep from interrupting your series with other events such as special occasions or guest speakers.

**Q:** Briefly describe the difference between the Bible reading and the expository sermon. Explain the value of systematic Bible readings or Bible studies.

**A:** The Bible reading emphasizes doctrine; the expository sermon focuses on action. Bible readings or Bible studies will ground believers in their faith. It is a follow-up to their conversion experience. 6 CHAPTER

#### 5.3.2 — Explain the link between congregational needs and sermon materials.

Personal Bible Study

We should base our preaching on our own personal study of the Scriptures. This type of study is necessary to make us so familiar with the Scriptures that we will be able to draw on an ever-enlarging fund of knowledge for both primary and supporting sermon material.

In Chapter 4, we emphasized the importance of a consistent devotional life distinct from the Bible study time. At this time of intimate communion, we open our lives up to the scrutiny of God and listen earnestly to what He reveals to us from the Scriptures. Very often it is because God has spoken to us through a portion of the Word that we are able to take that Word and minister it to our people. G. Campbell Morgan tells of being in the office of the great Joseph Parker when a man came in to thank Parker for a great sermon he had preached that morning. “It did me good,” he said. “Sir,” replied Parker, “I preached it because it had done me good” (Campbell 1974, 50). The message had been filtered through the preacher’s own life; it was part of him. Powerful preaching is the outflow of the heart God has touched.

We will gain maximum benefit from our devotional reading and certainly in our Bible study times if we will read longer sections of Scripture rather than the brief suggestions offered in some Bible reading courses. In the study time it is quite easy to read a whole book of the Bible at one sitting. Mark’s Gospel, for instance, takes about forty-five minutes at a normal pace; the New Testament letters vary from two minutes for 2 or 3 John to thirty minutes for Romans or 1 Corinthians. By adopting this method, we gain a new perspective on the flow of Scriptural events and actually read the narrative as the writers intended.

We must also learn to apply ourselves to the art of intensive Bible study. An example of this is systematically going through books of the Bible in the original languages, if possible, or else with good modern translations and sound exegetical commentaries, making notes as we go. Long ago I learned the rule of never looking into a commentary until I had first put in the basic work on the text itself. Let the Word speak directly to your heart before you ask what it said to others.

It is helpful to compile some sort of index to the notebooks you write so that you can trace the leading ideas. In this way we lay the foundation for textual, expository, and topical sermons. It is also wise to sort through the notebooks periodically and transfer the material to a loose-leaf binder, using one sheet or more per subject. By using the loose-leaf method, you can add more sheets at the appropriate place as you continue to study.

If you make your notes on a handheld device, you can transfer them to your computer, and file them by making folders for each topic using the note page below as a template for storing the information systematically. You may also want to add a cross reference link to related subject folders. Do not forget however to back up your material or your careful research may be lost.

This means that you can always have sermons that are in the course of preparation for your regular preaching ministry. Sometimes God gives you the full sermon straight off; at other times He puts His finger on a sermon that has been preparing for weeks and says, in effect, “This is the Word for Sunday night.”

Below is a sample of a notebook page for Bible study notes. In your own notebook, create a system that will work for you. Whether you are currently in ministry or not, begin now to organize your Bible study time.

Date :

Text:

Original language comments :

Commentary Notes :

Congregational Needs

Effective sermons are related to life; they are not conceived in a vacuum untouched by the pressing needs of the congregation. By visiting the flock, counseling troubled individuals, and observing the concerns of the day, the attentive preacher will find him or herself directed to speak on some themes more than others. So often God shows us a need and then moves us to minister to it. When Jesus encouraged His disciples to share His compassion for the “sheep without a shepherd,” He said, “The harvest is plentiful but the workers are few. Ask the Lord of the harvest, therefore, to send out workers into his harvest field.” Then, “he called his twelve disciples to him and gave them authority to drive out evil spirits and to cure every kind of disease and sickness” and He sent them out to minister to those “lost sheep” (Matthew 9:35–10:8). Unless we see the need, feel the pain, and are moved with compassion we are unlikely to minister effectively.

The preacher, then, should study the congregation. Their needs—whether physical, mental, moral, or spiritual—should be prayerfully considered when preparing a sermon. He or she should carefully tabulate the particular problems coming to light through day-to-day contact with the people and evaluate his or her preaching from that aspect.

Obviously one sermon cannot deal with every problem, but every sermon should minister to some current problem if it is to be relevant to the hearers’ needs. The preacher’s joy is in hearing someone say, “Pastor, your sermon has helped me. Thank you for it.”

The Theme of the Occasion

As preachers, our goal should be to preach the whole range of Christian truths in a systematic , regular fashion. If we approach preaching in a casual way, we will quickly discover that our ministry tends to regularly turn into familiar, comfortable channels. A survey of last year’s preaching will demonstrate how limited a diet we are serving our hearers. Balance is as important to preachers as it is to dietitians.

The easiest and most efficient way to achieve a balanced presentation of truth is to plan our preaching in advance. This does not eliminate the inspiration of the Holy Spirit nor ignore His direction in what to preach. Since God knows everything, He already knows the best sermon for our congregation next week, next month, and next year. There is, therefore, no real reason why we should not already be praying for His direction as to what we will preach on in the future. Throughout fifteen years of busy pastoral life I made it a habit to use December each year for praying over the next year’s preaching plan. No plan was so rigid that it could not be changed nearer the date, but if I had truly prayed and followed the Spirit’s leading, that was seldom necessary.

This concept of advance planning is by no means new to the church. Within the first five hundred years of its existence, the church developed an ecclesiastical calendar in which the major events of the faith were clearly marked and sermons appropriate to the theme of the day were preached. This church year began with the four Sundays of Advent leading up to Christmas, covering themes such as Creation, the Bible, prophecy, and the preparation for the second coming of Christ, climaxing on Christmas Day with the birth of Christ and the doctrine of the Incarnation. Although the first half of the church year was closely organized around the central events of the life of Christ and His Church, only Easter, with its emphasis on the death and resurrection of Christ, Pentecost ( commemorating the outpouring of the Holy Spirit), and Trinity Sunday (the following week) are widely observed today. The second half of the year was deliberately kept free for themes emphasizing Christian life and witness.

In this way, the developing church ensured that the major doctrines of the Bible were covered each year, and we can do the same. We cannot afford to assume that our people know and remember the biblical basis for the main teachings of the faith. The regular, systematic exposition of these truths will both enrich their spiritual lives and strengthen them against false doctrine. Also, the intellectual and spiritual discipline of making these truths clear and of showing their relevance to modern man will be of great value to our own spiritual lives.

Where people already have some knowledge of Christianity, the seasonal sermon will be especially suitable since their minds will already be thinking along the same lines. Of course, we must always be careful not to lose the essential freedom to preach as the Holy Spirit leads.

Other special occasions may include national and community commemorative days; local church events, such as church anniversaries, dedication of buildings, consecration of workers, baptismal and communion services; and occasional services such as infant dedications, weddings, and funerals. Whatever the occasion, Paul advised, “Rejoice with those who rejoice; mourn with those who mourn. Live in harmony with one another” (Romans 12:15–16). Preach sermons that are appropriate to the needs of the hearers on each occasion. Remember, however, when preaching at times of national or community significance, your people came to hear the Word of God, not your opinion of the government and its policies. Politics and preaching do not mix.

Sermon Series

In our quest for a balanced presentation of the whole range of Christian doctrine, we quickly realize the limitations of the single sermon on a topic. If we tried to deal exhaustively with the theme, the sermon would be excessive in length. It would more likely give people spiritual indigestion than inspiration, and to simply return to the theme periodically would not take us much further either. Since so much of the sermon time would be spent in covering introductory matters, our treatment would tend to be superficial. In a sermon series, however, we are able to follow a theme or Scripture portion through in a systematic, regular fashion at a deeper level than the occasional sermon would permit.

The sermon series needs to be clearly distinguished from the serial . In the serial, the whole is sectioned off into parts, each of which continues and is dependent on what has preceded. In the series, however, each section is complete in itself and may be understood independently. While the preceding and succeeding sermons may add to the total picture, they are not essential to understand the individual sermon. Where church attendance is spasmodic , the series, rather than the serial, will be better. Whether in the regular pastoral preaching plan or in the special evangelistic meetings, I have found that an announcement of a theme for the forthcoming five or six meetings helps stimulate enthusiasm in the congregation. This is especially true if the topic is of current interest. Usually in pastoral ministry, it works best to follow the series each Sunday morning or evening rather than use both morning and evening. Some people will be able to attend mornings regularly, and others will only attend evenings. For a week’s special meetings, however, you should encourage attendance at every service so the series may fill the whole period.

Any theme or topic is appropriate for a series of sermons provided there is sufficient material. We need to note Jesus’ parable of the man building the tower (Luke 14:28–30). Before we announce a series, we need to be sure that we can deal adequately with the theme and have enough material to finish. It is wise to prepare an outline of the whole series before announcing it.

The wide variety of source material for sermon series should eliminate any sense of boredom in the congregation. After a thematic or doctrinal series, such as the second coming of Christ, the exposition of a particular book of the Bible could be undertaken, followed by a series on prominent Bible characters or perhaps the parables of Jesus. The preacher can retain enthusiasm by varying the type of series and interspersing each series with occasional sermons. Planning the series to lead up to or out of a special church event, such as the doctrine of the Holy Spirit around the Day of Pentecost, is also effective.

Length is always of major concern, since few of us could rival Harold Ockenga of Park Street, Boston, who preached his way through the entire New Testament at his Sunday morning and Friday evening meetings for twenty-one years (Ockenga 1959, 115); or the noted Bible commentator Matthew Henry, who held a Thursday “course of sermons” over Scripture questions that “occupied no less than twenty years” (Williams 1974, vol. 2, 126); or his father Philip’s sermon series, “once he spent about two months in preaching over the doctrine of the Lord’s Supper...and one he spent about half a year in preaching over the epistle to Laodicea, Revelations [sic], iii” (Williams 1974, vol. 1, 354). The following is an example of one month in a year’s planning calendar. In a calendar of your own, plan a balanced year of preaching for a congregation. Be sure to include the special events and needs of your own church and culture.

Obviously one sermon cannot deal with every problem, but every sermon should minister to some current problem if it is to be relevant to the hearers’ needs. The preacher’s joy is in hearing someone say, “Pastor, your sermon has helped me. Thank you for it.”

The Theme of the Occasion

As preachers, our goal should be to preach the whole range of Christian truths in a systematic , regular fashion. If we approach preaching in a casual way, we will quickly discover that our ministry tends to regularly turn into familiar, comfortable channels. A survey of last year’s preaching will demonstrate how limited a diet we are serving our hearers. Balance is as important to preachers as it is to dietitians.

The easiest and most efficient way to achieve a balanced presentation of truth is to plan our preaching in advance. This does not eliminate the inspiration of the Holy Spirit nor ignore His direction in what to preach. Since God knows everything, He already knows the best sermon for our congregation next week, next month, and next year. There is, therefore, no real reason why we should not already be praying for His direction as to what we will preach on in the future. Throughout fifteen years of busy pastoral life I made it a habit to use December each year for praying over the next year’s preaching plan. No plan was so rigid that it could not be changed nearer the date, but if I had truly prayed and followed the Spirit’s leading, that was seldom necessary.

This concept of advance planning is by no means new to the church. Within the first five hundred years of its existence, the church developed an ecclesiastical calendar in which the major events of the faith were clearly marked and sermons appropriate to the theme of the day were preached. This church year began with the four Sundays of Advent leading up to Christmas, covering themes such as Creation, the Bible, prophecy, and the preparation for the second coming of Christ, climaxing on Christmas Day with the birth of Christ and the doctrine of the Incarnation. Although the first half of the church year was closely organized around the central events of the life of Christ and His Church, only Easter, with its emphasis on the death and resurrection of Christ, Pentecost ( commemorating the outpouring of the Holy Spirit), and Trinity Sunday (the following week) are widely observed today. The second half of the year was deliberately kept free for themes emphasizing Christian life and witness.

In this way, the developing church ensured that the major doctrines of the Bible were covered each year, and we can do the same. We cannot afford to assume that our people know and remember the biblical basis for the main teachings of the faith. The regular, systematic exposition of these truths will both enrich their spiritual lives and strengthen them against false doctrine. Also, the intellectual and spiritual discipline of making these truths clear and of showing their relevance to modern man will be of great value to our own spiritual lives.

Where people already have some knowledge of Christianity, the seasonal sermon will be especially suitable since their minds will already be thinking along the same lines. Of course, we must always be careful not to lose the essential freedom to preach as the Holy Spirit leads.

Other special occasions may include national and community commemorative days; local church events, such as church anniversaries, dedication of buildings, consecration of workers, baptismal and communion services; and occasional services such as infant dedications, weddings, and funerals. Whatever the occasion, Paul advised, “Rejoice with those who rejoice; mourn with those who mourn. Live in harmony with one another” (Romans 12:15–16). Preach sermons that are appropriate to the needs of the hearers on each occasion. Remember, however, when preaching at times of national or community significance, your people came to hear the Word of God, not your opinion of the government and its policies. Politics and preaching do not mix.

Sermon Series

In our quest for a balanced presentation of the whole range of Christian doctrine, we quickly realize the limitations of the single sermon on a topic. If we tried to deal exhaustively with the theme, the sermon would be excessive in length. It would more likely give people spiritual indigestion than inspiration, and to simply return to the theme periodically would not take us much further either. Since so much of the sermon time would be spent in covering introductory matters, our treatment would tend to be superficial. In a sermon series, however, we are able to follow a theme or Scripture portion through in a systematic, regular fashion at a deeper level than the occasional sermon would permit.

The sermon series needs to be clearly distinguished from the serial . In the serial, the whole is sectioned off into parts, each of which continues and is dependent on what has preceded. In the series, however, each section is complete in itself and may be understood independently. While the preceding and succeeding sermons may add to the total picture, they are not essential to understand the individual sermon. Where church attendance is spasmodic , the series, rather than the serial, will be better. Whether in the regular pastoral preaching plan or in the special evangelistic meetings, I have found that an announcement of a theme for the forthcoming five or six meetings helps stimulate enthusiasm in the congregation. This is especially true if the topic is of current interest. Usually in pastoral ministry, it works best to follow the series each Sunday morning or evening rather than use both morning and evening. Some people will be able to attend mornings regularly, and others will only attend evenings. For a week’s special meetings, however, you should encourage attendance at every service so the series may fill the whole period.

Any theme or topic is appropriate for a series of sermons provided there is sufficient material. We need to note Jesus’ parable of the man building the tower (Luke 14:28–30). Before we announce a series, we need to be sure that we can deal adequately with the theme and have enough material to finish. It is wise to prepare an outline of the whole series before announcing it.

The wide variety of source material for sermon series should eliminate any sense of boredom in the congregation. After a thematic or doctrinal series, such as the second coming of Christ, the exposition of a particular book of the Bible could be undertaken, followed by a series on prominent Bible characters or perhaps the parables of Jesus. The preacher can retain enthusiasm by varying the type of series and interspersing each series with occasional sermons. Planning the series to lead up to or out of a special church event, such as the doctrine of the Holy Spirit around the Day of Pentecost, is also effective.

Length is always of major concern, since few of us could rival Harold Ockenga of Park Street, Boston, who preached his way through the entire New Testament at his Sunday morning and Friday evening meetings for twenty-one years (Ockenga 1959, 115); or the noted Bible commentator Matthew Henry, who held a Thursday “course of sermons” over Scripture questions that “occupied no less than twenty years” (Williams 1974, vol. 2, 126); or his father Philip’s sermon series, “once he spent about two months in preaching over the doctrine of the Lord’s Supper...and one he spent about half a year in preaching over the epistle to Laodicea, Revelations [sic], iii” (Williams 1974, vol. 1, 354). For most of us a month to six weeks is adequate early in our ministry. This also helps us fit the series in between special meetings that might interrupt the sequence.

Never plan a series of sermons without spending much time in prayer and sensing the leading of the Holy Spirit. The series is not simply an easy way out of the pressure of weekly preparation. Since there are many different ways to handle a Bible portion, the approach, as well as the subject matter, needs divine direction.

The following is an example of one month in a year’s planning calendar. In a calendar of your own, plan a balanced year of preaching for a congregation. Be sure to include the special events and needs of your own church and culture.

Bible Readings

The Bible reading or Bible study, as it is often called, is the systematic verse-by-verse exposition of a section of the Bible. It differs from the expository sermon in that while the sermon selects one main theme from a portion and applies it to the hearers’ consciences, the reading draws out all the main themes and shows their relationship to each other. The reading emphasizes doctrine, whereas the sermon stresses action. In the sermon we preach for verdicts; in the reading we teach for building up the church. The difference is in emphasis and style rather than in content, since all sermons have a doctrinal basis and involve elements of teaching. (See Chapter 2, Objective 2.3.1.) Paul delighted to describe himself as a preacher and a teacher.

Despite almost twenty centuries of Bible study there is widespread ignorance of the actual content of Scripture. The too-frequent loss of sincere Christian believers to false teaching should make us aware of the need for clear instruction in the truths of God’s Word. Systematic Bible exposition drives both preacher and people to the foundation of their faith in the Word of God.

The goal of the Bible reading is edification, building up God’s people. We must build soundly by personally exploring the scriptural foundations of our faith; we must build surely by carefully reasoning out our teachings on the basis of the Bible; and we must build simply by making abstract doctrines concrete. The key is this: “What does the Bible say about this, and how does it apply to my life?”

In the preparation of the Bible reading, it is essential to be fully familiar with the portion of Scripture to be expounded. Preparation involves the extensive use of study aids such as commentaries, Bible dictionaries, and encyclopedias to provide solid background material. Then, a clear outline is necessary if the reading is not to be a confusing ramble through the Bible countryside, plucking occasional flowers of thought blooming by the wayside.

The use of visual aids such as charts, diagrams, and printed handouts can help the congregation understand the major points of the study. As with any illustrative material, verbal or visual, it is better to have a few accurate and precise illustrations than many that are mediocre or obscure (Chapter 7).

Some teachers successfully involve the congregation in the study by asking and answering questions or by encouraging a general discussion of the topic following the introduction. The more mature the believers are, the better this works. Even so, the teacher has to provide firm and wise leadership to prevent dogmatic individuals from manipulating or dividing the congregation over some controversial issue.

With such a wealth of material from which to draw the substance of sermons, there is no reason for preachers ever to find themselves without fresh, relevant Bible content. As a young pastor, I was astounded when one of my church deacons said, “Pastor, I know where you got your sermon from this morning. I read the book myself last night.” I quickly began to defend the hours spent preparing the sermon when he continued, “The Bible! It was all from the Bible!”

**Q:** Why is it important to study the Bible in longer passages such as a whole book or letter?

**A:** Studying a whole section, book, or letter will give you the original intent of the author. It will also often give a new perspective of the material you are studying.

**Q:** Your author suggests that you study the original text or a modern translation of a text before reading a commentary. What effect will this have on your sermon?

**A:** This allows the Holy Spirit to inspire thoughts from the Word of God that are unique and specific. Your sermons will have a personalized meaning and will deeply minister to those who hear.

**Q:** What is the best way to organize your Bible study notes, and how is this beneficial in preparing sermons?

**A:** Create a system that will work best for you. Use either notebooks or a filing system. It is important to categorize by subject so you will be able to locate material as God directs you in sermon planning.

**Q:** How does Matthew 9:35–38 challenge the preacher? What kind of food (sermon material) should the shepherd provide?

**A:** The preacher (shepherd) needs to understand the people and preach sermons that will help them with their needs and problems.

**Q:** List some practical ways in which you might, as a pastor, learn the needs of your congregation.

**A:** Your own response. Every response will be different, but hopefully you will consider times of relaxing together over a meal or a cup coffee, as well as playing a game of golf or tennis. More intense times of sharing may take place in a counseling session or other private settings.

**Q:** Describe the link between congregational needs and sermon materials. How will ministering to the needs of the congregation expand sermon material possibilities?

**A:** When the pastor is responding to the needs of the congregation, there will not be a need to search for material. It will be a natural response, just as the shepherd feeds and comforts the lost sheep. The needs will dictate the sermons.

**Q:** What was the purpose for developing the ecclesiastical calendar?

**A:** The purpose of the church calendar was to ensure the teaching of the major biblical doctrines each year, as well as the scheduling of major church themes

**Q:** How can advance planning be beneficial to every church?

**A:** Every church should follow a similar plan to ensure balanced teaching, as well as organization.

**Q:** How can you use special occasions such as national or community celebrations as a time of evangelism?

**A:** Special occasions are times when many non-churched people will attend services. It is important to plan in advance to present the gospel at these times.

**Q:** How must you be careful when focusing on a special event?

**A:** The danger of special occasions is that the focus may be too heavily on the theme of the day rather than on Jesus Christ, who should be the central theme of every sermon.

**Q:** Explain the difference between the serial sermon and the sermon series .

**A:** The sermon serial is continued from one sermon to the next. In a sermon series each sermon is complete in itself. These sermons are connected by a common theme, book, or topic.

**Q:** Why would a preaching calendar be especially necessary when planning a series of biblical messages?

**A:** By planning a special series in advance, you can keep from interrupting your series with other events such as special occasions or guest speakers.

**Q:** Briefly describe the difference between the Bible reading and the expository sermon. Explain the value of systematic Bible readings or Bible studies.

**A:** The Bible reading emphasizes doctrine; the expository sermon focuses on action. Bible readings or Bible studies will ground believers in their faith. It is a follow-up to their conversion experience. 6 CHAPTER

#### 5.3.4 — Explain the value of systematic Bible readings.

Personal Bible Study

We should base our preaching on our own personal study of the Scriptures. This type of study is necessary to make us so familiar with the Scriptures that we will be able to draw on an ever-enlarging fund of knowledge for both primary and supporting sermon material.

In Chapter 4, we emphasized the importance of a consistent devotional life distinct from the Bible study time. At this time of intimate communion, we open our lives up to the scrutiny of God and listen earnestly to what He reveals to us from the Scriptures. Very often it is because God has spoken to us through a portion of the Word that we are able to take that Word and minister it to our people. G. Campbell Morgan tells of being in the office of the great Joseph Parker when a man came in to thank Parker for a great sermon he had preached that morning. “It did me good,” he said. “Sir,” replied Parker, “I preached it because it had done me good” (Campbell 1974, 50). The message had been filtered through the preacher’s own life; it was part of him. Powerful preaching is the outflow of the heart God has touched.

We will gain maximum benefit from our devotional reading and certainly in our Bible study times if we will read longer sections of Scripture rather than the brief suggestions offered in some Bible reading courses. In the study time it is quite easy to read a whole book of the Bible at one sitting. Mark’s Gospel, for instance, takes about forty-five minutes at a normal pace; the New Testament letters vary from two minutes for 2 or 3 John to thirty minutes for Romans or 1 Corinthians. By adopting this method, we gain a new perspective on the flow of Scriptural events and actually read the narrative as the writers intended.

We must also learn to apply ourselves to the art of intensive Bible study. An example of this is systematically going through books of the Bible in the original languages, if possible, or else with good modern translations and sound exegetical commentaries, making notes as we go. Long ago I learned the rule of never looking into a commentary until I had first put in the basic work on the text itself. Let the Word speak directly to your heart before you ask what it said to others.

It is helpful to compile some sort of index to the notebooks you write so that you can trace the leading ideas. In this way we lay the foundation for textual, expository, and topical sermons. It is also wise to sort through the notebooks periodically and transfer the material to a loose-leaf binder, using one sheet or more per subject. By using the loose-leaf method, you can add more sheets at the appropriate place as you continue to study.

If you make your notes on a handheld device, you can transfer them to your computer, and file them by making folders for each topic using the note page below as a template for storing the information systematically. You may also want to add a cross reference link to related subject folders. Do not forget however to back up your material or your careful research may be lost.

This means that you can always have sermons that are in the course of preparation for your regular preaching ministry. Sometimes God gives you the full sermon straight off; at other times He puts His finger on a sermon that has been preparing for weeks and says, in effect, “This is the Word for Sunday night.”

Below is a sample of a notebook page for Bible study notes. In your own notebook, create a system that will work for you. Whether you are currently in ministry or not, begin now to organize your Bible study time.

Subject :

Date :

Text:

Original language comments :

Commentary Notes :

Congregational Needs

Effective sermons are related to life; they are not conceived in a vacuum untouched by the pressing needs of the congregation. By visiting the flock, counseling troubled individuals, and observing the concerns of the day, the attentive preacher will find him or herself directed to speak on some themes more than others. So often God shows us a need and then moves us to minister to it. When Jesus encouraged His disciples to share His compassion for the “sheep without a shepherd,” He said, “The harvest is plentiful but the workers are few. Ask the Lord of the harvest, therefore, to send out workers into his harvest field.” Then, “he called his twelve disciples to him and gave them authority to drive out evil spirits and to cure every kind of disease and sickness” and He sent them out to minister to those “lost sheep” (Matthew 9:35–10:8). Unless we see the need, feel the pain, and are moved with compassion we are unlikely to minister effectively.

The preacher, then, should study the congregation. Their needs—whether physical, mental, moral, or spiritual—should be prayerfully considered when preparing a sermon. He or she should carefully tabulate the particular problems coming to light through day-to-day contact with the people and evaluate his or her preaching from that aspect.

Obviously one sermon cannot deal with every problem, but every sermon should minister to some current problem if it is to be relevant to the hearers’ needs. The preacher’s joy is in hearing someone say, “Pastor, your sermon has helped me. Thank you for it.”

## 6 - The Aim of the Sermon

### Evangelistic Preaching

#### 6.1.1 — Explain the purpose of the sermon objective.

The purpose of preaching has been defined as “to so present Christ by the power of His Spirit that man may be convicted of sin, converted to God, and challenged to strive for spiritual maturity” (Chapter 2). The goal of preaching—making and maturing believers—should always be kept in focus when preparing and delivering sermons. The task is not simply the communication of information, but also the development of disciples.

Taking the Bible as the basic material with which the structure of the sermon will be erected, the preacher soon realizes that the whole goal cannot be achieved in one super-sermon. The making and maturing of believers is a steady process. Therefore, each sermon may well focus on different parts of that process. The preacher must break down the general goal into specific, tangible objectives for each sermon. Through prayer, God’s goal for this particular sermon is established, and then all efforts are directed to achieving that goal.

Each specific objective may require a different approach. Thus, the preparation and delivery of a sermon intended to bring the unconverted sinner to faith in Christ will differ in more than just content from the message to the maturing believer. Even sermons directed toward believers may differ according to whether the goal is cognitive , behavioral , or affective .

On the basis of specific objectives, then, sermons may be categorized as evangelistic, doctrinal, ethical, or apologetic . However, although we may look at these categories in isolation, we find in practice that the average sermon may be primarily evangelistic, for example, and yet include elements from one or more of the other categories. This is because the average congregation includes people in various stages of spiritual development and because there may be more than one related objective in a particular sermon.

The Place for Evangelistic Preaching

What is evangelistic preaching? Does it have a place in pastoral ministry, or is it the prerogative of the professional evangelist?

Paul clearly distinguished the calling of the evangelist from that of the pastor-teacher in Ephesians 4:11. Therefore, some pastors see their ministry as not including the evangelistic sermon. Nevertheless, to the young pastor Timothy, Paul gave the exhortation not only to “preach the Word,” but also to “do the work of an evangelist” (2 Timothy 4:2, 5).

It is evident from the book of Acts that the early disciples of Jesus understood that the Great Commission to “go into all the world and preach the good news to all creation” and to “make disciples of all nations” was not limited to the apostles alone; it applied to every believer regardless of his or her personal ministry calling (Mark 16:15; Matthew 28:19). In the persecution that arose following the martyrdom of Stephen, the scattered believers went everywhere preaching the gospel, “and a great number of people believed and turned to the Lord” (Acts 8:1–7; 11:19–21). Notice that the apostles were not directly involved in this evangelistic preaching.

If we are called to preach, we are called to preach evangelistic sermons as much as any other kind of sermon. What W. E. Sangster described as “an awful incompleteness” hangs over the ministry in which evangelistic preaching rarely or never occurs. Paul’s proclamation of “the whole will of God” included “that they must turn to God in repentance and have faith in our Lord Jesus” (Acts 20:27; 20:21).

Characteristics of Evangelistic Preaching

In one sense, all preaching should be evangelistic. The great goal of the preacher is to win the hearers to wholehearted commitment to Christ. Every category of sermon in its own way should place the saving invitation before the people whether it be on the basis of the clear teachings of the faith, the moral demands of right living, or simply the sheer reasonableness of Christianity. Some, therefore, consider that the distinguishing mark of evangelistic preaching lies in the appeal or altar call with which the sermon ends.

Yet others define evangelistic preaching in terms of the simple gospel : you are a sinner; you need a Savior; Christ will save you if you repent and believe. Anything beyond that is not gospel preaching , but doctrine or ethics. Sangster told of the faithful pastor who dealt bluntly with his congregation about the prevalent sin of chicken stealing until the head deacon rebuked him with, “This congregation does not like sermons on chicken stealing and hopes that for the future you will keep to the gospel.”

Evangelistic preaching goes broader than the narrow, simple gospel addressed to the unconverted. The gospel touches every aspect of our lives. From that initial application of the gospel to the unconverted, evangelistic preaching continues to challenge the maturing believer with the claims of Christ. We do not arrive at such a state of maturity in this life that the call to further growth and development is no longer relevant.

The characteristics of evangelistic preaching may be distinguished as follows:

1. It is directed to the uncommitted.

2. It is directed to the will, through the intellect, emotions, and conscience.

3. It calls for a definite and immediate decision.

Jesus Christ is as much the Baptizer in the Holy Spirit as He is the Savior from sin; He is as fully the Healer of the physically sick body as He is the sin-sick soul. Therefore, our evangelistic message must include the full gospel of Jesus. Whatever the needs of the hearers may be, they must be made aware that Christ can meet those needs, and they must personally commit themselves in faith to Christ. Evangelistic preaching, then, is directed to the uncommitted.

Since we are to “preach for a verdict” (Chapter 2), our ultimate goal must be to move the will to action. We touch the will through the conscience—“I ought to do this”; through the emotions—“I feel I ought to do this”; and through the intellect—“I know I ought to do this.” These three approaches to the will are complementary and equally essential to moving the will to decisive action. To pressure the conscience alone can produce a sense of guilt and condemnation. To overemphasize the role of the emotions can result in emotional excess. To speak exclusively to the intellect can cause an arid rationalism. By retaining a proper balance between the three, the evangelistic preacher can avoid the dangers of extremism.

The glory of the gospel message is that God meets the needy person just where he or she is. “When we were still powerless, Christ died for the ungodly. . . . While we were still sinners, Christ died for us. . . . When we were God’s enemies, we were reconciled to him through the death of his Son” (Romans 5:6, 8, 10). God does not require the sinner to do anything to merit acceptance; Jesus did all that is needed. The evangelist can summon the uncommitted to faith here and now. The now of today is all the time we have. Tomorrow may be too late; it is too uncertain at best. The note of urgency, so obvious in the ministry of Jesus and the early apostles, cannot be missing from our own.

The call for a decision must be expressed in definite terms if a meaningful response is to be made. When Jesus summoned people to be His disciples, He did so tangibly and publicly. They left their old way of life and followed Him (Matthew 4:18–22). Evangelical commitment goes beyond mental or even verbal assent. It expresses a radical reorientation of life. It must, therefore, be demonstrated in appropriate action if it is to be meaningful. For this reason, the public altar call has proven a valuable tool for the evangelistic preacher (Chapter 10).

**Q:** How will 2 Timothy 4:2 affect your preaching priorities?

**A:** Evangelistic sermons should have a place of priority in preaching. Preaching evangelistic sermons should not be left to the visiting evangelist; instead, evangelistic sermons should be part of a balanced preaching program.

**Q:** In your own words, briefly state the three characteristics of evangelistic preaching.

**A:** Evangelistic preaching: (1) Focuses on the nonbeliever; (2) Is balanced preaching directed to the will, through the intellect, emotions, and conscience; (3) Asks for an immediate decision.

**Q:** Explain how you will use evangelistic preaching in your ministry.

**A:** Your own response. You should begin now to consider ways of including evangelistic opportunities in your sermon planning. Remember to call people to a point of decision while maintaining a balanced focus on the intellect, emotions, and conscience.

#### 6.1.2 — State the characteristics of evangelistic preaching.

The purpose of preaching has been defined as “to so present Christ by the power of His Spirit that man may be convicted of sin, converted to God, and challenged to strive for spiritual maturity” (Chapter 2). The goal of preaching—making and maturing believers—should always be kept in focus when preparing and delivering sermons. The task is not simply the communication of information, but also the development of disciples.

Taking the Bible as the basic material with which the structure of the sermon will be erected, the preacher soon realizes that the whole goal cannot be achieved in one super-sermon. The making and maturing of believers is a steady process. Therefore, each sermon may well focus on different parts of that process. The preacher must break down the general goal into specific, tangible objectives for each sermon. Through prayer, God’s goal for this particular sermon is established, and then all efforts are directed to achieving that goal.

Each specific objective may require a different approach. Thus, the preparation and delivery of a sermon intended to bring the unconverted sinner to faith in Christ will differ in more than just content from the message to the maturing believer. Even sermons directed toward believers may differ according to whether the goal is cognitive , behavioral , or affective .

On the basis of specific objectives, then, sermons may be categorized as evangelistic, doctrinal, ethical, or apologetic . However, although we may look at these categories in isolation, we find in practice that the average sermon may be primarily evangelistic, for example, and yet include elements from one or more of the other categories. This is because the average congregation includes people in various stages of spiritual development and because there may be more than one related objective in a particular sermon.

The Place for Evangelistic Preaching
