### Doctrinal Preaching

#### 6.2.1 — Identify effective methods of doctrinal preaching.

New Testament scholars use the Greek terms kerygma and didache to distinguish the two aspects of apostolic preaching and teaching. The kerygma is basically the proclamation of the basic gospel of Jesus Christ and corresponds largely to evangelistic preaching. The didache refers to the teaching based upon that proclamation that draws out the doctrinal, ethical, and moral implications of the gospel message. We will consider the ethical and moral aspects separately.

The Place for Doctrinal Preaching

While all preaching must include some element of doctrine or teaching if it is to be effective communication, there is a distinctive ministry of pastor-teacher separate from that of evangelist (Ephesians 4:11). Its goal is the maturing of the believer into the image of Christ.

The doctrinal aspect of preaching can never be eliminated from Christianity since any profession of belief or unbelief is necessarily a doctrine. Faith is not only an attitude of a heart towards God, but also a body of beliefs to be accepted and earnestly embraced (Jude 3). Among the responsibilities of the overseer / pastor is that “He must hold firmly to the trustworthy message as it has been taught, so that he can encourage others by sound doctrine and refute those who oppose it” (Titus 1:9). He must not only be devoted to the apostles’ teaching himself, but he also must entrust that teaching “to reliable men who will also be qualified to teach others” (Acts 2:42; 2 Timothy 2:2). This is the true apostolic succession —the continuation of the apostolic teachings as expressed in Scripture and illustrated in life. To Timothy, Paul wrote, “What you heard from me, keep as the pattern of sound teaching, with faith and love in Christ Jesus. Guard the good deposit that was entrusted to you—guard it with the help of the Holy Spirit who lives in us” (2 Timothy 1:13–14).

By avoiding doctrinal preaching, we cut the root of our evangelistic and ethical preaching. Perhaps the decline in doctrinal preaching may account for the rampant fanaticism devastating the church in some areas and for the false doctrines that subtly lead so many into the heretical cults. It certainly accounts for a lack of spiritual maturity.

The Characteristics of Doctrinal Preaching

The transmission of biblical teachings is not necessarily the dull, dry, and lifeless thing many fear it to be. We must admit that some preachers could jump into the ocean and come out dry, but the problem is with the preacher, not the ocean of divine truth. To take the great doctrines of the faith and to demonstrate to a congregation their vitality, centrality, and practicality may take hard work and clear thinking, but that should not be beyond the capabilities of the consecrated preacher. Our congregation has the right to know what we believe, why we believe what we believe, and how that belief affects daily life.

Doctrinal preaching is basically the preaching of the Bible, for that is foundational to Christian teaching. “All Scripture is God-breathed and is useful for teaching, rebuking, correcting and training in righteousness, so that the man of God may be thoroughly equipped for every good work” (2 Timothy 3:16–17). Paul even links the public reading of Scripture with preaching and teaching. He views it as worthy of the pastor’s devotion and contributing to the salvation of both the pastor and the people (1 Timothy 4:13–16). In many churches this biblical practice of including the public reading of a set portion of Scripture in each service continues today.

There are four basic instructional modes in effective teaching:

1. Didactic (telling)

2. Demonstrative (showing)

3. Interrogative (asking)

4. Contributive (sharing)

Didactic instruction is the basic form of the doctrinal sermon. It is the most direct and efficient way of communicating information; yet its effectiveness may be limited if it is not used in conjunction with the demonstrative mode. People remember only ten percent of what they hear compared with fifty percent of what they see and hear. We must, then, make our hearers see the point of what we are saying.

Demonstration may be mental: logical argumentation; verbal: illustrative stories; or visible: object lessons, pictures, and diagrams. The parables of Jesus are excellent examples of the art of demonstrative instruction.

The Lord also used the interrogative method with some frequency. He made people think by asking them pointed questions such as, “Whose portrait is this? And whose inscription? What do you think about the Christ? Whose son is he?” (Matthew 22:20, 42). Whether the audience supplies the answer verbally or mentally may depend on the preaching situation.

Even the contributive mode can be effectively incorporated directly in the sermon by allowing spontaneous comments or by incorporating material drawn from the hearers in advance of the sermon. An indirect contribution can be made by way of testimony or musical presentations earlier in the service.

The skillful combination of these instructional modes by one who has taken the time to understand thoroughly the teachings of Scripture can be invaluable in enriching and maturing the believers.

**Q:** Read Colossians 1:28, and discuss the importance of doctrinal preaching. What does Paul’s message say to you as a minister of the gospel, and how will it affect your ministry?

**A:** Doctrinal preaching will keep false teaching and heretical cults out of the church. Your goal as a pastor should be to present your congregation as “perfect in Christ.”

### Ethical Preaching

#### 6.3.1 — Describe how ethical preaching affects individuals and society.

The Place for Ethical Preaching

Some have regarded ethical preaching with suspicion as unworthy of the gospel preacher . Those who define evangelistic preaching in terms of the narrow simple gospel readily dismiss sermons on chicken stealing as “not the gospel.” Others remind us that God is more concerned with being than doing and warn against legalism and pharisaism. “For it is by grace you have been saved, through faith . . . not by works, so that no one can boast” (Ephesians 2:8–9). Having begun our Christian life by trusting in God, we cannot continue it by our own efforts (Galatians 3:3). Some consider ethical preaching to be useless since our best efforts are bound to fail. Even the great apostle Paul declared, “I have the desire to do what is good, but I cannot carry it out. For what I do is not the good I want to do; no, the evil I do not want to do—this I keep on doing” (Romans 7:18–19).

Such objections ignore the clear teachings of Scripture that we are responsible to God for the things that we do, good or bad (2 Corinthians 5:10). The Word of God clearly teaches that we are not saved by our own good deeds. Nevertheless, it also teaches, “For we are God’s workmanship, created in Christ Jesus to do good works, which God prepared in advance for us to do” (Ephesians 2:10). Although before we are saved we are bound to sin, we are now “controlled not by the sinful nature but by the Spirit” (Romans 8:9).

Ethics, then, is concerned with how we live out our salvation. Our hearers have a right to know what God expects of them as Christians. “Faith by itself, if it is not accompanied by action, is dead” (James 2:17). It is, therefore, vital that we preach an ethical gospel that clearly places before the believers God’s goal of right living.

The Characteristics of Ethical Preaching

Doctrinal preaching answers the question, “What should we believe?” Ethical preaching faces the issues of what should we do and how should we do it ?

The prophet Micah answered the first question in general terms: “He has showed you, O man, what is good. And what does the Lord require of you? To act justly and to love mercy and to walk humbly with your God” (Micah 6:8). Ethical preaching, therefore, has both a social and a personal aspect. Although politics and preaching do not mix, the preacher stands in the prophetic succession as God’s spokesperson against social evils, such as corruption, oppression, injustice, and immorality; he or she stands in favor of honesty, freedom, fairness, and moral integrity. This means the preacher must understand the biblical teaching about social righteousness in order to avoid “playing politics in the name of God.”

A minister once gave his small son a jigsaw puzzle of a map of the world to keep him occupied. A few minutes later, the young boy announced that he had finished. Amazed, his father asked him how he had fitted all the pieces together so quickly. “When I turned the pieces over,” the boy explained, “I discovered a picture of a man on the back, so I put the man together. When I turned the picture back over, I had put the world together also.” If we want to put our world together, we also must start with putting the man together. Changed people change society.

The problem with some ethical preaching is that it has tried to change society without changing people first. Personal ethics give a basis for social ethics. Paul’s clear instructions on living “as children of light” (Ephesians 4:8–5:21) provide directions to “wives and husbands,” “children and parents,” and “slaves and masters” (Ephesians 5:22–6:9). Society is composed of individuals. Society cannot be just, merciful, and considerate if I, personally, am not.

The Bible also speaks clearly to those personal matters that seem to have no direct effect on anyone other than the individual, such as the inward sins of “impurity . . . hatred . . . jealousy . . . selfish ambition . . . and envy” (Galatians 5:19–21) . You should put off this “former way of life,” Paul preached, and you are to be made “new in the attitude of your minds” (Ephesians 4:22–23).

If ethical preaching only addresses the question, “What should we do?” and never answers the companion, “How should we do it?” it will inform but fail to provide practical solutions to the problems of life. Many, like Paul, “have the desire to do what is good, but . . . cannot carry it out” (Romans 7:18). It is the preacher’s responsibility to show his hearers how they can fulfill God’s commands.

Thus ethical preaching must be expressed in realistic, practical terms that

1. The preacher has proven to work in his or her own life;

2. Are simply expressed step by step;

3. Recognize the differences in personality and ability in the hearers; and

4. Are biblically based, not just good advice.

The greatest danger here lies in presenting a message that is problem-centered or human-centered rather than God-centered. When Peter focused his attention on the wind—his circumstances—instead of the Lord, he began to sink (Matthew 14:29–31). We must fill our hearers’ minds with God’s power rather than human problems if we are to mature victorious disciples.

**Q:** What is your personal definition of ethical preaching?

**A:** Student’s definition should represent their expectations for how to teach people to live out their salvation in practical obedience to the commands of Christ.

**Q:** Read Philippians 2:12–13. What effect did ethical preaching have on the Christians at Philippi?

**A:** The Philippian Christians learned to live godly lives whether in public or in private. It is God’s purpose to change our will and our actions to accomplish His purposes.

**Q:** Consider the daily temptations of the members of your congregation. How will ethical preaching from passages such as Titus 2:11–14 apply to the pressures of today’s living?

**A:** In an overwhelming world of confusion, the Word of God draws clear lines of right and wrong. By preaching on how we should live, we can direct people to self-control and godly living.

**Q:** Explain how ethical preaching is different from doctrinal preaching. Describe the effect this kind of preaching will have on an individual and on society.

**A:** Doctrinal preaching answers the question, “What should we believe?” Ethical preaching answers the questions, “What should we do?” and “How should we do it?” It focuses first on personal ethics, which will also have an effect on social ethics. Society cannot change until individuals change.

**Q:** In your own words briefly define apologetic preaching.

**A:** Apologetic preaching is a clear presentation of the gospel that answers the question, “Why should we believe?”

### Apologetic Preaching

#### 6.4.1 — Define apologetic preaching, and list its goals.

The Place of Apologetic Preaching

That Christianity is under attack throughout the world is obvious. Sometimes the attack is physical; often it is intellectual; always it is spiritual. Apologetic preaching seeks to present the Christian faith so clearly that it will repel the intellectual attacks upon Christianity and give the attackers every reason to believe in God. It seeks to answer the question “Why should we believe or do this?” It is not Christianity on the defensive, but a reasonable account of the faith. Peter commanded, “Always be prepared to give an answer to everyone who asks you to give the reason for the hope that you have” (1 Peter 3:15).

The contrast that some see between faith and reason has led them to neglect the mind of people and to appeal mainly to the emotions. Under evangelistic preaching, we saw the danger of an overemphasis on emotion. When faced only by an emotional presentation of the gospel, many educated people do not realize that the Christian faith is also intellectually sound and so refuse even to consider it.

It is true that we are not saved by our intelligence, but by faith. It is also true that unaided, people will never understand and accept the truth of God; the Holy Spirit must reveal the things of God to them (1 Corinthians 2:6–14). Nevertheless, the Word states, “For since the creation of the world God’s invisible qualities—his eternal power and divine nature—have been clearly seen, being understood from what has been made, so that men are without excuse” (Romans 1:20).

The evidence drawn from the natural sciences, philosophy, or other areas of study can provide a point of contact for us to present the message of God to those who may not otherwise listen to us at all.

The Characteristics of Apologetic Preaching

Jesus was concerned with what people think (Mark 8:27). He both asked questions and answered them (Matthew 21:28; 22:42). He left no one in doubt regarding the truth unless they were unwilling even to consider the truth.

In apologetic preaching, then, we must seek to

1. Refute the arguments of opponents of the gospel.

2. Reason so clearly that we convince honest inquirers of the credibility of the gospel.

3. Reassure believers by such a clear statement of the evidence for Christianity that their faith will be confirmed and they will be strengthened against false teaching.

To achieve the goals of apologetic preaching, we must carefully investigate every aspect of the topic we are considering. Then we must arrange the material discovered in clear, logical order (Chapter 8), ensuring that the arrangement and the conclusion directly answer the question with which the sermon began.

The following outline is an example of an apologetic sermon:

Why I Believe in the Resurrection of Christ

I. Because of the Absurdity of the Alternatives

A. No other theory fits the evidence.

B. No other theory could produce the results.

II. Because of the Reliability of the Witnesses

A. They were known for their honesty.

B. They were reluctant to believe.

C. They were never contradicted.

III. Because of the Necessity for Salvation

A. It is crucial to the claims of Christ.

B. It is integral to the gospel message.

IV. Because of the Certainty of Personal Experience

A. The testimony of the Christians

B. My personal testimony

While great service can be done to believers and nonbelievers alike by apologetic preaching, only the one who is thoroughly prepared and sure of the ground must do it. To present a weak argument or an untrue claim can do immense harm.

Whatever the approach we adopt in a particular sermon, we must plan all of our material to achieve the specific objective that God has given us for that sermon. We cannot wander from the path He has set if we are to arrive at His appointed goal.

**Q:** List the goals of apologetic preaching in a way that will help you recall them for future reference.

**A:** (1) refute arguments, (2) reason clearly, and (3) reassure believers.

**Q:** What question does apologetic preaching seek to answer? How is it different from other kinds of preaching?

**A:** Apologetic preaching seeks to answer the question, “Why should we believe or do this?” It goes beyond the basic presentation of evangelistic preaching and doctrinal teaching. It challenges the intellect with reason and reassurance. 7 CHAPTER

## 7 - The Illustration of the Sermon

### Purpose

#### 7.1.1 — Explain the purpose of sermon illustrations.

Illustrations are neither decorations added to beautify an otherwise plain construction, nor are they fillers included to use up time when the sermon is lacking in substance. They can never be substitutes for solid Bible exposition and argument, nor should they be used to entertain the hearers during an otherwise boring discourse on some obscure point of doctrine. Illustrations are intended to highlight the truth, not distract the hearers from the truth by drawing attention to themselves.

Illumination

The basic idea of illumination is to shed light upon. Spurgeon compared illustrations to windows in a house (Spurgeon 1890). The more decorative they are, like the stained glass windows of old European churches, the more they draw attention to themselves and the less you can see by their light.

The illustration must serve the sermon by making dark things clear. If no illustration is necessary, do not include one. If the illustration needs explaining because it is not easily understood, replace it with a better one. It is better to have none than one that is unclear or even misleading.

Many of the parable s of Jesus are introduced by statements such as, “Then Jesus told his disciples a parable to show them that they should always pray and not give up” (Luke 18:1). Even if the point is not given at the beginning, it is obvious in the story itself. “When the chief priests and the Pharisees heard Jesus’ parables, they knew he was talking about them” (Matthew 21:45).

Interest

By the wise and careful use of illustration the preacher can make contact with the mind of the hearers at a point of interest to them. For instance, an athletic illustration helps the athlete see the point (1 Corinthians 9:24–27; 2 Timothy 2:3–7). The sermon comes alive because the preacher and the hearer are on familiar ground.

Obviously, it is essential for you to check the details of the illustration for accuracy. If your analogy is not true, the expert in your congregation may not only doubt your story, but also reject your whole sermon as untrue. I have made it a practice not to use an illustration unless I can confirm its accuracy with an expert. Even textbooks may be out of date, especially in the physical sciences.

Impressiveness

The old story can become boring if repeated in the same old way. Even the most precious truths can seem trite and insignificant because everyone has heard it all before. But clear, fresh illustrations can make the familiar seem new. See how Jesus shows the love of God for the lost sinner in the three parables of Luke 15.

People who have long ago closed their minds to the gospel message can be made to think again by a striking story. It could even be the turning point in their life.

Recollection

So often people remember illustrations more than any other part of the sermon. Although they may forget your homiletical outline and your carefully balanced argument, your stories will live with them. How important it is, then, that you make sure your illustration carries the point of the sermon as clearly as possible. The story may be memorable, but your hearers must know and remember why you told it.

To be memorable the illustration must live. Dull stories, told in a dull fashion, are easily and gladly forgotten. The overuse of adjectives, such as the greatest , most amazing , and fantastic , has a deadening rather than exciting effect. The illustration must also be as brief as possible. It is always easier to remember a few things than a large number. The wordier you are, the greater the possibility of your hearers missing the point of the story. If your illustration is not understandable, your hearers will easily forget it. Leave the technical language to the experts, and put your illustration in words that anyone can understand. Finally, if you cannot remember the illustration clearly and accurately, you cannot expect others to.

Persuasion

The final test of the sermon and the illustration is this: does it do what it was intended to do? Persuasion is the goal of all sermons. Your hearers may reject your arguments and ignore your exhortations, but they cannot help being moved by the pictures you draw on their minds. See how King David was affected by the prophet Nathan’s story of the poor man’s lamb (2 Samuel 12). The well-chosen illustration is a servant to the message you deliver.

**Q:** According to the author of your IST, why did Jesus use parables?

**A:** Jesus used parables to illustrate His sermons.

**Q:** If you are preaching a sermon on tithing, what would be the purpose of an illustration for this sermon? What illustration would you use? Why would it be necessary to check the accuracy of your illustration?

**A:** An illustration would make the scriptural teaching relevant and applicable to the modern-day congregation. You could use a current example of this biblical principle that would help people understand the benefits of obedience to God’s Word. It is important that every illustration be accurate. If someone realizes that it is inaccurate, it will hinder that one’s ability to concentrate on the rest of the sermon.

**Q:** List at least three purposes of sermon illustrations.

**A:** (1) Illustrations help people remember the sermon point. (2) Illustrations make the familiar seem new. (3) An effective illustration can help persuade a person to act on the truth being presented.

### Types

#### 7.2.1 — Identify the various types of illustrations.

Illustrations fall into two groups: verbal and visual. Note the various kinds of illustrations that fall into the verbal category.

Verbal

Figures of speech can often be most effective illustrations. They slip in almost unnoticed but make the sermon live. Jesus is the master illustrator. See His metaphors to the Pharisees, “Blind guides . . . You snakes!” (Matthew 23:16, 33); or to His disciples, “Little flock” (Luke 12:32). See also His similes of Jerusalem, “How often I have longed to gather your children together, as a hen gathers her chicks under her wings” (Matthew 23:37); or of His second coming, “For as the lightning comes from the east and flashes to the west, so will be the coming of the Son of Man” (Matthew 24:27).

Quotations can effectively support a point you wish to make. You may even use the words of those you disagree with to provide contrast. You can see an example of this in Matthew 5:31–44. When using quotations, however, make sure that you are quoting accurately and in the sense that the original speaker intended. Beware of the quotation habit. Use only words of importance from people of some authority, especially in the subject on which you are quoting them. Most preachers have some favorite authors or favorite pieces of poetry; beware of frequent repetition.

Anecdotes are stories—incidents drawn from others’ lives, our own, or imaginary ones. History and biography are so full of illustrative events that preachers do not always need to talk about themselves and their families. The stories may be analogies (drawing parallels between natural things and the things of God—Luke 11:5–10). They may also be parables (everyday events demonstrating spiritual truths—Matthew 13:3–52); or even fables (imaginary situations highlighting human characteristics—Judges 9:8–20). To assert that a story is true when it did not happen in precisely the way you tell it is still lying. Sangster even warns against the true story that sounds untrue. You should not permit anything to draw attention away from the sermon itself, not even the most superb illustration.

Visual

The most obvious visual illustration of the sermon is the preacher him- or herself. The manner that the preacher uses to present the material will affect the way it is received by the hearers. Effective preachers preach with their whole body. Their physical movements, gestures, even the expressions on their faces show their sermons. George Whitefield preached eternal judgment with a tear in his eye. If we do not appear to be moved by our subject, we cannot expect our hearers to be. They are watching us throughout the presentation of the message.

The verbal presentation of the sermon can also be enhanced by the use of additional visual aids, such as objects, pictures, and diagrams. Some scholars believe that Jesus could see the sower at work when He told the parable of the sower (Mark 4:3), or that He held up a mustard seed (Matthew 13:31), or that He was walking through the temple near the vine sculpture when He was speaking of the True Vine (John 15:1). By showing an actual object, the dramatic effect of the point can be intensified. Even miracles of healing can both illustrate and confirm the message we preach by demonstrating God’s attitude toward human needs (Mark 2:10–12; 16:20).

The use of slides and movies by missionaries is common and very effective. Pictures, maps, chalkboard, black light artistry, or DVD clips can also be used to reinforce the impact of the sermon. Our hearers can literally see what we are talking about. This, however, is no substitute for prophetic and anointed content.

Particularly in a teaching situation, diagrams are invaluable in demonstrating the relationship between the different points of the message. Even a simple outline in the form of a handout or written on a chalkboard, overhead projector transparency, or PowerPoint can help the hearers follow the points as they are made. Historical and prophetic studies can be greatly enriched by clear time charts. The key to successful diagramming is simplicity. The easier your visual aid is to follow, the better it will be understood. As with any illustration, it is better to state things clearly.

**Q:** Choose an appropriate visual illustration for each sermon title and text:

**A:** a) “Lessons from the Tabernacle” (Exodus 26–30) b) “Let Your Light Shine” (Matthew 5:14) c) “God’s Armor” (Ephesians 6:10–20) d) “Love in Action” (Isaiah 58:6–14) Your responses may be different from these; however, here are some suggestions: a) When studying the tabernacle, either a chart, overhead projection, or miniature replica will allow the audience to understand and picture the various objects and locations. b) Demonstrate light by holding a light in front of the people. Another idea is to distribute candles to everyone in a darkened room. Begin with one candle and then, one by one, light each one throughout the church. c) Large, life-size pictures of the individual pieces of armor will remain in the minds of hearers for a long time. d) For a missions emphasis, show slides of giving to the needy people of the world. You could also use this as a day for a food collection for the poor people within your community.

### Sources

#### 7.3.1 — Describe the sources of sermon illustrations.

Someone urged W. E. Sangster as a young preacher to obtain two books to help him prepare his sermons: Five Hundred Sermon Outlines and Six Thousand Moral and Religious Anecdotes.

While this recommendation might seem to simplify the process of finding illustrations, it ignores the fact that such illustrations tend to be impersonal and contrived. Such shortcuts to sermon preparation have no place in the library of the preacher who intends to deliver the Word of the Lord. The illustration should be as much God’s Word to the preacher’s heart as the rest of the sermon. The more open we are to the guidance of the Holy Spirit in our sermon preparation, the more readily that guidance will come. “This is the word that came to Jeremiah the prophet from the Lord: ‘Go down to the potter’s house, and there I will give you my message.’ So I went down to the potter’s house, and I saw him working at the wheel” (Jeremiah 18:1–3).

As preachers, we must cultivate eyes that see and ears that hear. The illustrations are everywhere. “The heavens declare the glory of God; the skies proclaim the work of his hands. Day after day they pour forth speech; night after night they display knowledge” (Psalm 19:1–2).

God will either bring the illustration to you or direct you to the illustration, but the illustration will become part of you.

Literature

Of all the literature giving insight into the problems of life, none surpasses the Bible . Few of our hearers are really familiar with the Bible despite our many exhortations to read it. To use Bible incidents to illustrate our sermons can send our hearers home determined to read such stories for themselves. The greatest advantage in using Bible illustrations lies in their divine authority. “These things happened to them as examples,” wrote Paul, “and were written down as warnings for us” (1 Corinthians 10:11). God intends us to use the Bible for illustration. Jesus used Old Testament incidents to illustrate His sermons: “As it was in the days of Noah, so it will be at the coming of the Son of Man” (Matthew 24:37).

Probably the next most fruitful literary source of illustration is the newspaper. Since your hearers tend to be aware of what is happening in the world, you can show them how current events fit in with the Bible’s teachings. The problem is that today’s news becomes tomorrow’s history and can make your sermon seem out-of-date unless you keep your material fresh. Thanks to the Internet, it is possible to access newspapers on line, keep abreast of developments and acquire new breaking stories from other news media.

Books, especially if they are well-known to your hearers, often provide solid illustrations. Biographies are probably the most valuable. Nothing illustrates life like life. Even if subjects were not saints, we may still learn lessons from their life experiences. Well-written autobiographies can be helpful also, but avoid the sensationalist and sordid “My Wicked Life.” Look for those that give a balanced account, handle the material well, and present the subject interestingly. Since many books are now available in ‘liquid’ form on the Web, a good search engine can speed your research for specific topic illustrations. Care should be taken to avoid out-of-context quotations or anecdotes that just happen to contain your chosen phrase.

Experience

The parables of Jesus are clear examples of how to see spiritual truths in the ordinary things of life. The preacher must learn how to observe life. Often the message of God came to the prophets through the things they saw (Jeremiah 1:11–14; Ezekiel 47:6).

Some preachers carry a notebook with them to record their observations that would otherwise be forgotten. Your personal interests usually determine the things you notice at first, but once you start observing life your alertness will increase. You will become more sensitive to the Holy Spirit’s prompting.

A word of caution is needed regarding personal experiences and family incidents. No one is as interested in you and your family as you are. If your sermon is full of personal references, you will give the impression that you are self-centered. A deacon lamented that the worst thing they did was send the pastor to the Holy Land on vacation: “We thought it would enrich his ministry. Now he cannot open the Bible without reminiscing on his visit to this place or that Bible site.” An occasional personal reference is fine because it shows that you are in touch with real life, but keep it occasional.

Whatever the source of our illustrations, we must make sure that they are truly ours, fashioned and molded by being part of our lives. It is only as they reveal divine truth to us that they are likely to reveal that same truth to our hearers.

**Q:** Take some time to consider your literature resources. List some specific examples that you will want to use for sermon illustrations.

**A:** Everyone will have a different list. The Bible, of course, will be a primary source, but you should also have some specific biographies that are especially meaningful to you. You may also list some periodicals that offer insight into both everyday life and religious thought.

**Q:** Name as many sources as you can for sermon illustrations.

**A:** Your answer, but could include observations from daily life, a relevant news article, a meaningful biography.

### Use

#### 7.4.1 — Distinguish between correct and incorrect uses of sermon illustrations.

Appropriate

There is a right time in the sermon for each illustration just as there is a right illustration for each point in the sermon. This element of timeliness is essential if the illustration is to have the maximum effect. The sermon should flow naturally into and on from the illustration. If it breaks the flow, move it. Ask yourself whether the illustration introduces or raises the topic, whether it clarifies or illuminates the issues, or whether it proposes or points in the direction of a solution. This will help you determine the appropriate place for each illustration.

If you are not sure whether an illustration is suitable or not, you would be better leaving it out. Do not introduce it with, “I am not sure whether this story illustrates the point or not.” If you do not know as the preacher, how can you expect your hearers to know?

Accurate

Although there is a place for the imaginary story in preaching, illustrations should not be another term for lies. You owe it to your hearers and to your Master to be as accurate as possible in relating incidents and quotations. If you do not remember the details, do not use the illustration. You should do your best to check the details and note them in your store of illustrations so that you know where your information came from.

What Sangster calls “one of the commonest dishonesties in the pulpit” is describing incidents from the experience of others as though they happened to you. That kind of deception is certain to be exposed and you will be disgraced. Even if no one appears to know the truth, most people know when they are being lied to, and they lose confidence in their preacher.

Varied

Since there are so many different ways to illustrate sermons, you should never bore your hearers with a steady diet of quotations, reminiscences, or verses of familiar hymns. Not only can you vary the source and the type, but also the mood of your illustrations. By carefully using humorous, sad, challenging, and uplifting illustrations you will not dull your hearers’ senses by too much of one mood.

Sparing

There are no rules about how many illustrations you should have for each sermon or for each point of the sermon. Since the main purpose of the illustration is to illuminate, wherever extra light is needed is the place for an illustration. Some parts of the sermon are clear enough and work well without illustration. In other places you may use several to highlight different aspects of the truth. A note of caution is needed regarding filling a sermon with statistics or technological content designed to impress or even shock the hearers. Remember, “there are lies, big lies, and statistics.” Not all statistics are valid nor is their common interpretation valid. For example, statistics do not prove that fifty percent of all American marriages end in divorce each year, but rather there are twice as many marriages as divorces each year—a far different result.

To pack a weak sermon with an abundance of interesting stories may entertain, but it cannot substitute for solid exposition of the Word of God. Remember, it is the Word of God that brings life (Matthew 4:4; John 6:63, 68).

**Q:** Briefly describe how you would illustrate each of the following subjects: (a) the cost of discipleship; (b) friendship; (c) forgiveness; (d) a bitter spirit; (e) temptation; and (f) persistence.

**A:** Your answer will be different from these, but they should include a variety of personally appropriate illustrations. a) A quotation from the hymn by Isaac Watts, “When I Survey the Wondrous Cross” b) The biblical story of David and Jonathan (1 Samuel 20) c) The Parable of the Lost Son (Luke 15:11–32) d) An anecdote about a businessman who lost everything as a result of bitterness e) A personal story from childhood regarding the temptation to steal f) The true story of a blind person who learned to play the piano

### Storage

#### 7.5.1 — Create a filing system for sermon illustrations.

The Need

Few people can remember everything they have read or even encountered in life. Even the unforgettable incidents of yesterday may be forgotten today. The bishop was amused to hear a young preacher declare, “The happiest days of my life were spent in the arms of another man’s wife—my mother!” The next Sunday the bishop attempted to use the same alarming statement. “The happiest days of my life were spent in the arms of another man’s wife,” he paused, memory fleeing, “but I cannot remember who she was!”

We remember best by frequent repetition, but preachers must use fresh material in their sermons. They need some means of storing their illustrations ready for use, as the message requires.

The System

Most preachers develop a system of notebooks in which they record their illustrations, either writing them out in full with details of source, time, and place, or noting the book and page number where the illustration can be found. Since you will multiply notebooks over the years, you will need to develop a topical index for them. You can then find each illustration easily. This same basic method can easily be adapted to computer storage. The notes regarding an illustration from your handheld device can be transferred to your computer in an expanded form using the sample page as a template. Once again, however, remember to back up your material, or all may be lost in a hard drive failure.

Some preachers write out each illustration in full on index cards and file them in alphabetical order according to the topical key word. I found that method much too expensive in time and money.

I prefer to use a loose-leaf notebook with each topic on a fresh page in alphabetical order. As a page is filled up, an additional one can be inserted without disrupting the order. If an illustration will fit under more than one topic, a simple cross-reference can be included, such as, “Atonement (see also death of Christ, redemption, and salvation).” Not only is this easier to maintain, but also you do not need a topical index; your material is in topical order.

Sermon Illustrations

Topic:

Source:

Time:

Place:

Newspaper and magazine clippings can easily be kept in large envelopes. You can mark these envelopes according to the topic and record them in your topical notebook with a few key words to remind you of the contents. As illustrations often become dated—especially newspaper clippings—you will need to go through your store periodically and remove the unusable.

Whatever system you adopt needs to be workable for you, and it should not distract you from your primary task of ministry.

The task of providing applications for the truth is one that no preacher can afford to neglect. Our goal is to bring our hearers to God “not with wise and persuasive words, but with a demonstration of the Spirit’s power” (1 Corinthians 2:4).

**Q:** If you do not already have a system for filing sermon illustrations, begin one now. Set up a plan that will work for you. Use the suggestions given in this lesson to help you organize your ideas.

**A:** Answers will vary UNIT 3

## 8 - The Outline

### Advantages

#### 8.1.1 — Explain the importance of preparing a sermon outline.

We may compare the sermon outline to the skeleton of the body. Without it, the body is a formless mass of organs, muscles, veins, and skin. The skeleton provides not only beauty and balance to the body, but also strength and definition. William Evans said, “very often the only difference between a sermon great in power and one lacking in power is a difference in the arrangement” (Evans 1913, 69). The contents may be good, but poor structure or none at all will prevent that sermon from achieving the goal God has set for it.

A muddled sermon gives the impression that you do not understand your subject, that you have not spent time mastering your message, and that you are lazy in the area most vital to your ministry: communicating the Word of God. Skip the work here and you cannot blame your hearers for feeling cheated.

Clarity

A young minister preached in the presence of Joseph Parker and asked for his comments. “Young man,” said the great Bible expositor, “for the last half-hour you have been trying to get some thought out of your head instead of something into mine!” Unless you know your message well enough to put it into words that your hearers can understand, you are not ready to preach it. Every thought must be clearly defined and placed in its proper sequence. Before you begin to preach, you can review your outline with the sole purpose of making sure that the basic message is clear to you and that it comes through to others. Paul’s warning regarding spiritual gifts could apply here also. “If the trumpet does not sound a clear call, who will get ready for battle?” (1 Corinthians 14:8). Go back to the aim of the sermon and ask yourself, “What do I wish to say? Am I saying it clearly?”

Memory

It is naturally easier to remember a sermon that is clearly arranged than one that is confusing. For this reason, I prefer a well-defined outline with fairly prominent main divisions. Not only can I remember the outline more easily for preaching, but my hearers can also remember the message for future use.

A simple sermon structure is easier to remember. Division headings of more than five words are harder to remember. Complicated theological jargon has no place in the pulpit. You are not there to impress people with your cleverness. If you cannot get simple divisions in your own words, use the actual wording of the text. Notice the following example of a sermon outline.

Title: What do you know? (Ephesians 1:18–19)

I. The Hope of His Calling

II. The Riches of His Glorious Inheritance

III. The Greatness of His Power

It is also easier to remember a few things rather than a great number. My only memory of one sermon in the college chapel during my student days is the title “Ten Timeless Truths.” After sixty pages of manuscript and ninety minutes of preaching, nothing registered but the three words of the title. There seems to be a natural bias in favor of three or four as the ideal number of divisions, although some preachers prefer to use only two. Usually the text itself clearly determines the divisions that are natural to it, so the reading of the text will remind your hearers of your outline and your message.

**Q:** Discuss the benefits of the sermon outline for both the preacher and the hearer.

**A:** A sermon outline makes the sermon clear to both the preacher and the hearers. The preacher will always know where he or she is going and how he or she is going to get there. The hearers will have a clear presentation that will help them remember the main points of the sermon.

**Q:** Review Ephesians 1:18–19 and the suggested outline given by your IST author. List the various aspects of the outline that make it easy to remember.

**A:** Your answer should include these points: there are prominent main divisions, the division headings use very few words, the divisions are taken directly from the text, there are less than four divisions.

#### 8.1.2 — Discuss the benefits of a sermon outline for both the preacher and the hearer.

We may compare the sermon outline to the skeleton of the body. Without it, the body is a formless mass of organs, muscles, veins, and skin. The skeleton provides not only beauty and balance to the body, but also strength and definition. **Q:** Review Ephesians 1:18–19 and the suggested outline given by your IST author. List the various aspects of the outline that make it easy to remember.

**A:** Your answer should include these points: there are prominent main divisions, the division headings use very few words, the divisions are taken directly from the text, there are less than four divisions.

### Principles

#### 8.2.1 — Demonstrate the basic steps involved in developing a sermon outline.

In Chapter 4 we described the sermon as “the faithful exposition and application of Scripture to the needs of the hearers by the power of the Holy Spirit.” We see then that the sermon is not an essay. An essay is literature; it is meant to be read. Its style of writing and structure differ from the sermon’s. For example, the use of repetition is appropriate in a sermon but out of place in an essay. A major difference lies in the fact that the essay is intended to inform, interest, and entertain, whereas the sermon seeks a decisive response.

We also see that the sermon is different from the lecture. Although both are spoken, the lecture appeals mainly to the mind to inform and stimulate thought. The sermon appeals to the heart to stir it to action. The sermon’s ultimate goal is to move the will. To produce such a clear and lasting effect on the hearers, the sermon must have both unity and continuity.

Unity

In our sermon preparation, we began with the text from which we derived both the theme and the aim. For the outline to develop the text and theme in the light of the aim, there must be a unity of thought and structure about it.

A succession of unrelated or loosely related thoughts will fail to achieve the goal you are working towards. The outline must have coherence—that is, each part must have a logical, consistent, and intelligible relationship to what precedes and follows it.

To maintain unity, you must ensure that your divisions correspond to the nature and design of the subject. They must be part of the complete picture you are presenting. Martyn Lloyd-Jones told of an old Welsh preacher preparing a sermon on Numbers 22:21, “Balaam got up in the morning, [and] saddled his donkey.” His first division was “A Good Trait in a Bad Character” (he arose early); second, “An Antiquity of Saddlery” (he saddled his donkey); and third—every preacher must have three divisions—“A Few Remarks Concerning the Woman of Samaria.”

The two basic steps in obtaining a harmonious and coherent sermon structure are analysis and synthesis . In analysis we break the text or theme up into its constituent parts. Sometimes the text makes several statements about a central theme; at other times there are several themes that could each provide an outline. An example of this is John 3:16 where we find the Love of God, the Gift, and Eternal Life.

Study the three different themes outlined on the following page. I have filled in the main divisions of the first one as a suggested pattern. Please fill in the second two outlines; then compare your outlines with the ones given in the answer to Interactive Question 4.

John 3:16, The Love of God

I. The Scope —“God so loved the world”

II. The Measure —“He gave his one and only Son”

III. The Invitation —“Whosoever believes in him”

IV. The Provision —“Shall not perish but have eternal life”

John 3:16, The Gift

I.

II.

III.

IV.

V.

John 3:16, Eternal Life

I.

II.

III.

In our analysis of the text and chosen theme, we may find not only main divisions, but also subdivisions emerging under each main division (see Chapter 6 on Apologetic Preaching for an example). Main and subdivisions alike, however, must form a single thought. Analysis without synthesis may provide divisions that develop different themes. While each division should not say the same thing, each should deal with the various parts of the same thing so that when they are all put together they form one message.

Buttrick (1931) says “the mood of the sermon and often its very form and outline are found within the mood of the text. It is surely a prime necessity that the mood of the sermon should be congruous with the mood of the text” (153). If the text pleads, so should the sermon; if the text challenges, the sermon should also challenge; if the text reassures, so should the sermon. Each step of the outline should reinforce the dominant mood of the text to bring the hearers to a clear awareness of its message.

Continuity

Continuity is the orderly arrangement of your sermon material so that you begin at the beginning and end at the conclusion. There must be a beginning because you cannot say everything at once. There must be a conclusion in which you finish your message and not just stop speaking. The conclusion should correspond properly to your goal (Chapter 10). The outline of the main body of the message must, therefore, spell out step-by-step how you will reach that goal.

The first element of continuity is order: the right thing must come first if the second and third things are to be in the correct place and make sense. Correct sequence is important. If you are giving directions to a traveler and muddle the sequence of left and right turns, your friend will get lost. So it is with the sermon. An orderly sermon shows the hearers that you know where you are going and how you are getting there.

In determining the best order for your material, various considerations may affect your choice. Some examples of these are time— in a narrative, which event happened first; intensity— move from weaker points to stronger points; logic— first the problem, then the solution, or first the cause, then the results; and perspective— first the general background, then the particular details. Reverse the order and you end up preaching on an anticlimax .

Good order applies not just to the main divisions, but also to the subdivisions, the sequence of illustrations, supporting Scriptures, and arguments. The whole of the sermon must flow. Therefore, you must pay careful attention to your transitional sentences that bridge the gap between your points. The transition should be smooth and easy. Like mountain climbing, your path should be a gradual ascent, not a clumsy leap from one point to the next.

Continuity involves progression. You are not simply saying the same thing several different ways, you are moving on. Your argument is being developed, your case established, and your goal reached. Each point should naturally lead into the next and should carry your hearers onwards, irresistibly to the conclusion. You must therefore keep your aim clearly in mind as you prepare and deliver your sermon. You cannot afford to miss the mark by forgetting why you are preaching.

A great temptation for some preachers is to over-preach the first point and then allow the rest of the sermon to dribble out. A. J. Gossip told of the Scottish Highland preacher who announced his theme that he would prove: first, by the whole of the Bible; second, by all human history; and third, by the whole of human experience. Beginning with Adam, he reached Moses in his first point, lost heart, and sat down muttering, “and . . . and all the rest of them.” Essential to a good sermon outline is proportion. This gives a sense of balance to the message. If you are building up to a climax, your final points may even be longer than your first.

Do not spend so much time describing the problem that you have no time left to give the solution. As a young evangelist, I invited a former gang leader to testify to his salvation. For twenty minutes, he described in graphic detail his former life then as he turned to sit down he remembered something he had omitted. “Oh! By the way, Christ has saved me from all that now,” he declared, and that was it. He seemed more interested in talking about his sins than about his Savior. Too many evangelistic and ethical sermons fall into that same trap.

As a general rule, I take the time available for preaching and divide it by the number of main divisions in the sermon (the introduction and the conclusion together are as long as a main division). For example, thirty minutes for preaching four divisions plus the introduction and the conclusion give six minutes for each section. While I do not make the time rigid, it provides a helpful guide for my preparation and arrangement of material.

The basic idea of the outline is that it serves the sermon by making it easy for the hearers to understand the message. It makes no difference whether the main divisions are announced at once, at the end of the introduction, or as you come to each in turn, or are part of the flow of the sermon. What counts is that your hearers know where you are going and arrive there with you.

**Q:** Complete the assignment by filling in the two outlines on the following page. They will, of course, be different, but check to make sure the divisions form a single thought. The divisions should never develop different themes.

**A:** John 3:16, The Gift I. Who Gave?—God II. Why Did He Give?—Because He loved III. What Did He Give?—His only Son IV. To Whom Did He Give?—The world . . . whoever believes V. To What Purpose?—Shall not perish but have eternal life. John 3:16, Eternal Life I. The Source of Eternal Life—God II. The Provision of Eternal Life—Through His only Son III. The Condition of Eternal Life—Whoever believes in Him

**Q:** List the two steps that are necessary to create a harmonious and coherent sermon outline and explain their functions.

**A:** Analysis and synthesis. In analysis we break the text or theme up into main and subdivisions. In synthesis we deal with each division as a part of the whole, making sure the various parts, when put together, form one message.

**Q:** Define these terms as they relate to sermon outline continuity:

**A:** (1) Time— Explaining which events took place first (2) Intensity— Moving from the weaker points to the stronger points (3) Logic— Presenting the problem first, then the solution (4) Perspective— Giving the general background before the particular details

**Q:** 1) Time

**Q:** 2) Intensity

**Q:** 3) Logic

**Q:** 4) Perspective

**Q:** Suppose that you are to preach for forty minutes. Your sermon outline has three main divisions. How much time should you plan to spend on each main division?

**A:** Your answer should be approximately ten minutes for each division, which would allow you ten minutes for the introduction and conclusion.

#### 8.2.2 — Recognize the elements of continuity in a sermon outline.

In Chapter 4 we described the sermon as “the faithful exposition and application of Scripture to the needs of the hearers by the power of the Holy Spirit.” We see then that the sermon is not an essay. An essay is literature; it is meant to be read. Its style of writing and structure differ from the sermon’s. For example, the use of repetition is appropriate in a sermon but out of place in an essay. A major difference lies in the fact that the essay is intended to inform, interest, and entertain, whereas the sermon seeks a decisive response.

We also see that the sermon is different from the lecture. Although both are spoken, the lecture appeals mainly to the mind to inform and stimulate thought. The sermon appeals to the heart to stir it to action. The sermon’s ultimate goal is to move the will. To produce such a clear and lasting effect on the hearers, the sermon must have both unity and continuity.

Unity

In our sermon preparation, we began with the text from which we derived both the theme and the aim. For the outline to develop the text and theme in the light of the aim, there must be a unity of thought and structure about it.

A succession of unrelated or loosely related thoughts will fail to achieve the goal you are working towards. The outline must have coherence—that is, each part must have a logical, consistent, and intelligible relationship to what precedes and follows it.

To maintain unity, you must ensure that your divisions correspond to the nature and design of the subject. They must be part of the complete picture you are presenting. Martyn Lloyd-Jones told of an old Welsh preacher preparing a sermon on Numbers 22:21, “Balaam got up in the morning, [and] saddled his donkey.” His first division was “A Good Trait in a Bad Character” (he arose early); second, “An Antiquity of Saddlery” (he saddled his donkey); and third—every preacher must have three divisions—“A Few Remarks Concerning the Woman of Samaria.”

The two basic steps in obtaining a harmonious and coherent sermon structure are analysis and synthesis . In analysis we break the text or theme up into its constituent parts. Sometimes the text makes several statements about a central theme; at other times there are several themes that could each provide an outline. An example of this is John 3:16 where we find the Love of God, the Gift, and Eternal Life.

Study the three different themes outlined on the following page. I have filled in the main divisions of the first one as a suggested pattern. Please fill in the second two outlines; then compare your outlines with the ones given in the answer to Interactive Question 4.

John 3:16, The Love of God

I. The Scope —“God so loved the world”

II. The Measure —“He gave his one and only Son”

III. The Invitation —“Whosoever believes in him”

IV. The Provision —“Shall not perish but have eternal life”

John 3:16, The Gift

I.

II.

III.

IV.

V.

John 3:16, Eternal Life

I.

II.

III.

In our analysis of the text and chosen theme, we may find not only main divisions, but also subdivisions emerging under each main division (see Chapter 6 on Apologetic Preaching for an example). Main and subdivisions alike, however, must form a single thought. Analysis without synthesis may provide divisions that develop different themes. While each division should not say the same thing, each should deal with the various parts of the same thing so that when they are all put together they form one message.

Buttrick (1931) says “the mood of the sermon and often its very form and outline are found within the mood of the text. It is surely a prime necessity that the mood of the sermon should be congruous with the mood of the text” (153). If the text pleads, so should the sermon; if the text challenges, the sermon should also challenge; if the text reassures, so should the sermon. Each step of the outline should reinforce the dominant mood of the text to bring the hearers to a clear awareness of its message.

Continuity

Continuity is the orderly arrangement of your sermon material so that you begin at the beginning and end at the conclusion. There must be a beginning because you cannot say everything at once. There must be a conclusion in which you finish your message and not just stop speaking. The conclusion should correspond properly to your goal (Chapter 10). The outline of the main body of the message must, therefore, spell out step-by-step how you will reach that goal.

The first element of continuity is order: the right thing must come first if the second and third things are to be in the correct place and make sense. Correct sequence is important. If you are giving directions to a traveler and muddle the sequence of left and right turns, your friend will get lost. So it is with the sermon. An orderly sermon shows the hearers that you know where you are going and how you are getting there.

In determining the best order for your material, various considerations may affect your choice. Some examples of these are time— in a narrative, which event happened first; intensity— move from weaker points to stronger points; logic— first the problem, then the solution, or first the cause, then the results; and perspective— first the general background, then the particular details. Reverse the order and you end up preaching on an anticlimax .

Good order applies not just to the main divisions, but also to the subdivisions, the sequence of illustrations, supporting Scriptures, and arguments. The whole of the sermon must flow. Therefore, you must pay careful attention to your transitional sentences that bridge the gap between your points. The transition should be smooth and easy. Like mountain climbing, your path should be a gradual ascent, not a clumsy leap from one point to the next.

Continuity involves progression. You are not simply saying the same thing several different ways, you are moving on. Your argument is being developed, your case established, and your goal reached. Each point should naturally lead into the next and should carry your hearers onwards, irresistibly to the conclusion. You must therefore keep your aim clearly in mind as you prepare and deliver your sermon. You cannot afford to miss the mark by forgetting why you are preaching.

A great temptation for some preachers is to over-preach the first point and then allow the rest of the sermon to dribble out. A. J. Gossip told of the Scottish Highland preacher who announced his theme that he would prove: first, by the whole of the Bible; second, by all human history; and third, by the whole of human experience. Beginning with Adam, he reached Moses in his first point, lost heart, and sat down muttering, “and . . . and all the rest of them.” Essential to a good sermon outline is proportion. This gives a sense of balance to the message. If you are building up to a climax, your final points may even be longer than your first.

Do not spend so much time describing the problem that you have no time left to give the solution. As a young evangelist, I invited a former gang leader to testify to his salvation. For twenty minutes, he described in graphic detail his former life then as he turned to sit down he remembered something he had omitted. “Oh! By the way, Christ has saved me from all that now,” he declared, and that was it. He seemed more interested in talking about his sins than about his Savior. Too many evangelistic and ethical sermons fall into that same trap.

As a general rule, I take the time available for preaching and divide it by the number of main divisions in the sermon (the introduction and the conclusion together are as long as a main division). For example, thirty minutes for preaching four divisions plus the introduction and the conclusion give six minutes for each section. While I do not make the time rigid, it provides a helpful guide for my preparation and arrangement of material.

The basic idea of the outline is that it serves the sermon by making it easy for the hearers to understand the message. It makes no difference whether the main divisions are announced at once, at the end of the introduction, or as you come to each in turn, or are part of the flow of the sermon. What counts is that your hearers know where you are going and arrive there with you.

The Condition of Eternal Life—Whoever believes in Him

**A:** Analysis and synthesis. In analysis we break the text or theme up into main and subdivisions. In synthesis we deal with each division as a part of the whole, making sure the various parts, when put together, form one message.
