**A:** (1) Time— Explaining which events took place first (2) Intensity— Moving from the weaker points to the stronger points (3) Logic— Presenting the problem first, then the solution (4) Perspective— Giving the general background before the particular details

**Q:** 1) Time

**Q:** 2) Intensity

**Q:** 3) Logic

**Q:** 4) Perspective

**Q:** Suppose that you are to preach for forty minutes. Your sermon outline has three main divisions. How much time should you plan to spend on each main division?

**A:** Your answer should be approximately ten minutes for each division, which would allow you ten minutes for the introduction and conclusion.

### Dangers

#### 8.3.1 — Identify problem areas in the preparation of the outline.

Misplaced Priorities

Medieval sermons had to follow definite and carefully defined rules of construction. More effort was devoted to the outline than to the exposition of the text. Even today, some preachers are convinced that they must always have three points or that their division headings must all begin with the same letter, or end with the same sound ( alliteration ). To achieve their goal such preachers will devote time that could be more profitably spent on the content of the sermon or they will manipulate their text until it fits their pattern. The problem is misplaced priorities.

As memorable as alliteration may be when it arises naturally from the sermon material, any artificial device, especially one intended to impress your hearers with your cleverness, is to be avoided. The outline is intended to clarify the message, not glorify the messenger. To invent words or to distort their normal meaning so that you can use them in an alliterative scheme is improper and will confuse your hearers.

Monotony

Nothing will dull the keen edge of truth like monotony . When you announce your text or topic, no one should be able to say, “I know how he will handle that!” If you regularly use the same outline, your hearers will remember it and either mentally switch you off or mock your repetition. A fellow student informed me that he had found the perfect outline for a topical sermon. He was to preach on pride, and his outline was:

I. What Pride Is

II. What Pride Does

III. What Christ Can Do With Pride

The outline served well, but the next two years saw a succession of sermons on humility, love, peace, hope, joy, and sin, all with the same outline. Then came his final sermon before graduation. He announced his topic, faith, and with one voice the congregation recited, “What Faith Is, What Faith Does, What Christ Can Do With Faith.” And the sermon died, killed by repetition.

God loves variety. There is no monotony in creation. Each rock, each plant, each animal, and each person is different even though from the same general family. In the Bible, the Holy Spirit inspired each of the forty or so different authors. Yet, even where the message is the same you can tell Isaiah from Jeremiah or Paul from John. Jesus’ message to the Samaritan woman (John 4) is different from His words to the rich young ruler (Luke 18:18–22), even though He is talking about eternal life on both occasions.

By retaining the power of surprise, you will keep your hearers alert and interested. They will appreciate the effort you put into the preparation of your sermon, which will be apparent even in the outline you use.

To some preachers, the arrangement and structure of the sermon is an easy task because their thought patterns are orderly and structured. To others it is hard work. However, never feel that good, clear arrangement is impossible or a waste of time. With practice it becomes easier and it is always worthwhile. Our calling as preachers is to communicate the Word of God. If it does not come across, we have failed our hearers and our Master. Therefore, let us devote ourselves to this work and be approved as workers who do “not need to be ashamed and who correctly handle the word of truth” (2 Timothy 2:15).

**Q:** Identify the problem areas in the following statements that refer to outline preparation.

**A:** a) Limit the main divisions to three points. b) Begin each division heading with the same letter. c) Arrange every sermon outline in the same format. a) Although three main divisions are an ideal, there may be times when the text or topic would need to vary from this number. It is important to focus on the content rather than the number of divisions. b) Alliteration is a useful tool. However, it should not be the determining pattern, or it may dictate the outline rather than the sermon text and content. c) If the sermon format is always the same, the hearers will soon find the sermons monotonous. Make sure that the outlines offer creativity and variety. 9 CHAPTER

## 9 - The Introduction

### The Purposes

#### 9.1.1 — Explain the purposes of the introduction.

Having carefully prepared and arranged the outline and body of your sermon, you are now ready to face the matter of bringing that sermon before your hearers. All the work invested and the potential you know to be there will be lost if your introduction fails to bridge the gap between the mind of the preacher and the mind of the hearer. Your goal in the introduction is to interest your hearers in your subject, to establish its relevance to them, and to prepare them for what is to follow.

To Interest

One of the basic rules of radio and television work is that the opening moments of a program will determine whether the listener will stay tuned in or switch it off. No matter how good the material that follows, you cannot regain the listener who is no longer receiving your signal. It is essential that you make the listener want to stay with you.

So it is with preaching. Your hearers may still be there physically, but mentally they may have switched over to think about something else. They may have come from a variety of situations and may not have been thinking about anything remotely connected with the sermon topic. All that has taken place earlier in the service may have failed to properly prepare for the sermon. Some who take part in the service may selfishly distract the congregation from the sermon rather than lead into it. You must ensure that your hearers are with you before you begin the body of the sermon.

Most congregations will, out of courtesy, give you some attention when you first begin to speak. You can waste those precious moments by making flattering remarks about some of your hearers or entertaining them with some irrelevant, humorous stories, or you can use them wisely to awaken your hearers’ interest in your message. If you gain their interest at the beginning, there is every possibility of keeping it to the end.

As distressing as Paul found the idolatry in Athens, he began his sermon to the Areopagus by declaring, “Men of Athens! I see that in every way you are very religious” (Acts 17:22). Rather than launching an attack on their pagan worship, he courteously used those opening moments to focus their interest on his topic.

To Establish

Since the needs of the hearers are the focus of exposition and application of Scripture (Chapter 4), you must make your hearers aware of how your sermon applies to them. Like King Zedekiah, they have come asking, “Is there any word from the Lord?” (Jeremiah 37:17). They need to know that this word from God is relevant to their situation. Exposition without application may be fascinating, but few have the education or training to understand what difference hearing this particular sermon will make to their lives.

Although you must avoid preaching your conclusion until you have expounded your message, your hearers need to know why you are preaching on this subject. If you cannot establish relevance at this point, your hearers may give you the benefit of the doubt and still listen, believing that it will all be made plain eventually. In their hearts, however, they will feel that the preacher has not done his or her preparation thoroughly.

Billy Graham was a master at using some topic of current concern as an introduction to a Bible exposition. He establishes contact from the opening sentences because his hearers know where he is coming from, and he knows where he is taking them. The route and goal of the sermon may not be apparent at the start, but there is a meeting of minds at the commencement.

There is never any need to apologize for your sermon. Establish its relevance at the outset, and your hearers will gladly hear you out.

To Prepare

The introduction is the introduction to a topic: it is the means to an end and not an end in itself. An old Methodist preacher described his sermon structure in this way: “First, I tell them what I am going to tell them; then I tell them; then I tell them what I told them!” While the introduction should prepare the hearers’ minds for what follows, you should avoid preaching a shortened version of your sermon and thereby weakening the impact of your message.

You have spent time preparing your sermon; you have a burden to share this subject; your hearers need to have their minds opened to your subject if they are to share your burden. The key word is explanation . You cannot afford to assume that your hearers already have all the background information necessary to understand your sermon.

A skillful introduction can make your hearers not only think about your subject, but also think about it from the perspective you desire. If you were to talk about power, you would need to make it clear to your hearers whether you are speaking of physical strength, authority, ability, or effectiveness. If you and your hearers begin together, you are more likely to end together.

**Q:** Review Acts 17:16–23, and summarize the purpose of Paul’s introduction to his sermon.

**A:** Paul’s words gained the attention and interest of his hearers.

**Q:** Why were his words of introduction important?

**A:** Paul focused on the needs of the people. He did not want to offend them or lose their attention, so his sermon began with a positive statement.

**Q:** Suppose that you are preparing the introduction for a sermon on the topic of love. Explain how you will establish and prepare the hearers for your sermon that will follow. Why are these steps important?

**A:** Your answer should include establishing the relevance of love. It is important to let the congregation know why you are preaching on the subject so that they will want to listen to your entire sermon. Also, give the hearers a clear idea of what you are presenting in the sermon. Your explanation of love will prepare their minds for the sermon that will follow.

**Q:** What areas are important to remember when selecting appropriate sermon titles to use in an introduction?

**A:** Choose a title that will appeal to the imagination and cause the hearer to want to listen to the entire sermon. A title should be simple rather than long and complex. It should be easy to remember. It should not be deceptive, alarming, or negative.

**Q:** For each of the following examples of subjects, choose two or three sermon titles that can be used effectively in the sermon introduction: (a) forgiveness; (b) peace; (c) spiritual gifts; and (d) Christian service.

**A:** Your answer. Here are some examples: (a) Living is Forgiving; (b) Peace for a World at War; (c) Receiving Spiritual Gifts; (d) A Servant’s Heart and Hands

### The Method

#### 9.2.1 — Identify sermon titles that can effectively be used in a sermon introduction.

The Title

It is always wise to give your sermon a title even if you do not intend to announce it to your hearers. It may simply state your subject: ”The Love of God”; or it may specify a particular aspect of the subject: “How Can a Good God Love Bad People?” It may serve to arouse interest in the subject, such as “Good News for God’s Enemies.” A good title can seize your hearers’ imagination and help introduce the theme of your sermon.

A simple title is better than a complex one. As with division headings, the longer the title the harder it is to remember. Even when the sermon itself is forgotten, the stark simplicity of a title such as “Ten Timeless Truths” can be remembered for many years.

As the title is properly part of the introduction and not just an advertising gimmick, it should prepare for what is to follow. Although exciting titles may arouse interest and puzzling titles may stimulate curiosity, if the sermon does not live up to the title, the hearers will feel that they have been tricked. Deceptive or alarming titles, such as “The Greatest Liar in This Church” (John 8:44) or “How to Commit Adultery” (Matthew 5:27–32), quickly lose their impact and even have a negative effect. Your goal is not just to draw a crowd, but to lead your hearers to Christ.

The Topic

Having gained your hearers’ attention with the title, you must carefully select your opening sentences in order to lead them into the sermon. Most preachers write these out in full even if the remainder of the sermon is prepared only in outline form. As it is only possible to make a first impression once, so you can only introduce your sermon once. If it is not done right the first time, much of your time will be wasted trying to undo the initial poor impression.

When the introduction is over, the hearers should have a clear idea of your subject. Everything you say must revolve around that. Eliminate all irrelevant and unimportant matters lest they distract your hearers from the topic of the sermon. Give greetings and words of appreciation earlier in the service, and save the introduction for the sermon topic.

How the topic is presented depends upon several factors: the text, the aim of the sermon, the structural type, and the psychological method used. Many texts require some knowledge of their setting; therefore, it would be appropriate to sketch in the background. To be done properly, it must be done interestingly, using short phrases that help your hearers get the feel of the situation. Include only as much detail as is absolutely necessary. Your intention is to establish your text’s relevance to your hearers, not to show how much you know about the Bible or ancient history.

The ethical sermon could be introduced by an illustration highlighting the topic of the sermon, perhaps posing the question you intend to answer. If the psychological method is cooperative, such as the preacher and hearers together seeking the answers to questions, you would not want to give the answer at the beginning. You would simply raise the question you and your hearers will consider.

However you introduce your sermon, your hearers should have no doubt about the topic by the end of the introduction.

The Text

It used to be customary for preachers to read the text out before beginning to preach. The sheer monotony of “My text this morning is . . . and my title is . . .” probably did more to send the church to sleep than anything else.

By making the announcement of the text the climax of the introduction, you are using the best possible moment when your hearers are fully prepared for the Word of God. The text is introduced naturally and leads directly into the exposition. As an example, I introduced my sermon on John 3:16, “The Gift,” (see Chapter 8) in this way:

A minister wrote to a businessman asking for a donation towards the new church building. The businessman responded, “Give, give, give! This Christianity is all give!” “Thank you,” replied the minister, “for the best definition of Christianity I have heard. Giving begins with God: ‘For God so loved the world that he gave his one and only Son, that whoever believes in him shall not perish but have eternal life’ (John 3:16). Who gave? God did!”

Although it may not always be possible to find an illustration in which your sermon text actually appears, a few well-chosen words can tie the text into your introduction.

Whether you have included your text in a formal Scripture reading earlier in the meeting or not, you will find it helpful to have your hearers open their Bibles to the chapter in which your text is found. This will reduce distractions at the crucial point of transition from the introduction to the first main division. Whenever you announce a text reference, it is wise to remember that lesser known books of the Bible are often difficult to find. I usually give the reference, make a few comments about the portion, and then repeat the reference to give opportunity for everyone to find it.

Sometimes the text itself is all you need for the introduction, and the first main division arises naturally out of the text. An example of this is a sermon on 2 Corinthians 12:9 that is entitled, “Enough.” You could begin with the Scripture reading from 2 Corinthians 12:1 and end with verse 9: “But he said to me, ‘My grace is sufficient for you.’” Is it? Is God’s grace enough? Look at whose grace it is.

1. It Is Divine—“My”

2. It Is Undeserved—“Grace”

3. It Is Unlimited—“Sufficient”

4. It Is Personal—“For You”

By interrupting the Scripture reading at the crucial point, you focus the attention of the hearers on the last words you read—your text.

**Q:** How will the opening sentences of the introduction affect the entire sermon, and how can you make them effective?

**A:** The opening lines are the most important part of the sermon. You must concentrate on engaging the minds of the hearers at this time so that they will listen throughout the sermon. Your IST author suggests that you write these lines out, including only what is necessary and pertinent to presenting the topic.

**Q:** When is the best time to give greetings and words of appreciation?

**A:** Use the sermon introduction exclusively for the presentation of the topic. Give all other greetings, announcements, and words of appreciation earlier in the service.

**Q:** What factors determine how to present the topic, and why is it important to consider them before planning the introduction?

**A:** The text, the aim of the sermon, the structural type, and the psychological method are the key factors in determining how to introduce the sermon topic. These factors are the key to establishing the text’s relevance to the hearers. Sometimes a short sketch of the biblical background is appropriate during the introduction. Depending on the type of sermon, an illustration or question may help to introduce the sermon topic.

**Q:** Describe the importance of the timing and placement of the text in the sermon introduction.

**A:** When the text is read at the beginning of the introduction, it may seem monotonous to those listening. However, if it is read at the end of the introduction, it naturally leads into the body of the sermon. Sometimes an appropriate illustration can lead into the text, gaining the full attention of the hearers. Regardless, it is important to use well-chosen words of introduction that will easily flow into the words of the Scripture text.

**Q:** Write down some of the things that you will want to remember from this section regarding the practical aspects of reading the Scripture text.

**A:** When you plan the church service, you may want to use the suggestion of reading the Scripture text earlier in the service, so that people are prepared for the sermon introduction. It is helpful to have the listeners look up the Scripture text before you actually begin the discussion of the outline. Remember as well to allow extra time for finding lesser-known books of the Bible, and always repeat the reference so that everyone can follow the reading. 10 CHAPTER

#### 9.2.2 — Explain how to present the sermon topic in the introduction.

The Title

It is always wise to give your sermon a title even if you do not intend to announce it to your hearers. The Text

The text is introduced naturally and leads directly into the exposition. As an example, I introduced my sermon on John 3:16, “The Gift,” (see Chapter 8) in this way:

A minister wrote to a businessman asking for a donation towards the new church building. The businessman responded, “Give, give, give! This Christianity is all give!” “Thank you,” replied the minister, “for the best definition of Christianity I have heard. Giving begins with God: ‘For God so loved the world that he gave his one and only Son, that whoever believes in him shall not perish but have eternal life’ (John 3:16). Who gave? God did!”

Although it may not always be possible to find an illustration in which your sermon text actually appears, a few well-chosen words can tie the text into your introduction.

Whether you have included your text in a formal Scripture reading earlier in the meeting or not, you will find it helpful to have your hearers open their Bibles to the chapter in which your text is found. This will reduce distractions at the crucial point of transition from the introduction to the first main division. Whenever you announce a text reference, it is wise to remember that lesser known books of the Bible are often difficult to find. I usually give the reference, make a few comments about the portion, and then repeat the reference to give opportunity for everyone to find it.

Sometimes the text itself is all you need for the introduction, and the first main division arises naturally out of the text. An example of this is a sermon on 2 Corinthians 12:9 that is entitled, “Enough.” You could begin with the Scripture reading from 2 Corinthians 12:1 and end with verse 9: “But he said to me, ‘My grace is sufficient for you.’” Is it? Is God’s grace enough? Look at whose grace it is.

1. It Is Divine—“My”

2. It Is Undeserved—“Grace”

3. It Is Unlimited—“Sufficient”

4. It Is Personal—“For You”

By interrupting the Scripture reading at the crucial point, you focus the attention of the hearers on the last words you read—your text.

**Q:** How will the opening sentences of the introduction affect the entire sermon, and how can you make them effective?

**A:** The opening lines are the most important part of the sermon. You must concentrate on engaging the minds of the hearers at this time so that they will listen throughout the sermon. Your IST author suggests that you write these lines out, including only what is necessary and pertinent to presenting the topic.

**Q:** When is the best time to give greetings and words of appreciation?

**A:** Use the sermon introduction exclusively for the presentation of the topic. Give all other greetings, announcements, and words of appreciation earlier in the service.

**Q:** What factors determine how to present the topic, and why is it important to consider them before planning the introduction?

**A:** The text, the aim of the sermon, the structural type, and the psychological method are the key factors in determining how to introduce the sermon topic. These factors are the key to establishing the text’s relevance to the hearers. Sometimes a short sketch of the biblical background is appropriate during the introduction. Depending on the type of sermon, an illustration or question may help to introduce the sermon topic.

**Q:** Describe the importance of the timing and placement of the text in the sermon introduction.

**A:** When the text is read at the beginning of the introduction, it may seem monotonous to those listening. However, if it is read at the end of the introduction, it naturally leads into the body of the sermon. Sometimes an appropriate illustration can lead into the text, gaining the full attention of the hearers. Regardless, it is important to use well-chosen words of introduction that will easily flow into the words of the Scripture text.

**Q:** Write down some of the things that you will want to remember from this section regarding the practical aspects of reading the Scripture text.

**A:** When you plan the church service, you may want to use the suggestion of reading the Scripture text earlier in the service, so that people are prepared for the sermon introduction. It is helpful to have the listeners look up the Scripture text before you actually begin the discussion of the outline. Remember as well to allow extra time for finding lesser-known books of the Bible, and always repeat the reference so that everyone can follow the reading. 10 CHAPTER

#### 9.2.3 — Describe the importance of the timing and placement of the text in the sermon introduction.

The Title

It is always wise to give your sermon a title even if you do not intend to announce it to your hearers. It may simply state your subject: ”The Love of God”; or it may specify a particular aspect of the subject: “How Can a Good God Love Bad People?” It may serve to arouse interest in the subject, such as “Good News for God’s Enemies.” A good title can seize your hearers’ imagination and help introduce the theme of your sermon.

A simple title is better than a complex one. As with division headings, the longer the title the harder it is to remember. Even when the sermon itself is forgotten, the stark simplicity of a title such as “Ten Timeless Truths” can be remembered for many years.

As the title is properly part of the introduction and not just an advertising gimmick, it should prepare for what is to follow. Although exciting titles may arouse interest and puzzling titles may stimulate curiosity, if the sermon does not live up to the title, the hearers will feel that they have been tricked. Deceptive or alarming titles, such as “The Greatest Liar in This Church” (John 8:44) or “How to Commit Adultery” (Matthew 5:27–32), quickly lose their impact and even have a negative effect. Your goal is not just to draw a crowd, but to lead your hearers to Christ.

The Topic

Having gained your hearers’ attention with the title, you must carefully select your opening sentences in order to lead them into the sermon. Most preachers write these out in full even if the remainder of the sermon is prepared only in outline form. As it is only possible to make a first impression once, so you can only introduce your sermon once. If it is not done right the first time, much of your time will be wasted trying to undo the initial poor impression.

When the introduction is over, the hearers should have a clear idea of your subject. Everything you say must revolve around that. Eliminate all irrelevant and unimportant matters lest they distract your hearers from the topic of the sermon. Give greetings and words of appreciation earlier in the service, and save the introduction for the sermon topic.

How the topic is presented depends upon several factors: the text, the aim of the sermon, the structural type, and the psychological method used. Many texts require some knowledge of their setting; therefore, it would be appropriate to sketch in the background. To be done properly, it must be done interestingly, using short phrases that help your hearers get the feel of the situation. Include only as much detail as is absolutely necessary. Your intention is to establish your text’s relevance to your hearers, not to show how much you know about the Bible or ancient history.

The ethical sermon could be introduced by an illustration highlighting the topic of the sermon, perhaps posing the question you intend to answer. If the psychological method is cooperative, such as the preacher and hearers together seeking the answers to questions, you would not want to give the answer at the beginning. You would simply raise the question you and your hearers will consider.

However you introduce your sermon, your hearers should have no doubt about the topic by the end of the introduction.

The Text

It used to be customary for preachers to read the text out before beginning to preach. The sheer monotony of “My text this morning is . . . and my title is . . .” probably did more to send the church to sleep than anything else.

By making the announcement of the text the climax of the introduction, you are using the best possible moment when your hearers are fully prepared for the Word of God. The text is introduced naturally and leads directly into the exposition. As an example, I introduced my sermon on John 3:16, “The Gift,” (see Chapter 8) in this way:

A minister wrote to a businessman asking for a donation towards the new church building. The businessman responded, “Give, give, give! This Christianity is all give!” “Thank you,” replied the minister, “for the best definition of Christianity I have heard. Giving begins with God: ‘For God so loved the world that he gave his one and only Son, that whoever believes in him shall not perish but have eternal life’ (John 3:16). Who gave? God did!”

Although it may not always be possible to find an illustration in which your sermon text actually appears, a few well-chosen words can tie the text into your introduction.

Whether you have included your text in a formal Scripture reading earlier in the meeting or not, you will find it helpful to have your hearers open their Bibles to the chapter in which your text is found. This will reduce distractions at the crucial point of transition from the introduction to the first main division. Whenever you announce a text reference, it is wise to remember that lesser known books of the Bible are often difficult to find. I usually give the reference, make a few comments about the portion, and then repeat the reference to give opportunity for everyone to find it.

Sometimes the text itself is all you need for the introduction, and the first main division arises naturally out of the text. An example of this is a sermon on 2 Corinthians 12:9 that is entitled, “Enough.” You could begin with the Scripture reading from 2 Corinthians 12:1 and end with verse 9: “But he said to me, ‘My grace is sufficient for you.’” Is it? Is God’s grace enough? Look at whose grace it is.

1. It Is Divine—“My”

2. It Is Undeserved—“Grace”

3. It Is Unlimited—“Sufficient”

4. It Is Personal—“For You”

By interrupting the Scripture reading at the crucial point, you focus the attention of the hearers on the last words you read—your text.

**A:** The opening lines are the most important part of the sermon. You must concentrate on engaging the minds of the hearers at this time so that they will listen throughout the sermon. Your IST author suggests that you write these lines out, including only what is necessary and pertinent to presenting the topic.

It is helpful to have the listeners look up the Scripture text before you actually begin the discussion of the outline. Remember as well to allow extra time for finding lesser-known books of the Bible, and always repeat the reference so that everyone can follow the reading. 10 CHAPTER

### The Principles

#### 9.3.1 — Identify the basic principles of the sermon introduction.

Simplicity

If there is ever a place for complexity in a sermon, it is certainly not in the introduction. If you lose your hearers here, you will find it almost impossible to regain them. The introduction requires plain, uncomplicated language.

Your purpose is to lead your hearers into the body of the sermon, not dazzle them with your brilliance. Anything that distracts from what is to follow should be eliminated. The introduction is not the climax of the sermon; rather, it represents the first steps towards the climax that properly belongs to the conclusion (Chapter 10). It is wise, therefore, to reserve the spectacular displays of noise and color for later in the sermon. If you begin too loudly or too strongly, you will find it difficult to go on from there. Everything that follows will seem like an anticlimax.

Congruity

The introduction must fit in with the rest of the sermon both in mood and in scope. If you are preaching on a serious matter, it would be inappropriate to begin in a light and frivolous way. If you are going to use humor at all, it should be of the thought-provoking kind. Your goal is not just to gain the attention of your hearers, but also to prepare them for the main thrust of the message.

The use of shock tactics quickly loses its effectiveness and may even have an opposite result. A zealous young preacher is supposed to have rushed to the pulpit of his church yelling, “Fire! Fire! Hellfire and brimstone!” His sermon on the fires of hell was lost on his panic-stricken hearers who fled toward the exits of the church.

Sangster (1951) told of the small house near his church that had an enormous, ornate porch. The entrance was totally inappropriate (124). It is like preparing the table for a feast and serving bread and water. If the introduction promises more than the rest of the sermon will deliver, the hearers will feel cheated. Probably the solution is to improve the rest of the sermon rather than weaken the introduction.

There is no such thing as a general, all-purpose, all-occasion introduction. Each sermon needs its own introduction, which is carefully and suitably fashioned. By preparing the body of the sermon first, you can easily check the temptation to manufacture a sermon to fit an introduction.

Brevity

The length of the introduction should be in keeping with the rest of the sermon. As a general rule the introduction should be as brief as possible and certainly no longer than the shortest of the main divisions. Some preachers are like the lady who took so long preparing the table for the meal that her family decided to eat at a nearby restaurant.

Sometimes the briefest introductions are the most memorable and the most effective, as when Sparhawk Jones preached at Princeton University Chapel on the text, “Is thy servant a dog, that he should do this great thing?” (Sangster 1951, 135). After reading his text, he introduced his sermon with, “Dog or no dog, he did it!” That was enough to fasten his hearers’ attention on a masterly sermon on being true to one’s principles.

If no introduction is necessary, then do not force one on just because it is usual to have one. Your first main division may outline a problem to which you will propose a solution, and therefore, no previous comment may be needed. Once more, the sermon determines the introduction.

Variety

There is no need for a tiresome monotony in the way you introduce your sermons. In the past, most preachers would announce the title, text, and theme, then sketch in the background of the text before expounding it. Too often the contextual background was simply a rewording of the Bible reading. Later it became fashionable to begin with an illustration or a description of some current situation in order to be more relevant to the hearers.

No one method is divinely anointed above all others. Whatever is appropriate to the particular sermon is the right way to introduce that sermon. You may begin with the historical background, the immediate context of the text, a current life situation, an illustrative story, a quotation, a question, refer to the occasion of the sermon, the way in which God brought the message to your heart, or any combination of these.

The key is to be fresh. Be relevant. Be yourself. Because a particular introduction worked well for another preacher does not mean that you should copy it. Evangelist Aimee McPherson was famous for her “illustrated sermons” (Epstein 1993, 252–259). One Easter at Angelus Temple, Los Angeles, she came on the platform concealed in a large, paper Easter egg and burst through the top clad in an Easter bunny costume.

Whatever it takes to communicate the message effectively should be our prime consideration. From the first word we utter, we should make contact with our hearers, and as rapidly and clearly as possible lead them into the message God has given us. We cannot afford to be careless or hope for a sudden flash of inspiration. We must know how God wants us to begin and plunge straight in.

Begin right, and you can end right.

## 10 - The Conclusion

### The Purpose

#### 10.1.1 — Describe the relationship between the aim of the sermon and the conclusion.

The conclusion could be described as the most important part of the sermon, partly because many in the congregation will be eagerly looking forward to it, partly because it marks the climax of the message being delivered, and partly because the results of the sermon are usually decided by it. It is possible to lose all the hard work of the introduction and exposition with a weak or inappropriate conclusion. Therefore, it is essential that we know how the sermon will end.

If we compare the sermon to a journey, the text corresponds to our starting point and the aim to our destination. We then plan the route we must take, noting the roads to be followed; these are the main and subdivisions of the outline. The introduction traces the path from our start to the first main road, and the conclusion takes us from the last major road to our actual destination.

Once, when traveling to a nearby town for an evangelistic crusade, I failed to get precise directions as to the location of the church. I thought I would easily be able to find the church when I arrived in the town, but no one seemed to know of it, and I was lost. Fortunately, I was able to telephone the pastor for those essential directions, and I discovered that I had passed close by the church when I had first arrived in the town. You cannot afford to get lost so close to your destination when preaching. Those final steps are vital to a safe and timely arrival at your intended goal.

To Conclude

If the sermon is to reach its destination, you must do more than just stop traveling; you must arrive. When the sermon ends, there should be a sense of completeness. That which was to be said, has been said. The message from God has been delivered in its entirety, and each hearer must now respond to that message.

Whatever topics you have brought to the hearers’ attention in the course of the sermon must be resolved if you are to achieve a proper conclusion. To raise topics that will not, or cannot, be satisfactorily dealt with is a great mistake. If you were assembling a bicycle from a kit, you would feel uneasy if you discovered several parts left over when you had finished. You would wonder whether your bicycle was safe or whether it would suddenly collapse. You would check and recheck, maybe even rebuild it until every part fitted. So it is with the sermon. Every part of the sermon must fit together so that in the conclusion you present the finished message with nothing left over.

The conclusion is much like the closing argument of the lawyer in a trial. Lawyers draw together all the strands of evidence to ensure that nothing is omitted that would create doubt or uncertainty regarding the intended conclusion. They will use every shred of evidence and every element of persuasion they can to ensure that the verdict given is the one they desire. In the conclusion, they leave their hearers with no doubt regarding the desired response.

It is the same with the preacher. It is not enough to merely present the facts and leave it to the hearers to draw their own conclusions from those facts. The preacher must direct the hearers to the appropriate conclusion. Paul summarized his ministry, “We try to persuade men” (2 Corinthians 5:11). We do not just preach for a verdict, but like Paul, we preach for a verdict in favor of the message we proclaim. Our great commission is to “make disciples of all nations” (Matthew 28:19). Our conclusion should leave our hearers with no doubt that this is our goal every time we preach.

To Apply

While riding through the run-down area of a large city, a wealthy businessman remarked to his friend, “Do you see all the crime and violence in this area? Ministers like you have been preaching gospel sermons for nineteen hundred years, yet there are still evil people around. Why do you continue such a futile practice?” Just then they passed a group of vagrants sitting and drinking around a fire. “Do you see those tramps over there?” asked the preacher. “You make soap, and yet there are still dirty people around. Why do you continue to sell such a worthless product?” “The problem is not with the soap,” answered the businessman, “but with the people not applying it!” As with your soap, so with the sermon. Without application it cannot work.

Too many excellent expositions of biblical truth fail at this very point: they are not applied. No matter how brilliant the argument, how flawless the logic, how penetrating the insights, how gripping the illustrations, how fervent the delivery—unless the hearer recognizes in his or her heart, “This I must do! This I must achieve!” the sermon fails.

It is not enough to present the message as theory; preachers must clearly demonstrate to their hearers’ minds how it works out in practice. It is not surprising that some reject the Christian message simply because they do not see what difference it makes to their daily lives. Jesus did not say, “I am the idea and the theory.” He said, “I am the way and the truth and the life” (John 14:6). Without truth at the center, the way may be misleading and the life impossible. Without the way and the life, truth goes nowhere and accomplishes nothing.

The hearer asks, “What difference does it make?” It is the preachers’ responsibility to show their hearers how to apply the Word of God to life. No one should ever leave our meetings without a clear understanding of what to do with the message we have proclaimed to them.

To Stimulate

The task of preaching does not end when the preacher has presented the message and applied it to the hearers. The goal is to stimulate the hearers to a wholehearted response to the message. The preacher is commissioned not just to preach and to teach, but also to “make disciples” (Matthew 28:19). His or her message is a call to action. Thus Paul described his ministry as “to call people from among all the Gentiles to the obedience that comes from faith” (Romans 1:5).

Sometimes referred to as the appeal, the altar call, the exhortation, or the evangelistic invitation, this aspect of the sermon has been questioned as unbiblical by some who see it as an attempt to take over the work of Christ or of the Holy Spirit. Both of them are called Paraclete, the One who summons people to repentance and faith (John 16:7–11; 1 John 2:1–2). Paul, however, reminded his readers, “He has committed to us the message of reconciliation. We are therefore Christ’s ambassadors, as though God were making his appeal [ parakaleo, the verb from paraclete ] through us. We implore you on Christ’s behalf: Be reconciled to God” (2 Corinthians 5:19–20). Not only is God appealing through the preacher, but “as God’s fellow workers we urge you [ parakaleo ]” (2 Corinthians 6:1).

The terms of this evangelistic appeal are clearly demonstrated at the conclusion of Peter’s sermon on the Day of Pentecost:

When the people heard this, they were cut to the heart and said to Peter and the other apostles, ‘Brothers, what shall we do?’ Peter replied, ‘Repent and be baptized, every one of you. . . .’ With many other words he warned them; and he pleaded [ parakaleo ] with them, ‘Save yourselves from this corrupt generation.’ Those who accepted his message were baptized. (Acts 2:37–41)

First, the appeal was based upon a proclaimed message. Without a valid proclamation of the truth there can be no valid response. Paul thus exhorted young Timothy, “Preach the Word . . . correct, rebuke and encourage [ parakaleo ] — with great patience and careful instruction” (2 Timothy 4:2). Similarly, among the qualifications of the pastor is the ability to “hold firmly to the trustworthy message as it has been taught, so that he or she can encourage [ parakaleo ] others by sound doctrine and refute those who oppose it” (Titus 1:9). Faithful proclamation provides a sound basis for the evangelistic invitation. It is foolish to expect hearers to respond without telling them what they are responding to.

Second, the appeal answered the basic question: “What shall we do?” Billy Graham actually states the question for his hearers, using it as a transition from the exposition into the invitation. Whether publicly stated or not, the question must underlie the wording of the appeal. Our hearers have a right to know what they are expected to do with the truth we have proclaimed to them.

Third, the appeal then translated the response into positive action: “Repent and be baptized.” Although we will deal with this later in the section, we must recognize the need for action that is appropriate both to the sermon and the hearer.

Fourth, opportunity was given for the hearers to respond there and then: “Those who accepted the message were baptized.” The great nineteenth-century evangelist D. L. Moody’s preaching was transformed following October 8, 1871. That day, at the conclusion of a powerful evangelistic sermon, he neglected to give his hearers an opportunity to respond to Christ. Many in his congregation did not come the next Sunday. The great fire of Chicago swept across the city, destroying the Illinois Street Church, the homes, and even the lives of more than one-third of his congregation. Moody resolved never to preach again without urging his hearers to quickly respond to the message (Dorsett 1997, 152).

In the conclusion, then, the preacher calls upon the hearers to take immediate and appropriate action in response to the truth presented. He or she is preaching for a personal commitment.

**Q:** Why is it important for the preacher to know how the sermon will end?

**A:** It is the conclusion of the sermon that determines the results of the message. The hearers are waiting for the climax and a time of decision.

**Q:** What is the relationship between the aim of the sermon and the conclusion?

**A:** Just as the text corresponds to the introduction, the aim points to the conclusion. The goal of the conclusion, therefore, is to establish the aim in the minds of the hearers.

**Q:** How does the sermon compare to a lawyer’s closing remarks at a trial?

**A:** It should be clear and persuasive.

**Q:** Give a practical illustration of application. Show how it is possible to hear the truth and yet not apply it.

**A:** Your answer. One example is a person who has studied in a book the techniques of driving but has never actually driven. The rules of driving will not help this person until he or she gets behind the wheel and drives.

**Q:** According to John 16:7–11 and 2 Corinthians 6:1–2, how does the Holy Spirit appeal? What is the preacher’s responsibility in the appeal?

**A:** The Holy Spirit will convict people of sin. However, it is the responsibility of the preacher to give an appeal, to urge and persuade.

**Q:** Based on Acts 2:37–41, what question should the appeal answer in the mind of the unbeliever? What is the desired response?

**A:** The appeal should answer the question, “What shall we do?” The desired response is positive action—to repent and be baptized.

Not only is God appealing through the preacher, but “as God’s fellow workers we urge you [ parakaleo ]” (2 Corinthians 6:1).

The terms of this evangelistic appeal are clearly demonstrated at the conclusion of Peter’s sermon on the Day of Pentecost:

When the people heard this, they were cut to the heart and said to Peter and the other apostles, ‘Brothers, what shall we do?’ Peter replied, ‘Repent and be baptized, every one of you. . . .’ With many other words he warned them; and he pleaded [ parakaleo ] with them, ‘Save yourselves from this corrupt generation.’ Those who accepted his message were baptized. (Acts 2:37–41)

First, the appeal was based upon a proclaimed message. Without a valid proclamation of the truth there can be no valid response. Paul thus exhorted young Timothy, “Preach the Word . . . correct, rebuke and encourage [ parakaleo ] — with great patience and careful instruction” (2 Timothy 4:2). Similarly, among the qualifications of the pastor is the ability to “hold firmly to the trustworthy message as it has been taught, so that he or she can encourage [ parakaleo ] others by sound doctrine and refute those who oppose it” (Titus 1:9). Faithful proclamation provides a sound basis for the evangelistic invitation. It is foolish to expect hearers to respond without telling them what they are responding to.

Second, the appeal answered the basic question: “What shall we do?” Billy Graham actually states the question for his hearers, using it as a transition from the exposition into the invitation. Whether publicly stated or not, the question must underlie the wording of the appeal. Our hearers have a right to know what they are expected to do with the truth we have proclaimed to them.

Third, the appeal then translated the response into positive action: “Repent and be baptized.” Although we will deal with this later in the section, we must recognize the need for action that is appropriate both to the sermon and the hearer.

Fourth, opportunity was given for the hearers to respond there and then: “Those who accepted the message were baptized.” The great nineteenth-century evangelist D. L. Moody’s preaching was transformed following October 8, 1871. That day, at the conclusion of a powerful evangelistic sermon, he neglected to give his hearers an opportunity to respond to Christ. Many in his congregation did not come the next Sunday. The great fire of Chicago swept across the city, destroying the Illinois Street Church, the homes, and even the lives of more than one-third of his congregation. Moody resolved never to preach again without urging his hearers to quickly respond to the message (Dorsett 1997, 152).

In the conclusion, then, the preacher calls upon the hearers to take immediate and appropriate action in response to the truth presented. He or she is preaching for a personal commitment.

**Q:** Why is it important for the preacher to know how the sermon will end?

**A:** It is the conclusion of the sermon that determines the results of the message. The hearers are waiting for the climax and a time of decision.

**Q:** What is the relationship between the aim of the sermon and the conclusion?

**A:** Just as the text corresponds to the introduction, the aim points to the conclusion. The goal of the conclusion, therefore, is to establish the aim in the minds of the hearers.

**Q:** How does the sermon compare to a lawyer’s closing remarks at a trial?

**A:** It should be clear and persuasive.

**Q:** Give a practical illustration of application. Show how it is possible to hear the truth and yet not apply it.

**A:** Your answer. One example is a person who has studied in a book the techniques of driving but has never actually driven. The rules of driving will not help this person until he or she gets behind the wheel and drives.

**Q:** According to John 16:7–11 and 2 Corinthians 6:1–2, how does the Holy Spirit appeal? What is the preacher’s responsibility in the appeal?

**A:** The Holy Spirit will convict people of sin. However, it is the responsibility of the preacher to give an appeal, to urge and persuade.

**Q:** Based on Acts 2:37–41, what question should the appeal answer in the mind of the unbeliever? What is the desired response?

**A:** The appeal should answer the question, “What shall we do?” The desired response is positive action—to repent and be baptized.

### The Methods

#### 10.2.1 — Describe the correct use of summary as a method of conclusion.

Every preacher knows that sooner or later he or she must stop speaking. The airplane pilot circling the airport must choose either to keep going until the gas tank is empty and the plane crashes, or to come down to earth safely in good time. Too many preachers never learn “to land”; they just switch off the engine and dive for the ground. Others keep going until they run out of things to say and then hope that they can land. Yet others make repeated false attempts to land on the runway only to take off again with a succession of “lastly,” “finally,” “in conclusion,” “just a few more minutes,” and “with this I close.”

One hot July afternoon, as a faculty member of a large British Pentecostal college, I listened with astonishment to the well-known graduation service speaker begin by informing his hearers that he had no idea what he was to say; then he spent over one hour saying it and for the last twenty minutes told us no less than seventeen times that he was about to end. Blessed is the preacher who, having nothing left to say, ceases to demonstrate the fact. Basically, there are three distinct methods of concluding a sermon.

Summary

This is probably the most common method of conclusion and usually involves restating the central theme of the sermon. While repetition may help our hearers remember, it is better not to use the exact wording of our headings, but to rephrase the outline in clear and concise terms. Your intention is to give a synopsis or résumé of how you arrived at your destination.

However, to leave it there as many do is to miss the crucial point. To remind your hearers how you arrived is pointless if you do not tell them where you have arrived. The destination is more important than the journey itself; therefore, your final sentences should focus on the aim. For example, a doctrinal sermon could end with a clear and forthright affirmation of faith; an ethical sermon with a declaration of our intention to obey God in this specific area or a challenge to live out the Word of God—“Go and do likewise” (Luke 10:37); and an apologetic sermon with a direct answer to the question that the preacher was exploring— “I believe in the resurrection of Christ because . . . ” (Chapter 6).

Application

Some preachers apply each point of their sermon as they come to it, and some sermons demand that approach. Precious truths may surface that would be missed if they were not pointed out there and then. Even if you do not focus attention on some gem of truth, the Holy Spirit may. Individuals will be helped or challenged by some passing remark or minor point of the sermon that you hardly noticed yourself. However, be careful not to press home the earlier parts of the sermon too vigorously, causing your sermon to end with an anticlimax. Just a passing comment, succinct question, or personal testimony will usually be adequate.

The main weight of the application belongs properly to the final sentences when the whole message comes together in one powerful thrust. Some preachers quicken their rate of speaking, raise the tone of their voice, and become more emotional. If it is genuine, fine, but if it is not, your hearers will know it as much as you do. Emotion worked up for effect is worthless. If you do not truly feel it, do not do it. God can speak as clearly through quiet tones as through loud tones.

The use of pointed questions is often helpful in the application, such as, “Do you know this to be true?” “Do you see yourself here?” “What does this truth mean to you?” “What will you do?” A popular form of the application a generation ago was to encourage the hearers to insert their own names in the text: “For God so loved [John (your name)] that he gave his one and only Son, that if [John] believes in him, [John] shall not perish but have eternal life” (John 3:16).

Appeal

In speaking of evangelistic preaching (Chapter 6), we noted as one of its characteristics, it calls for a definite decision here and now. Although this call to commitment may be stressed repeatedly throughout the sermon, the conclusion provides the perfect opportunity for summoning the hearers to respond. Billy Graham will often begin his sermon by reminding his hearers that they are not there by accident but to meet with God. Frequently, throughout the sermon he will reemphasize the call to a decisive commitment to Christ: “Tonight could be the most decisive moment you ever live. But you must choose! Choose! Choose!” (Frady 1979, 508). When he gives the concluding invitation for people to come forward to make their decision for Christ, they are ready to respond. They already know what they are to do, and they readily do it.

There are three basic forms of invitation: the private response, the delayed response, and the immediate response. Some preachers prefer not to call for any public response to the gospel message nor to pressure the hearers to reveal the work of God in their lives. Salvation is the work of the Holy Spirit upon the human heart, not the work of the preacher upon the emotions. Any response is and should be strictly private and personal. The preacher’s role is to proclaim the gospel faithfully rather than be overly concerned with the number of responses. Paul described it as, “I planted the seed, Apollos watered it, but God made it grow” (1 Corinthians 3:6). Only eternity will reveal the true results of our preaching. As a result, we should be faithful in preaching whether we see the results or not, rather than be results-oriented.

Other preachers, however, see the appeal as an integral part of the sermon and believe there can be no justification for failure to challenge the hearers to respond to Christ. The crucial question is how that response is to be made known. Martyn Lloyd-Jones usually exhorted his hearers to repent and believe. Then he invited them to meet with him privately after the service or in his office the next day for personal counseling. Other methods of the delayed response would include inviting the earnest seeker to remain behind or to join a special class for those wanting to know more about the faith. The goal is to encourage individuals to make thorough and thoughtful commitments to Christ without embarrassment or pressure.

Although some situations may make the delayed response the preferable method, the only form demonstrated in the Bible is both immediate and public. In the Old Testament, Moses “stood at the entrance to the camp and said, ‘Whoever is for the Lord, come to me.’ And all the Levites rallied to him” (Exodus 32:26). Similarly Joshua invited the Israelites, “Choose for yourselves this day whom you will serve,” and they emphatically responded, “We will serve the Lord our God and obey him” (Joshua 24:14–24).

Jesus consistently summoned people, “Come, follow me,” and they responded openly and immediately (Matthew 4:19–22; See also 8:21–22; 9:9). At no time did Jesus object to those responding by suggesting that He did not intend for them to respond so openly and immediately; rather, He rebuked those who sought to delay their response to His call (Luke 9:59–62). In the early church, the disciples both expected and kept some statistical records of the response to the preaching. “Those who accepted his message were baptized, and about three thousand were added to their number that day . . . And the Lord added to their number daily those who were being saved” (Acts 2:41, 47; See also 4:4; 5:14; 6:1, 7).

Probably the most common immediate response is the altar call. Here, the preacher invites the earnest seeker to come forward to the front of the congregation where specially prepared workers will meet and help that one individually. Whether the seeker is helped at the front or taken into another room will depend upon the situation, but everyone coming forward should be met as he or she responds. Rex Humbard told of the young man who came forward and knelt at the altar of a country church. After a few minutes of waiting there alone, he arose and left, bitterly remarking to his sister that he was never going into another church again. He felt unwanted, and so he devoted his life to crime (www.craigdailypress.com). John Dillinger, one of America’s most dangerous criminals, might have been saved if someone had knelt beside him in that church meeting and prayed with him that night.

There may be good reasons why those seeking God cannot be invited to come forward. Sometimes it is the lack of space or the culture of the congregation. The preacher may then prefer to have the seekers raise their hands, stand, or simply repeat an appropriate prayer. Christian workers may then be assigned to move alongside those indicating their need.

Some preachers first ask the seeker to pray, and then as an act of public testimony, to come forward for further help or, as in the early church, to be baptized in water as a public confession of Christ (Acts 2:38, 41). Not only does this method remove any emotional pressure to come forward in order to be saved, but it also allows those who have already committed their lives to Christ to acknowledge Him publicly as their Savior.

The advantages of calling people forward are that it provides an opportunity for personal contact with the seeker, it may encourage others also to respond, and it allows a variety of needs to be dealt with. The sermon may relate to rededication, the baptism in the Holy Spirit, healing of the body, or spiritual problems other than salvation. It is wise to have the challenged believer respond publicly to God as well as the convicted sinner. God can meet every need, and you can be His fellow worker in prayer.

**Q:** Describe the correct use of summary as a method of conclusion.

**A:** In the summary method, preachers rephrase the outline in clear, concise terms. They should not only summarize how they arrived at the conclusion, but also clearly state the aim of the sermon by showing where they arrived.

**Q:** Explain the balance between application throughout the sermon and the final application in the conclusion.

**A:** Sometimes it is necessary to apply each point of the sermon as it is preached. However, one must be careful not to overuse application during the sermon and, in doing so, miss the climax of the final application. The main emphasis of application should come at the end of the sermon. Many times application is the work of the Holy Spirit and will occur at different times throughout the sermon.

**Q:** How does emotion affect the application?

**A:** Genuine emotion is effective and necessary in the sermon application. The hearers need to know that the preacher believes what he or she is preaching.

**Q:** What do you consider the proper use of emotion and tone in sermon application?

**A:** Each person’s answer will be different. Some are comfortable and genuine in using a more forceful tone in the application; others are not. Some will find raising their voice is helpful during the application, whereas others may feel that this is inappropriate. 11 CHAPTER
