Not only does this method remove any emotional pressure to come forward in order to be saved, but it also allows those who have already committed their lives to Christ to acknowledge Him publicly as their Savior.

The advantages of calling people forward are that it provides an opportunity for personal contact with the seeker, it may encourage others also to respond, and it allows a variety of needs to be dealt with. The sermon may relate to rededication, the baptism in the Holy Spirit, healing of the body, or spiritual problems other than salvation. It is wise to have the challenged believer respond publicly to God as well as the convicted sinner. God can meet every need, and you can be His fellow worker in prayer.

**Q:** Describe the correct use of summary as a method of conclusion.

**A:** In the summary method, preachers rephrase the outline in clear, concise terms. They should not only summarize how they arrived at the conclusion, but also clearly state the aim of the sermon by showing where they arrived.

**Q:** Explain the balance between application throughout the sermon and the final application in the conclusion.

**A:** Sometimes it is necessary to apply each point of the sermon as it is preached. However, one must be careful not to overuse application during the sermon and, in doing so, miss the climax of the final application. The main emphasis of application should come at the end of the sermon. Many times application is the work of the Holy Spirit and will occur at different times throughout the sermon.

**Q:** How does emotion affect the application?

**A:** Genuine emotion is effective and necessary in the sermon application. The hearers need to know that the preacher believes what he or she is preaching.

**Q:** What do you consider the proper use of emotion and tone in sermon application?

**A:** Each person’s answer will be different. Some are comfortable and genuine in using a more forceful tone in the application; others are not. Some will find raising their voice is helpful during the application, whereas others may feel that this is inappropriate. 11 CHAPTER

### The Principles

#### 10.3.1 — Identify the basic principles of preparing effective conclusions.

The conclusion is the ending to a specific sermon delivered to a specific congregation by a specific preacher on a specific occasion. There can never be an interchangeable conclusion suited for any occasion. Even if you are preaching on the same text with the same outline, your conclusion must be prepared for the new group of hearers. For the evangelist especially, the temptation to switch on the standard invitation is great. The aim may be the same whenever he or she preaches, and the instructions may follow an identical pattern on each occasion. However, the evangelist who simply switches on a recording of a standard conclusion is no longer ministering to the hearers. He or she is simply going through a ritual.

Appropriate

Since the aim of the sermon controls the development of the exposition (see Chapter 8), the conclusion also must be appropriate to the aim. Whatever you ask people to do must be planned with that ultimate destination in mind. If you are preaching on personal evangelism and your aim is to arouse your hearers to their responsibility to share their faith, you must do more than simply have them pray or stand in commitment to the work. Give them each several tracts to give to unsaved friends; have them pray specifically for those potential contacts; or plan a night for witnessing in a particular area. Similarly, a sermon on divine healing should give an opportunity for prayer for the sick.

Your conclusion should also be appropriate to the congregation. An inexperienced young preacher gave an evangelistic sermon to a hospital ward of very sick people and asked them to “come forward to receive Christ.” As he vainly waited for someone to respond, he realized that they were all in bed. Maybe he was related to the American radio preacher who asked his hearers to close their eyes for prayer. However, most of his listeners were driving cars, not sitting in church.

Personal

Each of your hearers should feel that your closing words were prepared with him or her specifically in mind. If God directed you in preparing that sermon for that occasion, it was because He knew who would be there and what they would need to hear. Therefore, you should never begin an appeal with, “If there is anyone here . . .” If there is no one there to whom the message applies, you are preaching the wrong sermon. I was horrified to hear a discouraged evangelist end his sermon with, “I don’t suppose there’s anyone who would like to accept Christ tonight.” Who would, with that kind of invitation? Be confident: “God is inviting you to believe in Christ as your personal Savior.” Let your hearers know that God expects them to respond and so do you.

Even in doctrinal, ethical, and apologetic sermons, the personal application is essential. Your hearers want to know what your sermon should mean to them and what they should believe and do as a result of listening to your message from God.

Varied

You can always tell when the television program or film is ending because the credits start rolling on the screen. Now is the time to move; interest has gone; action is finished. Too many sermons suffer the same death because the preacher always ends in the same way. The final sentences should be the most gripping moments of the sermon. This is the climax that you have been aiming for since you began. You must choose your words carefully to ensure that you will not lose attention at the end.

Your conclusion could be an appropriate illustration, a series of pointed questions, an exhortation, an affirmation, a testimony, a prayer, a piece of poetry, or any combination of these. With such variety available there is no excuse for monotony here.

Prepared

By now it should be obvious that your conclusion should be prepared beforehand and not left to the inspiration of the moment. If you do not know your destination, how will you know when you have arrived? By preparing extensively, even to the very words you will use, you can eliminate fumbling and distracting your hearers.

Resist the temptation to bring in new material, especially if it is material you forgot earlier in the sermon. Your hearers will start thinking about the new ideas, and you will lose their attention from the conclusion. The proper place for new information is in the body of the sermon. If you missed it then, save it for next time.

It is essential that the conclusion be as brief and as clear as possible. This will avoid confusion. If people are not sure what you want them to do, they will not do it. If you make sure your instructions cannot be misunderstood, you will serve your hearers well. Read them to your spouse or a good friend, and change what they do not understand.

The conclusion, then, can be the high point of the sermon. It demands our best efforts and will provide our greatest encouragement as we see God’s purposes take place in the lives of our hearers.

## 11 - Sermon Delivery

### Speech

#### 11.1.1 — Describe the essentials of effective delivery.

The manner in which we deliver our sermons is extremely important. The most carefully prepared message can be ruined by careless delivery. God has called us to be His ambassadors, pleading with the lost to be reconciled to God (2 Corinthians 5:20). Therefore, anything that distracts from the message or blunts its impact must be ruthlessly excluded. Eternity is at stake. We must ask ourselves every time we preach, “Could I have presented it better?” Then resolve next time to do so.

Since preaching is essentially a verbal form of communication, the way we speak can say as much as the words we use. A change in emphasis or inflection can alter the meaning of words. Many missionaries speaking in a foreign language have discovered this, much to their embarrassment. The speech psychologist Albert Mehrabian (1971), estimated that only seven percent of communication comes from our words themselves, but thirty-eight percent results from the way we say those words and fifty-five percent from our gestures and facial expressions (44). Yet, most of our sermon preparation is devoted to the words themselves.

Character

Although the words themselves are not the most important element in communication, there is no speech without them. Words express ideas and explain actions. If the words we use do not communicate, they are worthless. W. E. Sangster (1958) relates how John Wesley changed the way he communicated using “a simple domestic servant” to help by “urging her to stop him at every word she did not understand. His manuscript became a maze of erasures and interlinings until he had a speaking style so plain and unpretentious that people with no formal education whatever had no difficulty following his thought” (62). Every word she did not understand he changed until she knew exactly what he meant to say. The wise King Solomon noted the value of well-chosen words: “A word aptly spoken is like apples of gold in settings of silver” (Proverbs 25:11).

In the world of literature, great authors often write and rewrite many times to ensure that their masterpieces say exactly what is needed. In preaching, we may not be able to go to such lengths unless we make a practice of writing out our sermons in full. Nevertheless, we must take care to select our words carefully since the eternal destiny of our hearers may rest on the clarity of our communication. The preaching of Jesus was marked by the “gracious words that came from his lips” (Luke 4:22). His speech had an attractiveness that both drew the attention of His hearers and communicated life to them. “You have the words of eternal life,” declared Peter (John 6:68).

To master the language in which we are to speak, we may read good literature and listen to effective speakers, noting how they communicate their ideas. Ask yourself, “What was it that moved me? What can I learn from this?” Our goal is not just to string some words together, but also to use the right words for the occasion, for the message, and for the hearer.

Two words of warning are necessary, however. First, never say things designed to impress people with your eloquence, education, or even your spirituality. Your aim is to communicate Christ as effectively and clearly as you can. Anything that distracts the hearer from Him must be ruthlessly excluded from your preaching. As noted earlier, a visitor to Edinburgh, the Scottish capital, heard two renowned preachers. After the first sermon he remarked to his companion, “What a preacher!” His mood changed after hearing the second one preach. “What a Savior!” he declared. That preacher had done his work well.

Second, do not fail to rely on the Holy Spirit. The success of Paul’s preaching did not rest upon “wise and persuasive words” but upon the Spirit’s power (1 Corinthians 2:4). “This is what we speak, not in words taught us by human wisdom but in words taught by the Spirit, expressing spiritual truths in spiritual words” (1 Corinthians 2:13). Let the Holy Spirit give you the right words for the occasion.

Tone

Since the way we say words can strongly affect their meaning, it is important that we pay attention to the tone of voice we use. Some topics naturally demand a particular tone. To speak of love in an angry and belligerent voice, or of peace in an agitated manner, or to deliver a warning of impending doom for the unrepentant sinner in an unconcerned fashion, will not convince our hearers that we believe or even know what we are saying. We need to pray over our sermons until the message burns in our hearts. Then we can speak in convincing and compelling tones (Psalm 39:3).

To say that we should speak naturally does not mean that we should speak in a conversational tone as though our preaching were no more important than a casual comment about the weather. The Bible describes the preacher as a herald—one who proclaims good news (see Chapter 2). A herald must speak loudly and clearly enough to be heard. If the sermon cannot be heard, the message is lost regardless of how well it is prepared.

In the fall of 1740, the great revivalist George Whitefield preached on Boston Common in Massachusetts and was clearly heard by more than fifteen thousand people. A century later, another famous preacher, Rowland Hill, while walking in the Cotswold Hills in Gloucestershire, England, saw a gravel pit collapse burying three workers. His call for help was distinctly heard in the town a mile away. Today we have become so dependent on amplifiers that we have forgotten how to project our voices. Our goal as preachers should always be to proclaim the good news to all we can so that our hearers might know the message we bring.

Emphasis

Monotony kills sermons. The most exciting truths can be presented in a flat, dull voice, and their impact will be totally lost. You are proclaiming eternal, life-transforming truth. Do it with all the effectiveness you can.

All preachers are not born with highly expressive voices, but all are capable of developing expressive ways of speaking that will make people want to listen. Emphasis introduces variety to your speech and draws attention to particular points. There are five ways we can emphasize our words: variations in volume, pitch, pace, the dramatic pause, and repetition.

Volume is probably the most commonly used method of emphasis. However, when God wanted to get Elijah’s attention, He spoke in “a gentle whisper” (1 Kings 19:12). The full-voiced whisper or stage whisper can be as effective as the roar of the hurricane. It is better to reserve great volume for high points in the sermon rather than maintain a constant level of loudness. Change your volume appropriately, but do not suddenly drop your voice below the level of clear audibility .

Pitch is the melody of the voice, like moving up and down a musical scale. A rising inflection at the end of a sentence may change it into a question. By pitching different words at a different level, the inference of the sentence may vary radically. For example, practice saying, “the Lord is my Shepherd” (Psalm 23:1), emphasizing a different word each time. The point of the sentence changes every time. Even if you do not have much variation in your voice, practice the public reading of Scripture passages, placing as much emphasis as you can in the appropriate places until your reading lives.

Combined with variations in volume and pitch changes is pace. A quick rate of speech suggests excitement, concern, and passion. However, a continuous fast pace becomes difficult to follow, words are mispronounced and run into each other, and the hearers become exhausted. By pronouncing some words more slowly and deliberately, we draw attention to them and retain the interest of our hearers. The opposite problem is the slow, ponderous style of the statesman preacher who utters weighty declarations and allows time for attention to wander between words. “Preaching in a staccato manner first modeled on the machine-gun delivery of the radio gossip-monger Walter Winchell, Graham injected a strident urgency all his own into a mostly traditional message” (Whitfield 1991, 79).

By interrupting the steady flow of words with an occasional use of the dramatic pause , we can pinpoint particular ideas just as the Psalmist would interject “Selah” (Psalm 3:2, 4, 8). We are saying, “Stop here and think about this.” Pauses are the punctuation marks of speech. They help the hearer identify important points. By pausing briefly immediately before or after a word or phrase, we separate it from the surrounding words. Pauses should always be kept brief, but remember: they will seem longer to you than to your hearers. A pause should be long enough to draw attention to the point made but not so long as to focus attention on the silence.

Students of the poetic writings of the Bible acknowledge the place of repetition as a characteristic means of emphasis. While to say the same thing in the same words may be boring and ineffective, to repeat ideas in different words is one of the most valuable tools of communication. Those who failed to understand the first statement might well grasp the second. Even those who did understand the words from the first will be impressed with the importance of the idea when the same truth is expressed differently. Repetition is not the echoing of a phrase or the last few words spoken. Even though some prominent preachers have acquired “that habit, that habit,” it can be “quite irritating, quite irritating” to the hearers. And every word does not need to be “put another way.” If it is plain enough to be understood and needs no special emphasis, do not draw unnecessary attention to it.

With the variety of ways available for us to emphasize our sermons, there is no reason for our delivery to be flat and monotonous. Avoid overuse of one type of emphasis. Our goal should be to make truth live so that our hearers grasp and are gripped by the immense importance of our message.

**Q:** List some things that you can do to make your use of words more effective.

**A:** It is important that people understand your words. Concentrate on the audience to whom you will speak as you prepare your words. Listen to effective speakers and notice how they communicate their ideas. Practice or write out your sermons so that each word is well chosen.

**Q:** What caution does your IST author give regarding the effective use of words?

**A:** Remember that words do not have to be eloquent or intellectual. Your aim is to preach Christ. Depend on the Holy Spirit to use your words to convince men and women to follow Christ.

**Q:** Ask a friend to help you with this practical assignment. Stand at a distance and speak to your friend. Take care not to shout. Increase the distance until you reach your maximum range without straining your voice.

**A:** I hope you found this exercise helpful. Refer to this method at various times in your future ministry. With practice you can speak effectively to large numbers of people.

**Q:** Allowing the hearers to think about a certain point is an example of which method of voice emphasis?

**A:** Pause

**Q:** Rephrasing a statement so that everyone clearly understands is an example of which method of voice emphasis?

**A:** Repetition

### Gestures

#### 11.2.1 — Distinguish between appropriate and inappropriate pulpit gestures.

The human body was intended to move; so God made it with joints, muscles, and a controlling nervous system. When God calls someone to preach, He does not make that one less human, but He uses that mobile human frame to express the gospel. There is no reason for preachers to imitate talking statues when standing before their hearers. They offer them life; let them live as they proclaim that gospel.

While the matter of gestures arises from our own personality, I personally like preachers to put both body and soul into the message. This does not mean that they should move around wildly, but that their voice should be emphasized by the movements of their body. The very act of moving your arms or the position of your body can introduce vigor into your voice. The kind of movements we make should be determined by the content of our message. To shake the fist while declaring, “We need more love!” would not seem appropriate.

Natural

This applies in two ways: natural to the preacher and natural to the point. Gestures that look awkward or do not fit the preacher’s personality will make the hearers uncomfortable. Some naturally use gestures more than others because of their culture. In preaching, we should not gesture less than we would in conversation. In reality, the larger the congregation the larger, more pronounced, and emphatic the gestures need to be, if they are to convey the same emotion.

Although some gestures may be natural to the preacher, they are inappropriate to the topic. The sermon content should always determine the movement. Gentle points require gestures that suggest gentleness in the culture of the hearers. Not all cultures use the same gestures to represent the same emotion. In many countries to move the head from side to side means “No!” but in Bulgaria and parts of Greece it means “Yes!” If you are not sure of the meaning of a gesture, check it out first. Be observant of the customs of your hearers lest your voice says “No!” when your body says “Yes!”

Intentional

Avoid anything that injects a sense of artificiality into your sermons. Nevertheless, in a desire to be natural, your gestures may be little more than nervous mannerisms such as fidgeting with eyeglasses or parts of the clothing or sermon notes. As a student in Britain, I noticed that one of my professors would wind the tapes of his degree robe around his ears while lecturing or roll up his tie under his chin. The quality of his teaching was often lost on his students as we watched him in fascination.

It is easier to develop such mannerisms than it is to stop them. Young preachers especially tend to adopt the characteristic mannerisms of preachers they greatly admire. It is as though the secret of their spiritual power lies in their peculiarities rather than in their walk with God. The Scottish preacher James Stewart used to remind his student preachers, “Be yourself; forget yourself.” Be yourself because you can be you better than you can be anyone else, and God called you to preach because He wanted to use someone just like you. Forget yourself because self-conscious actions tend to look artificial or overly dramatic.

The rehearsed movement falls into the same trap of self-consciousness. You can overcome this by being so absorbed in your sermon and so conscious of the anointing of the Holy Spirit before you preach that you are aware of nothing but an overwhelming desire to preach the message God has given you. You may not be as polished as some preachers, but you will shine with the glow of the Spirit.

When you first learned to drive a car, you were probably very conscious of the actions you had to perform in correct sequence, but eventually you did those same actions almost automatically. So it is with preaching. The best cure I know for nervous mannerisms is to do something intentional and constructive with your body. You cannot use gestures and fidget at the same time. You cannot slouch over the pulpit or hang onto it as though your life depended on it and walk across the platform to face part of your congregation simultaneously. You cannot gaze into space and look people in the eye at the same time. Do something positive, and negative actions are impossible.

Varied

Repetition can produce boredom, especially if a preacher repeats the same movements. Some have gained reputations as “pump handle” preachers because of the regular pumping motion of their arms; others prowl back and forth on the platform like caged lions. The repetition of any gesture draws attention to itself instead of to the point it is intended to emphasize.

When it has been estimated that man is capable of making about 700,000 different movements with his hands and arms, there is no reason to limit gestures to a frequent few. Observe how the mime is able to nonverbally communicate whole stories. Often the gesture is more effective than speech. For instance, a shrug of the shoulders cannot easily be translated into words. Even the expression on the face or a glance of the eyes can speak more eloquently than the words verbalized.

In normal speech, the gesture should slightly precede the word; in vigorous, forceful speech, it should accompany the word. Never should the gesture follow the word it emphasizes; it will look melodramatic. The well-timed, appropriate gesture makes the sermon live.

**Q:** Give some examples of inappropriate sermon gestures that you have observed while listening to sermons. Then contrast those instances with the use of appropriate sermon gestures.

**A:** Your answer. Some examples are pacing back and forth across the platform while preaching, moving the hands constantly while speaking, and nervous habits such as twisting the cord of the microphone. In contrast, some appropriate gestures would be taking a step backwards, folding one’s hands, shaking the head, pausing with a look of question on the face, and stretching the arms to visually demonstrate length or width.

### Methods

#### 11.3.1 — Evaluate the strengths and weaknesses of the reading method.

A sermon is not truly a sermon until it is preached. All that precedes the act of preaching is preparation, and all that follows is a response to the message. Only as the Word of the Lord is delivered does it become the seed that will bring forth a harvest. Therefore, it is of great importance to choose an effective method of proclamation.

There are three basic methods available to us: to preach from a full manuscript, entirely without notes, or with brief outline notes. Each of these methods, or a variation of them, has been used effectively by outstanding preachers. No one method is singularly anointed by the Holy Spirit, although some preachers feel more at ease with one than with others. What finally determines the choice is the preference of the preacher, the type of congregation (some people consider that a Spirit-directed preacher will not use notes), and the nature of the sermon (some messages involving more precise wording and timing, as in a speech or a script, need to be written out more fully than others).

Reading

It is good practice, especially when one first begins preaching, to write a sermon out in full occasionally, even if you will only use a shortened version of the manuscript in the act of preaching. This helps you concentrate on your message and develop the habit of precise thinking and speaking. The best way to eliminate wandering thoughts in the pew is to eliminate them in the pulpit. The written sermon is a great help.

Although some believe that written sermons limit the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, the fact is that if the Holy Spirit can inspire a preacher while preaching, He can just as fully inspire the preparation and writing out of a sermon. Outstanding preachers of the past, such as the American revival leader Jonathan Edwards, or the notable Scot Thomas Chalmers, effectively read fully written sermons. Our bookshelves would be considerably poorer if some of the great Bible expositors had not written out their messages in full.

For some preachers the manuscript would hinder their rapport with the congregation. This is partly because the preacher loses eye contact with hearers while looking at the manuscript and partly because the preacher loses flexibility or responsiveness to the immediate needs and responses of the congregation. The first problem can be overcome by such a familiarity with the sermon that an occasional glance at the manuscript is sufficient to refresh the memory. This will also help in dealing with the issue of flexibility. Certainly no preacher ought to be so bound by a manuscript that he or she cannot respond to the immediate leading of the Holy Spirit or needs of the hearers and, if necessary, repeat, rephrase, or rework a point.

In preparing the manuscript for the pulpit, you should keep clearly in focus the fact that you are writing a sermon to be spoken, not a dissertation , essay, or article to be read. If it does not come over as conversational, change it. If you are especially pleased with your manuscript and do not wish to waste your fine writing, submit it to a Christian journal for publication. In any case, do not read it to your congregation without putting it into true sermon form.

Extemporaneous

The term extempore properly refers to preaching from the inspiration of the occasion without prior thought or preparation. In popular usage, however, it can cover everything from unprepared speeches to speaking without notes but with extensive preparation. Some preachers call preaching without notes “free delivery” and preserve the more proper usage of extempore . We will consider both in this section since many of the same principles apply to both.

While it may seem that extemporaneous preaching requires less effort on the preacher’s part and relies more extensively on the Holy Spirit, that is a total misunderstanding. The only reduction in effort is in the actual writing of the sermon itself. God has not promised to anoint unprepared servants; indeed Jesus spent some three years preparing His disciples to be His witnesses. There may be occasions when we are required to speak with the minimum or no time for immediate preparation. In circumstances such as these, we are assured of the direct prompting of the Holy Spirit (Mark 13:11).

As we saw in Chapter 3, the preparation of a sermon really begins with the preparation of the preacher. Your life experiences, your walk with God, and your knowledge of the Word of God all help make you the person God can and will use. Alongside the personal preparation is the particular preparation for the sermon itself as expressed in Unit 2, especially Chapter 4. As you seek God for His word for the people and meditate on that word, it will burn in your soul until you cannot fail to speak it (Psalm 39:3).

The final stage of preparation is immediately prior to preaching when, like a runner getting ready to start a race, you await the moment you will begin to preach the Word of Life. This brief time may be crucial to the effectiveness of the message. Any disturbance of your spirit, any break in your relationship with God, and any distraction from the main task can hinder the flow of the Holy Spirit through you and rob you of the consciousness of His anointing. Satan knows how important this is and will do all in his power to tempt you to anger, irritation, lustful thoughts, worry, or doubt. You must consciously pray for the anointing of the Holy Spirit if you want to minister effectively (Acts 4:8; 1 Thessalonians 1:5).

For the fully prepared preacher, extemporaneous preaching gives the impression that God is speaking directly to the hearers. The preacher maintains eye contact easily, and learns to depend on the Holy Spirit even as he preaches. Unfortunately, this last factor can degenerate into preaching according to the whim of the moment or become so habitual that every sermon sounds basically the same regardless of the text.

Probably the most common problem is that of length; just one more illustration or text comes to mind, and into the sermon it goes. This problem becomes more pronounced with age and experience. Keep to the point and keep it concise. People may stop listening before you stop talking. Eternal describes God’s truth, not your sermon’s length. Also, the preacher may possibly suffer a sudden lapse of memory and lose that carefully prepared sermon. For this reason, many preachers choose a middle road between the extremes of free or extemporaneous delivery and using a full manuscript.

Notes

Probably the most popular method—preaching from brief notes—allows the preacher all the advantages of extemporaneous delivery while minimizing the problems associated with that method. If you have a single-sided piece of paper with the main points boldly illuminated and can slip it into your Bible at your text, a simple glance will be sufficient to keep you on track. Many preachers find it helpful to add a few words of introduction and conclusion to the outline such as some pertinent Scripture references and reminders regarding illustrations to be used. I recommend only using one side of your notepaper so that you can keep an overall view of your message during the preaching and avoid over-preaching side one to the loss of side two. If you prefer to record your outline notes on a handheld device for use in the pulpit, I advise you to also have a hard copy available in case of battery failure or accidental erasure.

In the early stages of your ministry, you may find it helpful to write out a full manuscript even if you will reduce the sermon to notes for preaching. With experience and increasing demands on your time, you may find that the pulpit notes are all that you need to commit to writing. If you do not have a fuller manuscript, however, you will find it harder to preach that sermon again, if the Lord should so direct you, without repeating most of the preparation.

You may also find it helpful to give your full manuscript to your spouse or a friend in case your notes fail to prompt your memory. One of my former students in preaching came to the cryptic note, “the neighbor’s cat.” His memory failed. What did it mean? He decided to say the words out loud hoping to recapture his fleeing thoughts, but nothing happened. Finally, frustrated he announced, “My neighbor’s cat knows more about what I was going to say next than I do” and ended his sermon there. His wife could have prompted him had she known the full story.

Our hearers have come to receive the word from God, and we are responsible to God to deliver it as clearly and effectively as we can. God forbid that anyone should miss the point because we failed to present it properly. Only eternity will reveal the value of the well-chosen word, the sincere facial expression, or the momentary eye contact from the pulpit.

**Q:** Evaluate the strengths and weaknesses of the reading method.

**A:** The reading method helps the preacher make clear, concise statements. It is often a good way for a new preacher to organize his or her thoughts. Many of the greatest sermons in history followed the reading method. Today we are fortunate to have them recorded to help us in spiritual insight. On the negative side, the reading method can hinder the preacher from having rapport and eye contact with the audience. It is important, therefore, to know the material well enough to keep the audience in focus. Another area of concern is that the manuscript may become a dissertation rather than a sermon. Even when a sermon is read, it should be the preaching of God’s Word.

**Q:** What caution does your IST author point out regarding the inspiration and leading of the Holy Spirit during a sermon reading? What can you do as a preacher to make sure you are sensitive to the Spirit’s instructions while you are reading a sermon?

**A:** Many criticize the reading method because it does not allow the preacher to respond to the leading of the Holy Spirit during the sermon. However, if you as the preacher are familiar with your manuscript, you will be able to maintain rapport with the hearers while being sensitive to the Holy Spirit. The reading method does not mean that you cannot change or add something while you are reading from the manuscript.

**Q:** What are the obvious dangers of the extemporaneous method?

**A:** There is often a temptation, when one knows the sermon will be extemporaneous in method, to avoid taking time for preparation. While depending on the Holy Spirit to help deliver a sermon, it is still necessary to carefully plan and prepare the message ahead of time. Another danger is length. It is sometimes difficult to judge the length of a sermon when it is not completely written out, or it is easy to keep adding just “one more idea.”

**Q:** What does the Word of God tell us regarding extemporaneous preaching?

**A:** The Holy Spirit certainly will bless our words when we are living for Christ. God’s Word teaches that we should “be prepared in season and out of season” (2 Timothy 4:2). As we speak out for Him, regardless of the method of preaching we choose, He will use us as His ambassadors.

**Q:** Can you think of some examples of times when a preacher might be asked to preach extemporaneously? How would you handle such a situation in your ministry?

**A:** Your answer. Ministers are often asked to share extemporaneous messages at gatherings such as celebrations for business, community, or family. Perhaps in your culture a minister is asked to give impromptu sermons when he visits another church. At those times, it is necessary to remember that God will give you thoughts and ideas that will be appropriate for the occasion. Remember that your life is full of experiences from which to draw testimonies and illustrations. Remember, though, that there is a difference between planned extemporaneous sermons and an impromptu sermon.

**Q:** What are the advantages of using notes as a method of preaching? Compare this method to the other methods of preaching.

**A:** Using notes allows the preacher to maintain eye contact and rapport with the audience, which is often difficult in the reading method. It is also possible to have a clear and concise presentation, which is sometimes a weakness of the extemporaneous method. There are times when using notes combines the strengths of the other methods, such as writing out the sermon in full and then making a brief outline for the actual sermon delivery. The method of using notes allows the preacher flexibility; one can make extensive or brief notes depending on the particular preacher or sermon. UNIT 4

#### 11.3.2 — Explain how to appropriately use the extemporaneous method of preaching.

A sermon is not truly a sermon until it is preached. All that precedes the act of preaching is preparation, and all that follows is a response to the message. Only as the Word of the Lord is delivered does it become the seed that will bring forth a harvest. Therefore, it is of great importance to choose an effective method of proclamation.

There are three basic methods available to us: to preach from a full manuscript, entirely without notes, or with brief outline notes. Each of these methods, or a variation of them, has been used effectively by outstanding preachers. No one method is singularly anointed by the Holy Spirit, although some preachers feel more at ease with one than with others. What finally determines the choice is the preference of the preacher, the type of congregation (some people consider that a Spirit-directed preacher will not use notes), and the nature of the sermon (some messages involving more precise wording and timing, as in a speech or a script, need to be written out more fully than others).

Reading

It is good practice, especially when one first begins preaching, to write a sermon out in full occasionally, even if you will only use a shortened version of the manuscript in the act of preaching. This helps you concentrate on your message and develop the habit of precise thinking and speaking. The best way to eliminate wandering thoughts in the pew is to eliminate them in the pulpit. The written sermon is a great help.

Although some believe that written sermons limit the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, the fact is that if the Holy Spirit can inspire a preacher while preaching, He can just as fully inspire the preparation and writing out of a sermon. Outstanding preachers of the past, such as the American revival leader Jonathan Edwards, or the notable Scot Thomas Chalmers, effectively read fully written sermons. Our bookshelves would be considerably poorer if some of the great Bible expositors had not written out their messages in full.

For some preachers the manuscript would hinder their rapport with the congregation. This is partly because the preacher loses eye contact with hearers while looking at the manuscript and partly because the preacher loses flexibility or responsiveness to the immediate needs and responses of the congregation. The first problem can be overcome by such a familiarity with the sermon that an occasional glance at the manuscript is sufficient to refresh the memory. This will also help in dealing with the issue of flexibility. Certainly no preacher ought to be so bound by a manuscript that he or she cannot respond to the immediate leading of the Holy Spirit or needs of the hearers and, if necessary, repeat, rephrase, or rework a point.

In preparing the manuscript for the pulpit, you should keep clearly in focus the fact that you are writing a sermon to be spoken, not a dissertation , essay, or article to be read. If it does not come over as conversational, change it. If you are especially pleased with your manuscript and do not wish to waste your fine writing, submit it to a Christian journal for publication. In any case, do not read it to your congregation without putting it into true sermon form.

Extemporaneous

The term extempore properly refers to preaching from the inspiration of the occasion without prior thought or preparation. In popular usage, however, it can cover everything from unprepared speeches to speaking without notes but with extensive preparation. Some preachers call preaching without notes “free delivery” and preserve the more proper usage of extempore . We will consider both in this section since many of the same principles apply to both.

While it may seem that extemporaneous preaching requires less effort on the preacher’s part and relies more extensively on the Holy Spirit, that is a total misunderstanding. The only reduction in effort is in the actual writing of the sermon itself. God has not promised to anoint unprepared servants; indeed Jesus spent some three years preparing His disciples to be His witnesses. There may be occasions when we are required to speak with the minimum or no time for immediate preparation. In circumstances such as these, we are assured of the direct prompting of the Holy Spirit (Mark 13:11).

As we saw in Chapter 3, the preparation of a sermon really begins with the preparation of the preacher. Your life experiences, your walk with God, and your knowledge of the Word of God all help make you the person God can and will use. Alongside the personal preparation is the particular preparation for the sermon itself as expressed in Unit 2, especially Chapter 4. As you seek God for His word for the people and meditate on that word, it will burn in your soul until you cannot fail to speak it (Psalm 39:3).

The final stage of preparation is immediately prior to preaching when, like a runner getting ready to start a race, you await the moment you will begin to preach the Word of Life. This brief time may be crucial to the effectiveness of the message. Any disturbance of your spirit, any break in your relationship with God, and any distraction from the main task can hinder the flow of the Holy Spirit through you and rob you of the consciousness of His anointing. Satan knows how important this is and will do all in his power to tempt you to anger, irritation, lustful thoughts, worry, or doubt. You must consciously pray for the anointing of the Holy Spirit if you want to minister effectively (Acts 4:8; 1 Thessalonians 1:5).

For the fully prepared preacher, extemporaneous preaching gives the impression that God is speaking directly to the hearers. The preacher maintains eye contact easily, and learns to depend on the Holy Spirit even as he preaches. Unfortunately, this last factor can degenerate into preaching according to the whim of the moment or become so habitual that every sermon sounds basically the same regardless of the text.

Probably the most common problem is that of length; just one more illustration or text comes to mind, and into the sermon it goes. This problem becomes more pronounced with age and experience. Keep to the point and keep it concise. People may stop listening before you stop talking. Eternal describes God’s truth, not your sermon’s length. Also, the preacher may possibly suffer a sudden lapse of memory and lose that carefully prepared sermon. For this reason, many preachers choose a middle road between the extremes of free or extemporaneous delivery and using a full manuscript.

Notes

Probably the most popular method—preaching from brief notes—allows the preacher all the advantages of extemporaneous delivery while minimizing the problems associated with that method. If you have a single-sided piece of paper with the main points boldly illuminated and can slip it into your Bible at your text, a simple glance will be sufficient to keep you on track. Many preachers find it helpful to add a few words of introduction and conclusion to the outline such as some pertinent Scripture references and reminders regarding illustrations to be used. I recommend only using one side of your notepaper so that you can keep an overall view of your message during the preaching and avoid over-preaching side one to the loss of side two. If you prefer to record your outline notes on a handheld device for use in the pulpit, I advise you to also have a hard copy available in case of battery failure or accidental erasure.

In the early stages of your ministry, you may find it helpful to write out a full manuscript even if you will reduce the sermon to notes for preaching. With experience and increasing demands on your time, you may find that the pulpit notes are all that you need to commit to writing. If you do not have a fuller manuscript, however, you will find it harder to preach that sermon again, if the Lord should so direct you, without repeating most of the preparation.

You may also find it helpful to give your full manuscript to your spouse or a friend in case your notes fail to prompt your memory. One of my former students in preaching came to the cryptic note, “the neighbor’s cat.” His memory failed. What did it mean? He decided to say the words out loud hoping to recapture his fleeing thoughts, but nothing happened. Finally, frustrated he announced, “My neighbor’s cat knows more about what I was going to say next than I do” and ended his sermon there. His wife could have prompted him had she known the full story.

Our hearers have come to receive the word from God, and we are responsible to God to deliver it as clearly and effectively as we can. God forbid that anyone should miss the point because we failed to present it properly. Only eternity will reveal the value of the well-chosen word, the sincere facial expression, or the momentary eye contact from the pulpit.

**Q:** Evaluate the strengths and weaknesses of the reading method.

**A:** The reading method helps the preacher make clear, concise statements. It is often a good way for a new preacher to organize his or her thoughts. Many of the greatest sermons in history followed the reading method. Today we are fortunate to have them recorded to help us in spiritual insight. On the negative side, the reading method can hinder the preacher from having rapport and eye contact with the audience. It is important, therefore, to know the material well enough to keep the audience in focus. Another area of concern is that the manuscript may become a dissertation rather than a sermon. Even when a sermon is read, it should be the preaching of God’s Word.

**Q:** What caution does your IST author point out regarding the inspiration and leading of the Holy Spirit during a sermon reading? What can you do as a preacher to make sure you are sensitive to the Spirit’s instructions while you are reading a sermon?

**A:** Many criticize the reading method because it does not allow the preacher to respond to the leading of the Holy Spirit during the sermon. However, if you as the preacher are familiar with your manuscript, you will be able to maintain rapport with the hearers while being sensitive to the Holy Spirit. The reading method does not mean that you cannot change or add something while you are reading from the manuscript.

**Q:** What are the obvious dangers of the extemporaneous method?

**A:** There is often a temptation, when one knows the sermon will be extemporaneous in method, to avoid taking time for preparation. While depending on the Holy Spirit to help deliver a sermon, it is still necessary to carefully plan and prepare the message ahead of time. Another danger is length. It is sometimes difficult to judge the length of a sermon when it is not completely written out, or it is easy to keep adding just “one more idea.”

**Q:** What does the Word of God tell us regarding extemporaneous preaching?

**A:** The Holy Spirit certainly will bless our words when we are living for Christ. God’s Word teaches that we should “be prepared in season and out of season” (2 Timothy 4:2). As we speak out for Him, regardless of the method of preaching we choose, He will use us as His ambassadors.

**Q:** Can you think of some examples of times when a preacher might be asked to preach extemporaneously? How would you handle such a situation in your ministry?

**A:** Your answer. Ministers are often asked to share extemporaneous messages at gatherings such as celebrations for business, community, or family. Perhaps in your culture a minister is asked to give impromptu sermons when he visits another church. At those times, it is necessary to remember that God will give you thoughts and ideas that will be appropriate for the occasion. Remember that your life is full of experiences from which to draw testimonies and illustrations. Remember, though, that there is a difference between planned extemporaneous sermons and an impromptu sermon.

**Q:** What are the advantages of using notes as a method of preaching? Compare this method to the other methods of preaching.

**A:** Using notes allows the preacher to maintain eye contact and rapport with the audience, which is often difficult in the reading method. It is also possible to have a clear and concise presentation, which is sometimes a weakness of the extemporaneous method. There are times when using notes combines the strengths of the other methods, such as writing out the sermon in full and then making a brief outline for the actual sermon delivery. The method of using notes allows the preacher flexibility; one can make extensive or brief notes depending on the particular preacher or sermon. UNIT 4

## 12 - Topical Sermons

### Definition

#### 12.1.1 — Distinguish between topical sermons and topical preaching.

Two terms are often confused: topical sermon and topical preaching. What is the difference? When the adjective topical is applied to the subject matter of the sermon, we talk of topical preaching, but when we are referring to the structure or form of the sermon, we use topical sermons.

Topical preaching refers to sermons devoted to themes of current concern or interest among our hearers. This is so whether we are explaining the biblical teaching on a topic of concern such as abortion, euthanasia , or sexual morality; or applying biblical principles to matters of ethics like environmental issues or modern business practices; or addressing cultural and political problems such as racial bigotry , democracy, liberty, and the care of the poor. Such themes may form the subject matter of the sermon but they are not a separate category of sermons. They may be approached evangelistically, to precipitate a decision by the hearers; doctrinally, to clarify biblical teaching; apologetically, to defend the Christian position; or ethically, to change the conduct of our hearers (Chapter 6).

Topical sermons, however, are a separate category of sermons because here we are referring to the way in which we handle a portion of Scripture to produce the structure or skeleton of the sermon. As we saw in Unit 3, Sermon Construction, every sermon must have some kind of structure that provides its form, just as every body takes its shape from its skeleton. In topical sermons the structure is determined by the theme or topic of the text. The rules for the arrangement of the structure are the general principles for the development of the outline (Chapter 8), which we may summarize as faithful to the text and faithful to the topic.

In saying that the topical outline must be faithful to the text , we are applying sound hermeneutical principles to our portion of Scripture. Basically, it is not possible for a text to mean today what it could not mean at the time it was written; therefore, we must take care in developing the topic of the Bible portion not to import our meaning nor read later theological concepts into earlier texts. The wording of the text may prepare the way for later revelation, but we cannot make Moses speak as though he were Paul. Notice that the plural in Genesis 1:26 opens the door for the doctrine of the Trinity, but the full revelation was to come later.

The outline must also be faithful to the topic . By mastering as fully as possible the subject of the sermon, our outline can fully develop the theme. Be careful not to emphasize one area and omit other important aspects of the topic, which might lead to different conclusions than those we desire. For example, a sermon on the doctrine of salvation may omit all references to the eternal purposes of God in order to promote Pelagianism , or it may seek to promote Calvinistic determinism by ignoring the biblical teaching on human responsibility.

Although one sermon cannot exhaust a subject, our consideration should be as comprehensive as possible. Even if we are preaching a series of sermons on a theme, each sermon should be complete in itself. A brief reference to complementary aspects of the subject now being considered will be sufficient to remind our hearers that this one sermon is not the final word on the topic. Paul exhorted Titus: “You must teach what is in accord with sound doctrine” (Titus 2:1). The original Greek word for sound carries also the idea of “healthy and whole.” A true statement that is incomplete can be as misleading as an outright lie. Our goal is faithfulness to both topic and text.

**Q:** Decide if each circumstance indicates topical sermons or topical preaching :

**A:** a) topical preaching b) topical sermons

**Q:** a) Preaching on “Christian Ethics in the Workplace” and

**Q:** b) Developing the theme of “Christian Holiness” based on the first chapter of Ephesians.

### Forms

#### 12.2.1 — Demonstrate how to begin a topical outline.

Although the structure or outline of the topical sermon is determined by the theme or topic of the text, an inevitable question arises: “How?” The first step must be to determine what the theme is (Chapter 4). There may be several possible topics in a given portion of Scripture, but there can be only one dominant theme. If the sermon is to deal with a lesser topic from a text, it should be considered in relation to the dominant theme. The topic of faith in John 3:16 should only be developed in relation to the primary subject of God’s Universal Love . A sermon on “The Life of Faith” or “Faith, the Gift of the Spirit” should be drawn from a more appropriate text.

Once we have prayerfully determined what the theme of our sermon is to be and established the aim of the sermon (Chapter 6), we may then develop our outline (Chapter 8). By asking the right questions of the topic in the context of the Scripture portion, we can produce material that is easy to work into a good topical outline. Using the example of Faith , we may ask:

Such interrogation of our topic as this may be extended beyond the sample questions suggested. The goal is to open up the subject for our sermon. Instead of the formal outline heading noted, some preachers prefer either to use the actual question as a division title such as, “I. What is Faith?” or to invert it to “What Faith Is or Is Not . ”

The wide variety of topical forms arising from this approach may be summarized as analytical, illustrative, demonstrative, and biographical.

Analytical

The analytical form is probably the most commonly used because many themes can be successfully interrogated and the results expounded upon. For example:

Text: Acts 26:20 “I preached that they should repent and turn to God and prove their repentance by their deeds.”

Theme: Repentance

Main Divisions:

I. The Nature of Repentance

II. The Need for Repentance

III. The Fruit of Repentance

Text:

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

Illustrative

In the illustrative method, the theme is applied to various situations as examples of a principle. For example:

Text: 1 Samuel 26:21 “Surely I have acted like a fool and have erred greatly.”

Theme: The Folly of Sinful Living

Main Divisions:

I. It Brings Disgrace (Illustration: King Saul, 1 Samuel 28).

II. It Destroys Nations (Illustration: Sodom, Genesis 19).

III. It Closes Heaven (Illustration: The Sinning Prophets, Matthew 7:21–23).

IV. It Silences Christ (Illustration: Herod, Luke 23:8–9).

Text:

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

Demonstrative

When using the demonstrative method, we show why we believe a topical statement to be true either by argument, reason, or proven facts. For example:

Text: John 17:17 “Your word is truth.”

Theme: Why I Believe the Bible Is True

Main Divisions:

I. Because Its Author Cannot Lie (Titus 1:2).

II. Because It Is True to Life (Romans 5:12 ).

III. Because Its Prophecies Are Fulfilled (Matthew 5:18).

IV. Because Its Promises Are Certain (2 Corinthians 1:20).

Text:

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

Biographical

Among the most popular forms of topical sermons is the biographical. History is the story of people, and we enjoy a well-told story about interesting individuals, especially if they are significant. Even a minor Bible person can illustrate powerful spiritual truths. To make biblical persons come alive for our hearers demands that we explore the background material as fully as possible and develop some understanding of the culture of those days. We must beware, however, of presenting masses of historical data rather than of concentrating on the significant and spiritually beneficial. For example:

Text: 1 Kings 16:30 “Ahab son of Omri did more evil in the eyes of the Lord than any of those before him.”

Theme: Ahab the Apostate King

Main Divisions:

I. Ahab Provokes God (1 Kings 16:33).

II. Ahab Proves God (1 Kings 20:13).

III. God Reproves Ahab (1 Kings 22:17).

Text:

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

From the examples given, we can see the value of the occasional topical sermon and the wide variety of forms that can be used.

**Q:** Suppose the topic is a Bible person, such as Abraham. Study the example of faith , and then fill in the outline material that corresponds to the adjacent topic questions.

**A:** TOPIC QUESTION OUTLINE MATERIAL Who is Abraham? The Identity of Abraham Where did Abraham come from? The Background of Abraham What did Abraham do? The Exploits or Activity of Abraham Why is Abraham important? The Significance of Abraham

**Q:** Study the example; then select a text and theme of your own. Develop a brief illustrative outline according to this model.

**A:** Your answer. Compare your illustrative outline with the example in the “Illustrative” section of your IST under Objective 12.2.2. Did you apply the theme to various situations as examples of a principle?

**Q:** Study the example; then select a text and theme of your own. Develop a brief demonstrative outline according to this model.

**A:** Your answer. Compare your demonstrative outline with the example in the “Demonstrative” section of your IST under Objective 12.2.2. Does your outline endeavor to show why each statement is true by using either argument, reason, or proven facts?

**Q:** Study the example; then select a text and theme of your own. Develop a brief biographical outline according to this model.

**A:** Your answer. Compare your biographical outline with the example in the “Biographical” section of your IST under Objective 12.2.2. Does your outline highlight what is spiritually beneficial and significant about the biblical figure?

#### 12.2.2 — Develop various topical sermon outline forms.

Although the structure or outline of the topical sermon is determined by the theme or topic of the text, an inevitable question arises: “How?” The first step must be to determine what the theme is (Chapter 4). There may be several possible topics in a given portion of Scripture, but there can be only one dominant theme. If the sermon is to deal with a lesser topic from a text, it should be considered in relation to the dominant theme. The topic of faith in John 3:16 should only be developed in relation to the primary subject of God’s Universal Love . A sermon on “The Life of Faith” or “Faith, the Gift of the Spirit” should be drawn from a more appropriate text.

Once we have prayerfully determined what the theme of our sermon is to be and established the aim of the sermon (Chapter 6), we may then develop our outline (Chapter 8). By asking the right questions of the topic in the context of the Scripture portion, we can produce material that is easy to work into a good topical outline. Using the example of Faith , we may ask:

Such interrogation of our topic as this may be extended beyond the sample questions suggested. The goal is to open up the subject for our sermon. Instead of the formal outline heading noted, some preachers prefer either to use the actual question as a division title such as, “I. What is Faith?” or to invert it to “What Faith Is or Is Not . ”

The wide variety of topical forms arising from this approach may be summarized as analytical, illustrative, demonstrative, and biographical.

Analytical

The analytical form is probably the most commonly used because many themes can be successfully interrogated and the results expounded upon. For example:

Text: Acts 26:20 “I preached that they should repent and turn to God and prove their repentance by their deeds.”

Theme: Repentance

Main Divisions:

I. The Nature of Repentance

II. The Need for Repentance

III. The Fruit of Repentance

Text:

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

Illustrative

In the illustrative method, the theme is applied to various situations as examples of a principle. For example:

Text: 1 Samuel 26:21 “Surely I have acted like a fool and have erred greatly.”

Theme: The Folly of Sinful Living

Main Divisions:

I. It Brings Disgrace (Illustration: King Saul, 1 Samuel 28).

II. It Destroys Nations (Illustration: Sodom, Genesis 19).

III. It Closes Heaven (Illustration: The Sinning Prophets, Matthew 7:21–23).

IV. It Silences Christ (Illustration: Herod, Luke 23:8–9).

Text:

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

Demonstrative

When using the demonstrative method, we show why we believe a topical statement to be true either by argument, reason, or proven facts. For example:

Text: John 17:17 “Your word is truth.”

Theme: Why I Believe the Bible Is True

Main Divisions:

I. Because Its Author Cannot Lie (Titus 1:2).

II. Because It Is True to Life (Romans 5:12 ).

III. Because Its Prophecies Are Fulfilled (Matthew 5:18).

IV. Because Its Promises Are Certain (2 Corinthians 1:20).

Text:

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

Biographical

Among the most popular forms of topical sermons is the biographical. History is the story of people, and we enjoy a well-told story about interesting individuals, especially if they are significant. Even a minor Bible person can illustrate powerful spiritual truths. To make biblical persons come alive for our hearers demands that we explore the background material as fully as possible and develop some understanding of the culture of those days. We must beware, however, of presenting masses of historical data rather than of concentrating on the significant and spiritually beneficial. For example:

Text: 1 Kings 16:30 “Ahab son of Omri did more evil in the eyes of the Lord than any of those before him.”

Theme: Ahab the Apostate King

Main Divisions:

I. Ahab Provokes God (1 Kings 16:33).

II. Ahab Proves God (1 Kings 20:13).

III. God Reproves Ahab (1 Kings 22:17).

Text:

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

From the examples given, we can see the value of the occasional topical sermon and the wide variety of forms that can be used.

**Q:** Suppose the topic is a Bible person, such as Abraham. Study the example of faith , and then fill in the outline material that corresponds to the adjacent topic questions.

**A:** TOPIC QUESTION OUTLINE MATERIAL Who is Abraham? The Identity of Abraham Where did Abraham come from? The Background of Abraham What did Abraham do? The Exploits or Activity of Abraham Why is Abraham important? The Significance of Abraham

**A:** Your answer. Compare your list to the example given in your IST.

**A:** Your answer. Compare your analytical outline with the example in the “Analytical” section of your IST under Objective 12.2.2. Did you apply the method of interrogation to develop the outline?

**Q:** Study the example; then select a text and theme of your own. Develop a brief illustrative outline according to this model.

**A:** Your answer. Compare your illustrative outline with the example in the “Illustrative” section of your IST under Objective 12.2.2. Did you apply the theme to various situations as examples of a principle?

**Q:** Study the example; then select a text and theme of your own. Develop a brief demonstrative outline according to this model.

**A:** Your answer. Compare your demonstrative outline with the example in the “Demonstrative” section of your IST under Objective 12.2.2. Does your outline endeavor to show why each statement is true by using either argument, reason, or proven facts?

**Q:** Study the example; then select a text and theme of your own. Develop a brief biographical outline according to this model.

**A:** Your answer. Compare your biographical outline with the example in the “Biographical” section of your IST under Objective 12.2.2. Does your outline highlight what is spiritually beneficial and significant about the biblical figure?

### Evaluation

#### 12.3.1 — Describe the advantages of topical sermons.

Although it was not until about AD 1200 that topical sermons were first preached (and for a long time they were disputed as being oratorical rather than biblical in style), their value as an evangelistic and teaching tool eventually became apparent. For some preachers, like the American evangelist D. L. Moody, this method is the only effective one. Others plead for a return to the older forms of textual and expository preaching as more biblical. It is important that we realistically evaluate the topical method.

Advantages

There are some distinct advantages in the topical approach to sermon structure. First, there is a unity about the finished sermon because the method seeks to unfold or apply one particular theme or topic. Since we can focus directly on the goal, the topical sermon is more likely to make a powerful impression on the hearers. Therefore, it has been popular among evangelistic preachers who appeal for commitments and ethical preachers who seek to change conduct.

Also, understanding is increased because the intention is simply to proclaim one primary truth. Other themes in the text can easily be left to one side, for we are not expounding the whole text but only the selected topic. Relevant material from other parts of the Bible can just as easily be incorporated in our presentation to make the topical development complete. Since logical development determines the divisions of the outline, it is easier for thinking people to follow the progress of the sermon.

Further, this gives our sermon breadth. Because we must thoroughly examine and master the subject we are preaching, we learn to think it through before we speak. The completeness of our presentation means that our sermon is more likely to satisfy the needs and the minds of our hearers.

Disadvantages

Despite the stated advantages of the topical sermon structure, we must recognize several danger signals. The first is superficiality. In our desire to cover the ground, we may fail to dig deeply and may simply stay on the surface. Since each sermon is to be complete in itself and we have a limited amount of time for preaching, we just quickly summarize the main points of the subject and eliminate whatever does not fit.

This in turn leads to subjectivity. We draw from the Scriptures the material we think appropriate to our subject and goal. In doing this, we put our minds in the position of judge. God’s Word is no longer the final authority; we are. We determine which texts belong together on the basis of our theology instead of allowing the Bible to determine our theology for us. The nineteenth-century systematic theologians fell into this very snare: viewing the Bible as a collection of unconnected “proof-texts” to be rearranged at the whim of the theologian to prove any doctrine. It is not surprising that the same century gave birth to so many heretical cults, which used essentially the same systematic method.

Even the text itself may be handled with dishonesty. Although, properly, the text should determine the content of the sermon, in topical sermons the text may be simply the launching pad for our subject. We may select the text because it happens to mention our topic, but thereafter, we ignore it as we develop the outline and content of the sermon. As we have already noted (see Chapter 4), the text should determine the content of the sermon even if we are preaching a topical sermon.

Another danger is the temptation of novelty. While we do not wish to say the same truths in the same way, we should not spend our sermon preparation time searching for the latest, newest, or most unusual topics on which to preach (Acts 17:21). Equally, we may select our topics solely on the basis of whether they are preachable or not. Instead of proclaiming “the whole will of God” (Acts 20:27), we limit our sermons to those subjects on which we can preach an impressive discourse. Our goal should be relevance to the needs of the hearers, not just fascination with a topic or curiosity about an interesting theme or even personal ability to preach effectively on the subject.

The topical sermon is a valuable tool in the hands of the preacher who is prepared to devote the time in prayer and preparation needed to use it well. It has a significant place alongside the other approaches to sermon structure, but only as one of the ways in which we may bring forth the Word of God.

**Q:** Suppose that you are pastoring a church. Discuss the advantages of topical preaching. How and when would this type of sermon be helpful in your preaching ministry?

**A:** The advantages of the topical sermon include unity, understanding, and breadth. Your answer should explain how these will affect your particular ministry goals. Unity will allow you to focus on the goal of the sermon while making a great impact on the hearers. In a church that attracts nonbelievers, this approach also makes it possible for individuals to fully understand the message. The topical approach often aids the preacher in thoroughly studying the material before beginning the sermon. This will give the message breadth.

**Q:** Define in your own words each of the four disadvantages of the topical sermon: (1) Superficiality; (2) Subjectivity; (3) Dishonesty; and (4) Novelty.

**A:** 1) Superficiality overuses the method of summary. 2) Subjectivity can cause proof-texting, which uses Scripture to prove personal doctrine. 3) Dishonesty ignores the text in the sermon development. 4) Novelty limits sermons to sensational topics. 13 CHAPTER
