## 13 - Textual Sermons

### Definition

#### 13.1.1 — Identify the distinguishing characteristics of textual sermons.

The textual sermon has much in common with both the topical sermon and the expository sermon. Like the topical sermon it takes its subject from the text; however, the text itself controls the development of the outline. Other portions of Scripture may be quoted or referred to in support of the points made, but the whole outline is the outline of the text itself, not the logical development of the topic.

If the goal of the textual sermon is the exposition—that is, the explanation and application—of a specific portion of Scripture, how does it differ from the expository sermon except, perhaps, in the length of the portion selected? The common feature of both textual and expository sermons lies in their close relationship to the portion of Scripture. The expository sermon, however, takes not only its theme and outline from the chosen portion, but also the major part of its supporting material. It is concerned with the explanation and application of the whole passage in its context to the hearers. The textual sermon, on the other hand, uses the context to establish the focus of the text but draws in supporting material from other relevant parts of the Bible. Its primary concern is the explanation and application of a specific theme in its context to the hearers.

The textual outline is closely tied to the language of the text itself. Each point made in the outline should be clearly made in the text itself; it should not be the result of an ingenious manipulation of the language. Although the points made from 1 Corinthians 2:1–3 by the student preacher could have been made from another portion, they were invalid there. Reversing the order of the text, he spoke first of Paul’s Coming to Corinth—“I came to you” (v. 3), then of Paul’s Determination to Discover—“I resolved to know” (v. 2), and concluded with Paul’s Proclamation to the People—“I proclaimed to you” (v. 1). In reality each of his Scripture statements was incomplete because the omitted portions qualified and limited the application of his texts.

The textual sermon should be marked by a clear and forthright treatment of the chosen portion. Our goal is to explain the truths that are there, not to invent points that could not possibly have been in the mind of the author.

### Forms

#### 13.2.1 — Demonstrate how to begin a textual sermon.

A good textual sermon outline does not just happen. It normally arises from a careful exploration of a Scripture portion.

In selecting a suitable portion of Scripture for a textual sermon, the preacher must be careful. Although any portion of Scripture could be preached, some texts may simply provide a topic for exploration such as John 13:31–32, Glorified . Others can only be understood as part of a larger context as in John 11:35, Jesus wept . However, the ideal text for a textual sermon provides both the subject and the major divisions of the outline. It should be capable of exposition as an integral unit of Scripture. The complete text should make sense as it stands.

Given a suitable text, it should be a simple matter to discover what the portion is about. Without this specific subject in mind, it would be difficult to produce an outline that had some sense of unity about it. As you develop the outline, be sure that each division of the text has an obvious and integral relationship to the subject or topic. If you cannot establish such a relationship in your sermon, you must reexamine the text to see if you have really understood what the portion is about. A textual outline cannot be three (or four) sermonettes on one text.

Sometimes a problem arises because the ideas in the text overlap and are not easily separated into thoughts that will move the sermon and its hearers on to a conclusion (Chapter 8). Hebrew poetry, with its characteristic use of parallelism, can be difficult because often the same thought is repeated in different words. An example of this is Psalm 103:10. Notice this same repetitive characteristic in the New Testament in 2 Timothy 2:13: “He will remain faithful, . . . he cannot disown himself.” The final phrase is not the reason for God’s continuing faithfulness, as the translated text may imply, but the explanation of His faithfulness.

Progression is essential if the sermon is to achieve its goal. Therefore, it is the preacher’s responsibility to establish the sequence of separate ideas that will carry the hearers through the sermon to the conclusion. It takes prayer, study, and work to do it.

By carefully examining the language of the text, you will find that the sequence of ideas will readily appear. As with the topical sermon in which we interrogated the topic (Chapter 12), so it is with the textual sermon. We apply to the text itself the same basic questions: what, where, when, how, why, who, and so on. The result is a good supply of usable material and an outline that does justice to the main flow of the text. For example:

And you also were included in Christ when you heard the word of truth, the gospel of your salvation. Having believed, you were marked in him with a seal, the promised Holy Spirit, who is a deposit guaranteeing our inheritance until the redemption of those who are God’s possession—to the praise of his glory. (Ephesians 1:13–14).

To determine the starting point of the sequence, we must ask, “When were you included in Christ?” The text states that it was “when [or “after”] you heard the word of truth, the gospel of your salvation.” The opening phrase of the next sentence further clarifies the meaning of the first sentence—“Having believed.” What was the consequence of this faith relationship by which the person was “in Christ”? “You were marked . . . with a seal, the promised Holy Spirit.” What was the role of this activity of the Holy Spirit? He “is a deposit guaranteeing our inheritance until . . . .”

Text:

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

IV.

Textual sermons may take three principal forms: textual-natural, textual-analytical, and textual-topical. Let us examine each form in turn to see the significance of each and how one differs from another.

Textual-Natural

In the textual-natural form, the divisions are those of the text itself taken in a logical sequence. Often the outline arises from a simple division of the text. For example:

Text: 1 Thessalonians 5:16–18 “Be joyful always; pray continually; give thanks in all circumstances, for this is God’s will for you in Christ Jesus.”

Theme: God’s Will for You

Main Divisions:

I. Always Joyful

II. Always Prayerful

III. Always Thankful

Text: Hebrews 2:3 “How shall we escape if we ignore such a great salvation?”

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

As is apparent from these examples, the thoughts are specifically those of the text itself, and the outline develops the basic message of the text.

Textual-Analytical

The textual-analytical form develops the textual outline by interrogating the Scripture portion and arranging the results of the interrogation into an outline that remains faithful to the sequence of the text. For example:

Text: John 14:6 “Jesus answered, ‘I am the way and the truth and the life.’”

Theme: Christ’s Answer to Humankind’s Dilemma

Main Divisions:

I. Humankind Is Lost—Jesus Is the Way.

II. Humankind Is Ignorant—Jesus Is the Truth.

III. Humankind Is Dead—Jesus Is the Life.

In developing this outline, we simply ask, “Why did Jesus describe Himself in the terms He did?” Then we use the answers to form the division headings. Make sure that the questions asked are relevant to the text and bring out the truth of the portion more fully. If a text does not readily open up to this kind of interrogation, develop another kind of outline from it.

Text:

Theme:

Main Divisions:

I.

II.

III.

IV.

Textual-Topical

Some texts presuppose certain facts without explicitly stating it. To preach them effectively requires us to add a division to the outline that is not apparent in the text itself. This textual-topical form should be employed cautiously lest we distort the message of the text in our desire to “complete the sense of it.” Nevertheless, when done well, this form clarifies truths that modern readers of the Scriptures miss or mistake. For example:

Text: 2 Chronicles 34:30–31 “He read in their hearing all the words of the Book of the Covenant, which had been found in the temple of the Lord. The king . . . renewed the covenant in the presence of the Lord—to follow the Lord and keep his commands . . . and to obey.”

Theme: Returning to God’s Word

Main Divisions:

I. The Loss of the Scriptures

II. The Rediscovery of the Scriptures (verse 30)

III. The Renewing of the Covenant (verse 31)

From these basic forms, a considerable variety of textual sermons can be developed that may be employed occasionally by experienced preachers. With the wealth of material available, it is better to concentrate your efforts on approaching your texts in simple ways than to become overwhelmed by novel and exotic forms of textual preaching.

**Q:** Study the Scripture references, and select an appropriate title for the passage. Then outline the Scripture passage using the textual model.

**A:** Compare your outline to the one I have suggested below. How does it compare? Although your wording may be different, the idea should be similar. I. The Reception of the Word II. The Inclusion in Christ III. The Seal of the Holy Spirit IV. The Completion of Redemption Since the recurrent phrase of both verse 12 and verse 14 is “the praise of His glory,” an appropriate title could be “Steps to Glory.” Our goal would be to move the hearers along step-by-step from being hearers of the Word to believers, to Spirit-filled, to living in expectation of Christ’s return.

**Q:** It is possible sometimes to reverse the order of texts to make a preachable sequence of ideas. Study the text and see if you can arrange it in reverse order. Compare your outline to the answer to this Interactive Question at the end of the chapter.

**A:** Compare your outline with the following outline. The form of your outline should be similar to this one. Theme: No Other Way Main Divisions: I. The Greatness of This Salvation II. The Possibility of Neglect III. The Impossibility of Escape

**Q:** Select a text of your own. Then choose a theme, and briefly write a textual outline using this model.

**A:** Compare your textual outline with the examples given in your IST under the sections “Textual-Natural,” “Textual-Analytical,” “Textual-Topical.”

**Q:** After reviewing the outline, explain why its form is considered textual-topical.

**A:** Although the text does not directly refer to “the Book of the Covenant” as being lost, it presupposes that fact by recounting the finding of the Book. This adds to the point in that the loss may be unnoticed and unrecorded but somehow it happened—in the temple of the Lord (2 Chronicles 34:14–15). This outline, then, is textual-topical.

**Q:** In your own words, briefly define each form of the textual sermon: (a) textual-natural; (b) textual-analytical; and (c) textual-topical.

**A:** a) The textual-natural form takes its outline from the logical sequence of the text. b) The textual-analytical form interrogates Scripture and arranges the results according to the sequence of the text. c) The textual-topical adds a division to the outline that is not apparent in the text. The point, however, would be obvious and truthful, although not specifically mentioned in the text.

#### 13.2.2 — Develop various textual outline forms.

Theme: Returning to God’s Word

Main Divisions:

I. The Loss of the Scriptures

II. The Rediscovery of the Scriptures (verse 30)

III. The Renewing of the Covenant (verse 31)

From these basic forms, a considerable variety of textual sermons can be developed that may be employed occasionally by experienced preachers. With the wealth of material available, it is better to concentrate your efforts on approaching your texts in simple ways than to become overwhelmed by novel and exotic forms of textual preaching.

**Q:** Study the Scripture references, and select an appropriate title for the passage. Then outline the Scripture passage using the textual model.

**A:** Compare your outline to the one I have suggested below. How does it compare? Although your wording may be different, the idea should be similar. I. The Reception of the Word II. The Inclusion in Christ III. The Seal of the Holy Spirit IV. The Completion of Redemption Since the recurrent phrase of both verse 12 and verse 14 is “the praise of His glory,” an appropriate title could be “Steps to Glory.” Our goal would be to move the hearers along step-by-step from being hearers of the Word to believers, to Spirit-filled, to living in expectation of Christ’s return.

**Q:** It is possible sometimes to reverse the order of texts to make a preachable sequence of ideas. Study the text and see if you can arrange it in reverse order. Compare your outline to the answer to this Interactive Question at the end of the chapter.

**A:** Compare your outline with the following outline. The form of your outline should be similar to this one. Theme: No Other Way Main Divisions: I. The Greatness of This Salvation II. The Possibility of Neglect III. The Impossibility of Escape

**Q:** Select a text of your own. Then choose a theme, and briefly write a textual outline using this model.

**A:** Compare your textual outline with the examples given in your IST under the sections “Textual-Natural,” “Textual-Analytical,” “Textual-Topical.”

**Q:** After reviewing the outline, explain why its form is considered textual-topical.

**A:** Although the text does not directly refer to “the Book of the Covenant” as being lost, it presupposes that fact by recounting the finding of the Book. This adds to the point in that the loss may be unnoticed and unrecorded but somehow it happened—in the temple of the Lord (2 Chronicles 34:14–15). This outline, then, is textual-topical.

**Q:** In your own words, briefly define each form of the textual sermon: (a) textual-natural; (b) textual-analytical; and (c) textual-topical.

**A:** a) The textual-natural form takes its outline from the logical sequence of the text. b) The textual-analytical form interrogates Scripture and arranges the results according to the sequence of the text. c) The textual-topical adds a division to the outline that is not apparent in the text. The point, however, would be obvious and truthful, although not specifically mentioned in the text.

### Evaluation

#### 13.3.1 — Identify the advantages of the textual sermon.

In a desire to be true Bible preachers, some make the textual sermon their only method of expounding the Scriptures. Although this approach has distinct advantages, it should not be used exclusively, nor should its problem areas be ignored.

Advantages

The fact that the textual sermon is thoroughly biblical is a definite advantage. Because its subject and main divisions are drawn directly from the text, the hearers recognize that God’s Word is being expounded, not some topic selected by the preacher on the basis of a personal preference or a perceived need in the congregation. The distinctive qualities of the Bible become the characteristics of the faithful exposition of the chosen portion.

The textual sermon is also scripturally enlightening. Most believers want to understand God’s Word better. The careful exposition of the text’s words and phrases deepens the hearers’ knowledge of the Scriptures. Not only can they follow the sermon in their Bible, but also, maybe even years later, they will remember the substance of the sermon when they read the same portion.

Furthermore, since the textual sermon is concerned with the actual words of Scripture, it can be prophetically direct. The hearer who feels uncomfortable or convicted cannot accuse the preacher of aiming for him or her since the sermon is simply explaining what has been there in the text for two thousand years or more. It is God who is perceived to be speaking, not a vindictive or self-opinionated preacher.

With the 23,214 verses in the Old Testament and 7,959 in the New, there is a large variety of portions available for exposition. Every verse, however, cannot be treated on its own, nor would it necessarily be suited to textual exposition. Nevertheless, with such a wealth of material available, there is no reason for monotony or for a preacher’s lacking something to preach. Many find themselves with more sermons waiting to be preached than opportunities to preach them in a lifetime of ministry.

Disadvantages

Given all the advantages of the textual approach, we must be wary of the problems awaiting the unsuspecting preacher. First, false analysis is a real snare to the preacher intent on drawing the maximum amount of material from a text. In our desire to say something new about the text, we may impose our thoughts on it. If it has not been discovered before, it probably was never there until we invented it. Rarely do young preachers discover something truly original from the text. Fortunately, most congregations know that and wait for our enthusiasm for our discovery to subside.

To attach great significance to every word in the text may reveal more of your ignorance than your knowledge of Scripture. Some words do not appear as such in the original language of the text but are used by the translator to bring out the sense of it. For example, there is no indefinite article “a” or “an” in Greek as there is in English. Other words in the original language do not have the same emphasis that they would have in English. For instance, nouns in Greek are usually preceded by a definite article without any theological significance being intended.

As a young pastor I was astonished to hear a respected older preacher expound at great length on the phrase “and it came to pass” (Genesis 6:1, KJV). He told us first, that things do come; second, why they come—so that they can pass; and third, why they pass—so that we can endure them for a little while. In reality the phrase is a medieval English idiom for “it happened.”

Another danger is that of irrelevance . To include great detail about every word in the text will not only produce spiritual and mental indigestion, but also boredom. Your hearers are not all fascinated with detailed information. They are looking for something that will help them with their daily lives. As a general rule, nothing should be explained that will not be applied. Ask yourself, “What is the point of this?” If the hearer needs to know in order to better incorporate the truth into his or her life, include it. If not, omit it. By keeping the aim of the sermon clearly in focus, only relevant points of exegesis will be included. Omitted material can be reserved for a later sermon.

Textual sermons may well be described as the staple food of most preachers’ ministry. Certainly preparation takes preachers into the depths of God’s Word and their preaching does the same for hearers. When done well, it encourages Bible study and meditation. The textual preacher stands before his or her hearers as a preacher of the Word.

**Q:** Give an example of how you will avoid each of the following disadvantages in your preparation of a textual outline:

**A:** Your answers will be different from mine, but here are some suggestions: a) While being diligent to draw truth from the text, the preacher must be careful to avoid reading meaning into the text that was not intended by the author. One can avoid this by carefully researching the background of the text in available commentaries. b) One must make sure that in studying each line of the text that the original language and grammar is taken into consideration. Otherwise, the focus could be put on something never intended in the original biblical writing. This information is available in commentaries and Greek-to-English lexicons. c) Even if one discovers a lot of information about the text, he or she must make sure that it has relevance to the target audience. As the preacher prepares the sermon, he or she should keep the hearers and their needs in mind, making sure that every detail of the sermon is appropriate and interesting for the intended group. 14 CHAPTER

## 14 - Expository Sermons

### Definition

#### 14.1.1 — Distinguish between the expository sermon and other types of scriptural exposition, and describe its goal and how to achieve it.

The term expository comes from the Latin exposui : “to have laid open the truth for inspection and understanding.” We cannot open up to others what is not first open to us. The expository sermon is not simply an easy way out for the lazy or overworked preacher. It is the exposing of the message of a Scripture portion that provides directly all the main and subdivisions of the sermon.

Because the expository sermon deals with one basic Scripture portion, we must ensure that the portion is complete in itself and is not an artificial division of the Bible, like some chapters. Notice that the section beginning with 2 Corinthians 5:11 does not end with verse 5:21 but at verse 6:2, and the section beginning with verse 6:14 ends at verse 7:1. By applying good hermeneutics to our chosen portion, we will bring out the message that is there. Above all, we must be faithful to the text.

The expository sermon must be clearly distinguished from the exegetical paper, the commentary, and the Bible reading. Certainly it should be a true sermon with all the characteristics of a sermon: text, theme, aim, introduction, developed outline, and conclusion (see Units 2 and 3). The exegetical paper, however, simply recounts what the author has discovered about a passage, particularly from the original language and cultural context. Although exegesis contributes information for the sermon, the two are distinct in purpose and form. While exegesis concentrates on analysis and investigation, the expository sermon proceeds from there to synthesis and application.

Similarly the commentary should not be confused with the expository sermon. The commentary seeks to apply the results of the exegesis to the passage, phrase by phrase, in order to illuminate the meaning of the text, rather than to apply the text to the hearers. The purpose of the commentary is to record information, not to engage in proclamation.

Although the Bible reading or running commentary seeks to apply the passage to the hearers, in addition to making exegetical and devotional comments, it does so in a random fashion. This approach often does not have a discernible outline or cohesive purpose. One preacher expressed his preference for expository preaching because when he ran out of thoughts on one verse, he could run on to the next. That kind of expository preaching is a travesty of the real thing. Among seventeenth-century Puritans, the public reading of Scripture with comments was a regular part of worship. However, this did not take the place of the sermon, which followed later, nor was it an opportunity for an imaginative reader to share passing impressions about the portion being read. It demanded careful and prayerful research.

As a sermon, the expository sermon should have cohesion , movement, and direction. The various elements discovered in the portion should be woven into a harmonious discourse that will move the hearers toward the predetermined goal. No portion of Scripture was written simply to record information; therefore, at every stage in our preparation of the sermon and in all our exegesis, we must keep before us these questions: What is the point of this portion? What is its message? What is it intended to do to the reader? The expository sermon must have the same aim if it is to be true to the text. The application may vary according to the needs of the hearers, but the purpose will remain the same.

**Q:** Define the following terms, and explain how they differ from the expository sermon: (a) exegetical paper; (b) commentary; and (c) Bible reading.

**A:** a) outline structure. b) content. c) selection of subjects. d) preaching style.

**Q:** Describe the goal of the expository sermon and how you will endeavor to achieve it.

**A:** a) structure. b) delivery. c) subject matter. d) sermon outline.

### Forms

#### 14.2.1 — Develop an expository sermon outline.

An expository sermon can be developed from any self-contained unit of Scripture, ranging from a simple paragraph (such as a parable of Jesus) to an episode (like a miracle of Jesus), a chapter (like Hebrews 11), a psalm, or even a whole book of the Bible. George Campbell Morgan, the renowned British Congregationalist, specialized in tracing the message of each particular book of the Bible in a preliminary sermon in preparation for a series, giving detailed exposition of the text.

Once we have selected a portion, we must then analyze it for the theme. This usually requires reading it through several times. Even if it is a whole book, we should read it through completely at one sitting so that we understand clearly the flow of the portion. Until we can express the theme in a simple statement, we do not understand the portion sufficiently to preach it faithfully (Chapter 4).

The expository outline may be developed either naturally or analytically, as with the textual sermon (Chapter 13). The aim that we have prayerfully established for the sermon will determine the approach we adopt. It may be evangelistic, doctrinal, ethical, or apologetic (Chapter 6).

We will take for a sample portion Luke 12:13–21: “The Parable of the Rich Fool.” From the context of verses 1–12 and verses 22–34, we see the subject of self-centeredness dominating the thought-flow of the passage. We could summarize the theme of our portion, then, as “True riches consist in a person’s relationship to God, not in material possessions.”

Evangelistic

If our aim were evangelistic, we would seek to motivate people to seek true riches.

Title: First Things First

Main Divisions and Subdivisions:

I. Wrong Priorities

A. Things (vv. 16–17)

B. Security (v. 18)

C. Luxury (v. 19)

II. Right Priorities

A. The Kingdom of God (vv. 21–31)

B. The Relationship With God (vv. 32–34)

Doctrinal

If our aim were doctrinal, we would strive to teach the right attitude toward material things.

Title: The Christian’s Attitude Toward Possessions

Main Divisions:

I. Possessions Are Not the Measure of the Person (vv. 15–20).

II. Possessions Can Be a Distraction from Lasting Values (vv. 20–21, 30).

III. Possessions Can Be the Provision of God (vv. 31–34).

Ethical

If our sermon were ethical, we would aim to show how to behave in relation to material possessions.

Title: What Shall I Do with My Things?

Main Divisions:

I. The Abundance of Possessions (vv. 15–17)

II. The Anticipation of Plenty (vv. 18–19)

III. The Absence of Preparation (vv. 20–21)

IV. The Acknowledgment of Providence (vv. 22–31)

Apologetic

Finally, if our sermon’s aim were apologetic, we would seek to show why materialism does not work.

Title: The Failure of Materialism

Main Divisions:

I. Its Philosophy Is Mistaken (v. 15).

II. Its Attitude Is Selfish (vv. 16–19).

III. Its Outcome Is Futile (v. 20).

IV. Its Alternative Is Eternal (vv. 21, 31).

By relating the chosen portion to its context and allowing the aim to interact with the theme and text, preachable sermon outlines can be developed that will apply the message of the Scripture portion to the hearers. You should take from the portion only as much material as you need to fulfill the aim of the exposition and no more. Other minor themes should be left for other sermons, not dragged in as passing comments or asides.

With at least four different aims possible for a given portion and, therefore, four different sermons, you must take care not to introduce material from one sermon into another. Beware of the temptation of trying to say everything you can about a Bible portion in one sermon. You may want to preach on that text again.

**Q:** Study Matthew 25:1–13, The Parable of the Ten Virgins . Using the principles described, determine four different aims for this passage:

**A:** a) preaching style. b) method of delivery. c) volume of material. d) topic of the text.

**Q:** Create an outline for Matthew 3:1–12 using an evangelistic aim.

**A:** a) illustration. b) outline. c) conclusion. d) interaction.

**Q:** Choose an extended text of your own. Based on one of the four sermon aims, develop a brief outline according to the model given.

**A:** a) analytical. b) illustrative. c) demonstrative. d) biographical.

### Evaluation

#### 14.3.1 — Describe the advantages of the expository sermon.

Advantages

There are several distinct advantages for the preacher, for the preacher’s ministry, and for his or her hearers, both individually and corporately as the church. In the first place, the preparation of expository sermons demands that the preacher study the Scripture portion carefully, apply sound hermeneutics, and learn how to relate the Scriptures directly to the hearers. In short, it makes better Bible preachers. Preachers who devote themselves to expository preaching both deepen their own relationship with the Lord and strengthen their ministry.

Because the expository sermon deals directly with the meaning of the Bible, the preacher can speak with greater confidence and authority, knowing that he or she is proclaiming God’s Word. Preachers can face their congregations with a boldness that arises from the fact that they are expounding “the whole will of God” (Acts 20:27), not some private doctrine. By confining their exposition to the biblical text in context, they know that they are not distorting the true message of the Scriptures.

His or her ministry is marked by an increased effectiveness because God has promised that His Word will achieve its goal (Isaiah 55:11). As the Lord “confirmed his word” by miraculous signs through the first Christian preachers, the apostles (Mark 16:20), so the preacher today can depend upon the same faithful God when he or she faithfully expounds the Scriptures.

Hearers realize that what they are hearing is God’s Word rather than a human’s opinion, and so the preacher finds a more receptive congregation. King Zedekiah’s question (Jeremiah 37:17), “Is there any word from the Lord?” often lies unspoken on the lips of the congregation. As the preacher expounds that Word, it is received like the thirsty land drinks in the rain in summer. People are fed and their needs are met.

The expository preacher also produces a more perceptive congregation. While the textual sermon focuses on the detail of the text, the expository sermon takes in the larger picture of the paragraph, chapter, or book. The hearer learns to take a step back from the situation in his or her life and view it in context from a larger perspective. The God who made the grain of sand also made the universe. The Lord first directed Abraham’s attention to “the stars in the sky” and then to “the sand on the seashore” (Genesis 22:17). It is that sense of context which helps us keep things in proportion.

The expository preacher also finds a more responsive congregation, since the hearers know that even the most sensitive issues are being dealt with from the biblical perspective. People will accept from God what would be impertinence from any human. Because the preacher is expounding the written Word, the hearers can check it out for themselves like the noble Bereans who “examined the Scriptures every day to see if what Paul said was true” (Acts 17:11).

The expository preacher also models Bible study methods for the individual believer, building a more spiritual congregation by showing them how to explore the Scriptures. My first two pastors were both excellent Bible expositors and had a profound influence on my preaching ministry. Although I never consciously imitated either of them, I am sure my early ministry often reflected their preaching. In any case, they helped instill in me a deep and lasting love for the Bible. Your people will learn how to study the Scriptures by observing how you do it.

Disadvantages

The twin dangers of dullness and dryness are never more pressing than in expository preaching. To simply recount detail after detail without making the information alive and relevant to your hearers will effectively destroy your congregation’s appetite for the Word of God. Remember that you are declaring the life-changing revelation of the living God; it should never be dull or boring. If you are bored with the Bible, you do not understand it. Peter rightly described Jesus’ teaching as “the words of eternal life” (John 6:68). Do not make them any less. Let their life shine through and illuminate the lives of your hearers.

Even though your preparation may be sound exegetically, the expository sermon may appear disjointed if you do not develop a clear and cohesive outline. It is not enough to bounce your way down a chapter from one blessed thought to another. Your exposition needs a clear sense of direction if it is to communicate the message effectively. Your hearers want to know where you are going so that they can make the journey with you.

A related problem is that of confusion. Because the Scriptures are so full of detail that generations of preachers exploring the same portions have still found new depths of insight, you may be tempted to include everything you can about a text in one sermon. The result is that your hearers are overwhelmed by masses of half-understood and half-developed thoughts. It is far better to focus on one theme alone and to leave out all material that is irrelevant to this particular message. Even though the road may run in two directions, you cannot travel in both directions at the same time.

As with any sermon, but especially with expository sermons, lack of balance can be a major problem. With so much that could be said, even relevant material, it is easy to continue to interject one more comment on a particular point until important points in your outline you planned to speak on later are compressed or squeezed out altogether. As a result, you lose the careful progress of the outline and development of the theme. The end result is like the reflection in a distorting mirror at the carnival: an abnormally large head, a minute body, and feet seemingly attached to extended thighs. You know you do not really look like the reflection, but do your hearers realize that your distorted presentation of that message is not a true picture? Remember to keep truth in biblical proportions and you will not run this risk.

The answer to all of these problem areas lies in prayerful, careful toil. Expository preaching is not for the lazy preacher. You should never begrudge the time spent in preparing clear, faithful sermons that will illuminate the darkness with the light of God’s Word. “Preach the Word,” declared Paul, “discharge all the duties of your ministry” (2 Timothy 4:2, 5).

In this course, we have presented the methods and tools for preaching biblical yet contemporary messages. Regardless of what method of preaching you choose to use in your preaching ministry, it is now your responsibility to diligently apply your skills. The task to which you are called demands the best you can give.

“And who is equal to such a task? Unlike so many, we do not peddle the word of God for profit. On the contrary, in Christ we speak before God with sincerity, like men sent from God” (2 Corinthians 2:16–17).

**Q:** Review Acts 17:11, Acts 20:27, Isaiah 55:11, and Genesis 22:17. Describe the advantages of expository preaching to each of the following: (a) the individual believer; (b) the congregation; (c) the sermon presentation; and (d) the preacher.

**A:** a) analytical. b) illustrative. c) demonstrative. d) biographical.

**Q:** Consider and reflect on the advantages of expository preaching. Discuss how they will affect you personally, as a preacher of the gospel.

**A:** a) studying the chosen subject extensively. b) allowing the hearer to draw his or her own final conclusions. c) applying one particular theme. d) balancing between the text and the theme.

**Q:** Describe how to avoid the following disadvantages of expository preaching:

**A:** a) Subjectivity b) Superficiality c) Novelty d) Dishonesty

Because the Scriptures are so full of detail that generations of preachers exploring the same portions have still found new depths of insight, you may be tempted to include everything you can about a text in one sermon. The result is that your hearers are overwhelmed by masses of half-understood and half-developed thoughts. It is far better to focus on one theme alone and to leave out all material that is irrelevant to this particular message. Even though the road may run in two directions, you cannot travel in both directions at the same time.

As with any sermon, but especially with expository sermons, lack of balance can be a major problem. With so much that could be said, even relevant material, it is easy to continue to interject one more comment on a particular point until important points in your outline you planned to speak on later are compressed or squeezed out altogether. As a result, you lose the careful progress of the outline and development of the theme. The end result is like the reflection in a distorting mirror at the carnival: an abnormally large head, a minute body, and feet seemingly attached to extended thighs. You know you do not really look like the reflection, but do your hearers realize that your distorted presentation of that message is not a true picture? Remember to keep truth in biblical proportions and you will not run this risk.

The answer to all of these problem areas lies in prayerful, careful toil. Expository preaching is not for the lazy preacher. You should never begrudge the time spent in preparing clear, faithful sermons that will illuminate the darkness with the light of God’s Word. “Preach the Word,” declared Paul, “discharge all the duties of your ministry” (2 Timothy 4:2, 5).

In this course, we have presented the methods and tools for preaching biblical yet contemporary messages. Regardless of what method of preaching you choose to use in your preaching ministry, it is now your responsibility to diligently apply your skills. The task to which you are called demands the best you can give.

“And who is equal to such a task? Unlike so many, we do not peddle the word of God for profit. On the contrary, in Christ we speak before God with sincerity, like men sent from God” (2 Corinthians 2:16–17).

**Q:** Review Acts 17:11, Acts 20:27, Isaiah 55:11, and Genesis 22:17. Describe the advantages of expository preaching to each of the following: (a) the individual believer; (b) the congregation; (c) the sermon presentation; and (d) the preacher.

**A:** a) analytical. b) illustrative. c) demonstrative. d) biographical.

**Q:** Consider and reflect on the advantages of expository preaching. Discuss how they will affect you personally, as a preacher of the gospel.

**A:** a) studying the chosen subject extensively. b) allowing the hearer to draw his or her own final conclusions. c) applying one particular theme. d) balancing between the text and the theme.

**Q:** Describe how to avoid the following disadvantages of expository preaching:

**A:** a) Subjectivity b) Superficiality c) Novelty d) Dishonesty
